Abstract.
For any closed symplectic manifold, we show that the number of 11-periodic orbits of a nondegenerate Hamiltonian thereon is bounded from below by a version of total Betti number over ℤ{\mathbb{Z}} of the ambient space taking account of the total Betti number over ℚ{\mathbb{Q}} and torsions of all characteristic. The proof is based on constructing a Hamiltonian Floer theory over the Novikov ring with integer coefficients, which generalizes our earlier work for constructing integer-valued Gromov–Witten type invariants. In the course of the construction, we build a Hamiltonian Floer flow category with compatible smooth global Kuranishi charts. This generalizes a recent work of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith, which might be of independent interest.

1. Introduction
Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) be a closed symplectic manifold. Suppose H:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}} is a smooth function, which is usually referred to as a 11-periodic Hamiltonian function by identifying S1=ℝ/ℤS^{1}={\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}. Denote by HtH_{t} the smooth function obtained by restricting HH to {t}×M\{t\}\times M. Then the Hamiltonian vector field XHtX_{H_{t}} of HH is a vector field on MM determined by the formula
ω⁡(XHt,⋅)=d​Ht.\omega(X_{H_{t}},\cdot)=dH_{t}.
A smooth map x⁡(t):S1→Mx(t):S^{1}\to M solving the ordinary differential equation
x˙​(t)=XHt​(x⁡(t))\dot{x}(t)=X_{H_{t}}(x(t))
is called a 11-periodic orbit of HH. Write ϕt:M→M\phi_{t}:M\to M the time tt flow of XHtX_{H_{t}}. Then then the set of 11-periodic orbits of HH has a one-to-one correspondence with the set of fixed points ϕ1:M→M\phi_{1}:M\to M by evaluating x⁡(t)x(t) at t=0t=0. A periodic orbit x⁡(t)x(t) is called nondegenerate if the linear map
d​ϕ1:Tx⁡(0)​M→Tx⁡(0)​Md\phi_{1}:T_{x(0)}M\to T_{x(0)}M
does not have 11 as an eigenvalue. If all 11-periodic orbits of HH are nondegenerate, the Hamiltonian HH is called nondegenerate.
Given the symplectic manifold (M,ω)(M,\omega), there is a contractible choice of almost complex structures on XX which are compatible with ω\omega. The first Chern class of (M,ω)(M,\omega), denoted by c1​(M,ω)=c1​(M)c_{1}(M,\omega)=c_{1}(M), is defined to be the first Chern class of T​MTM endowed with a choice of, equivalently, any choice of almost complex structure compatible with ω\omega. The minimal Chern number of (M,ω)(M,\omega) is defined to be the nonnegative integer N∈ℤ≥0N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} such that the range of the map
π2​(M)→HurewiczH2​(M,ℤ)→c1​(M)ℤ\pi_{2}(M)\xrightarrow{\text{Hurewicz}}H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\xrightarrow{c_{1}(M)}\mathbb{Z}
is N​ℤ⊂ℤN{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}. For any i∈ℤ/2​Ni\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N, introduce the ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-module
Hi(2​N)​(M,ℤ):=⨁j≡i​ mod ​2​NHj​(M,ℤ).H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}):=\bigoplus_{j\equiv i\text{ mod }2N}H_{j}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}).
Namely, we collapse the natural ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-grading on H∗​(M,ℤ)H_{*}(M;\mathbb{Z}) to a ℤ/2​N{\mathbb{Z}}/2N-grading. Because Hi(2​N)​(M,ℤ)H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}) is a finitely generated ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-module, there exist an integer bi≥0b_{i}\geq 0 and a sequence of integers a1|a2​|⋯|​aka_{1}|a_{2}|\cdots|a_{k} such that
Hi(2​N)​(M,ℤ)≅ℤbi⊕ℤ/a1⊕⋯⊕ℤ/ak,H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{b_{i}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/a_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/a_{k},
where the integers a1,…,aka_{1},\dots,a_{k} are the invariant factors of Hi(2​N)​(M,ℤ)H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}) as a finitely generated ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-module. Using this decomposition, define the quantity
τi(2​N)​(M):=number of invariant factors of ​Hi(2​N)​(M,ℤ).\tau_{i}^{(2N)}(M):=\text{number of invariant factors of }H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}).
The main result of this paper is a solution to the homological Arnold conjecture over ℤ{\mathbb{Z}} for arbitrary closed symplectic manifolds. This bound is similar to the Morse inequality over ℤ{\mathbb{Z}} which takes into account the torsion part of the homology.
Theorem A.
Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) be a closed symplectic manifold with minimal Chern number NN. Suppose H:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}} is a nondegenerate 11-periodic Hamiltonian. Then the number of 11-periodic orbits of HH is bounded below by
rank ​H∗​(M,ℚ)+2​∑i∈ℤ/2​Nτi(2​N)​(M).\text{rank }H_{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}})+2\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N}\tau_{i}^{(2N)}(M).
(1.1)
We briefly comment on the historical background of the Arnold conjecture. Arnold conjectured ([Arn78, Appendix 9], [Arn65]) that the number of 11-periodic orbits of any nondegenerate Hamiltonian is at least the minimal number of critical points of a Morse function on MM (the strong Arnold conjecture) and suggested that it is a consequence of a version of Morse inequality. While the strong Arnold conjecture is difficult to prove, there have been significant progresses towards the homological Arnold conjecture, namely, the number of periodic orbits being bounded from below by the total Betti number.11
1
From now on, in this paper, the Arnold conjecture refers to the homological version. These progresses started with the breakthrough of Conley–Zehnder [CZ83], who solved the strong Arnold conjecture for T2​nT^{2n} using finite dimensional methods. Conley–Zehnder’s method was later extended by Floer to solve the strong Arnold conjecture for surfaces with genus at least 22 and for certain classes of Kähler manifolds [Flo86]. It was then followed by the revolutionary work of Floer [Flo89] which invented the Floer homology and established the Arnold conjecture for (M,ω)(M,\omega) satisfying [ω]=λ​c1​(M)[\omega]=\lambda c_{1}(M) for some λ∈ℝ\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}} (monotone symplectic manifolds). Floer’s result was extended by Hofer–Salamon [HS95] and Ono [Ono95] to cover all semi-positive symplectic manifolds, i.e., (M2​n,ω)(M^{2n},\omega) which does not have A∈π2​(M)A\in\pi_{2}(M) such that ω⁡(A)>0\omega(A)>0 and 3−n≤c1​(A)<03-n\leq c_{1}(A)<0. It is worth noting that these results hold over ℤ\mathbb{Z}.
For general symplectic manifolds, Fukaya–Ono[FO99], Liu–Tian[LT98b], and Ruan [Rua99] proved the homological Arnold conjecture with lower bound coming from the rational total Betti number. These papers are based on a kind of abstract machinery, generally called the “virtual technique,” which is designed for generalizing Floer’s construction (along side with the mathematical theory of Gromov–Witten invariants). The most recent advancement towards the homological Arnold conjecture by Abouzaid–Blumberg [AB21], which relies more heavily on stable homotopy theory, bounds the number of periodic orbits from below by the sum of Betti numbers in any finite field. In addition to the aforementioned works, using different versions of the virtual technique, the weak Arnold conjecture over rational numbers is reproved in [Par16] and [FW22].
Theorem A allows one to obtain a sharper lower bound for the number of 11-periodic orbits of a given non-degenerate Hamiltonian by treating the torsion components of H∗​(M,ℤ)H_{*}(M;\mathbb{Z}) uniformly and simultaneously. The lower bound provided by rank​H∗​(M,𝔽p){\rm rank}H_{*}(M;\mathbb{F}_{p}) as from [AB21] is also recovered from Theorem A by the universal coefficient theorem. Our bound is also strictly stronger than the bound of Abouzaid–Blumberg, for example, when Heven​(M)H_{\rm even}(M) has only pp-torsion and Hodd​(M)H_{\rm odd}(M) has only qq-torsion and p≠qp\neq q.
1.1. Proof strategy
The proof of Theorem A is based on constructing a version of Hamiltonian Floer homology over the Novikov ring Λ\Lambda with integer coefficients and exponents, where
Λ:={∑i=−m∞aiTi|m∈ℤ,ai∈ℤ},\Lambda:=\Big\{\sum_{i=-m}^{\infty}a_{i}T^{i}\ |\ m\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\Big\},
and a comparison with the Morse homology of MM with Λ\Lambda-coefficients using the Piunikhin–Salamon–Schwarz (PSS) [PSS96] map.
Theorem B.
Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) be a closed symplectic manifold such that [ω][\omega] is contained in the image of H2​(M,ℤ)→H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}). Assume that the minimal Chern number of (M,ω)(M,\omega) is NN. Suppose H:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}} is a non-degenerate 11-periodic Hamiltonian such that the symplectic action of any capped 11-periodic orbit of HH takes value in ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}. After choosing an almost complex structure JJ compatible with ω\omega and some other auxiliary data, there is a ℤ/2​N{\mathbb{Z}}/2N-graded chain complex
C​F∗​(H,Λ)CF_{*}(H;\Lambda)
freely generated over Λ\Lambda by all contractible 11-periodic orbits of HH, graded by the Conley–Zehnder index, with differential given by suitably counting stable Floer trajectories with trivial isotropy group connecting the 11-periodic orbits.
Theorem C.
Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) and H:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}} be the same as in Theorem B. Suppose f:M→ℝf:M\to{\mathbb{R}} is a smooth Morse function and let C​M∗​(f,Λ)CM_{*}(f;\Lambda) be the Morse chain complex associated with ff equipped with the reduced ℤ/2​N{\mathbb{Z}}/2N-grading. Then there exist a pair of Λ\Lambda-linear chain maps
ΨPSS:C​M∗​(f,Λ)→C​F∗​(H,Λ),\Psi^{\rm PSS}:CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)\to CF_{*}(H;\Lambda),
ΨSSP:C​F∗​(H,Λ)→C​M∗​(f,Λ),\Psi^{\rm SSP}:CF_{*}(H;\Lambda)\to CM_{*}(f;\Lambda),
such that their composition satisfies
ΨSSP∘ΨPSS=I​d+terms with positive T-exponent.\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}=Id+\text{terms with positive $T$-exponent}.
Proof of Theorem A.
When (M,ω)(M,\omega) and HH satisfy the assumptions in Theorem B, Theorem C implies that the induced map on homology
ΨPSS:H∗​(M,Λ)→H​F∗​(H,Λ)\Psi^{\rm PSS}:H_{*}(M;\Lambda)\to HF_{*}(H;\Lambda)
in an injection. Using the algebraic arguments in Section 4.7, we see that Theorem A holds in this setting.
It is easy to see that if [ω][\omega] lies in the image of H2​(M,ℚ)→H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Q}})\to H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) and the symplectic action of 11-periodic orbits of HH are all ℚ{\mathbb{Q}}-valued, Theorem A also holds. Indeed, one can suitably rescale the symplectic form ω\omega and the Hamiltonian HH by a positive integer to reduce to the integral setting, because such a rescaling process does not change the number of 11-periodic orbits of HH.
In general, we can choose a sequence of symplectic forms {ωk}\{\omega_{k}\} which represent rational cohomology classes and converge to ω\omega as k→∞k\to\infty. If H:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}} is nondegenerate, then for kk sufficiently large, the 11-periodic orbits of HH with respect to ωk\omega_{k} has a one-to-one correspondence with the 11-periodic orbits of HH with respect to ω\omega. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can assume that [ω][\omega] represents a rational cohomology class. Based on the elementary discussion after Hypothesis 4.1, we can modify HH to obtain a 11-periodic Hamiltonian whose 11-periodic orbits all have ℚ{\mathbb{Q}}-valued symplectic action without changing the number of 11-periodic orbits. Thus the theorem follows from the previous discussions.
∎
The proof of Theorem B has two major steps, which account for the most important novelties of this paper. First, we show that any moduli space of stable Floer trajectories can be globally presented as the zero locus of a continuous section on a smooth normally complex orbifold vector bundle over a smooth normally complex orbifold, by generalizing a recent result of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith [AMS21] which works for the moduli space of closed genus 00 JJ-holomorphic curves. Moreover, we show that these presentations are coherent, packaged using the language of flow categories introduced by Cohen–Jones–Segal [CJS95]. Second, we apply the Fukaya–Ono–Parker (FOP) perturbation scheme introduced in our early work [BX22], which was originally proposed by Fukaya–Ono [FO97], to obtain ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-valued virtual fundamental chains from the aforementioned presentations of the moduli spaces of stable Floer trajectories, and show that the induced algebraic counts from moduli spaces of virtual dimension 00 can be organized to define a chain complex over Λ\Lambda freely generated by contractible 11-periodic orbits of HH.
The proof of Theorem C is quite similar to the proof of Theorem B, except that the relevant moduli spaces parametrize solutions of the PSS/SSP equations, which are also known as the “spiked discs.” We package these moduli spaces and their presentations using the language of flow bimodules, which are investigated systematically in the symplectic context in the recent work of Abouzaid [Abo22].
Next, we give a slightly more detailed description of the technical ingredients.
1.2. Flow categories and derived orbifold presentation
In this subsection, we discuss about the geometric construction of compatible global charts for the moduli spaces arising from the proof of Theorem A.
Given a closed symplectic manifold (M,ω)(M,\omega) and a nondegenerate 11-periodic Hamiltonian H:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}, following [CJS95], one can introduce a topologically enriched category TFloerT^{{\rm Floer}} such that:
(1)
The objects are capped 11-periodic orbits of HH.
(2)
The morphism space from pp to qq is the compact Hausdorff topological space ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}, the moduli space of stable Floer trajectories connecting pp and qq.
(3)
For p,r,q∈TFloerp,r,q\in T^{{\rm Floer}}, the composition map
ℳ¯Floerp​r×ℳ¯Floerr​q→ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{rq}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
is given by the concatenation which forms a broken Floer trajectory breaking at rr. The composition maps are homeomorphisms onto their images and satisfy the natural associativity relation.
The composition maps naturally equip the space ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq} with a stratification structure, with strata indexed by words of the form pr1⋯rlqpr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q. The pr1⋯rlqpr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q-stratum of ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq} is homeomorphic to the product
ℳ¯Floerp​r1×⋯×ℳ¯Floerrl​q.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{r_{l}q}.
The moduli space ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq} is only an orbispace in general, though the usual Kuranishi reduction process describes it locally as the zero locus of a section on an orbifold vector bundle over an orbifold. Moreover, even if every ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq} has a global Kuranishi model, its restriction to the boundary stratum ℳ¯Floerp​r1×⋯×ℳ¯Floerrl​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{r_{l}q} may not be the product of the global Kuranishi models on each factor. This would obstruct any meaningful inductive construction on TFloerT^{{\rm Floer}} from these Kuranishi models, leaving alone the issue concerning the smoothness of the (thickened) moduli spaces. We solve all of these problems.
Theorem 1.1.
(See Theorem 4.7)
Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) and H:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}} be as in Theorem B. Then for any capped 11-periodic orbits p,qp,q of HH such that ℳ¯Floerp​q≠∅\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}\neq\emptyset, there exist a smooth effective normally complex orbifold with corners 𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} with the same stratification structure as ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}, a smooth normally complex orbifold vector bundle ℰp​q→𝒰p​q{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}, a continuous section 𝒮p​q:𝒰p​q→ℰp​q{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}, and a map
ψp​q:𝒮−1(0)→ℳ¯p​qFloer\psi_{pq}:{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
which defines an isomorphism of orbispaces.
We call the quadruple (𝒰p​q,ℰp​q,𝒮p​q,ψp​q)({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq},\psi_{pq}) a derived orbifold chart (D-chart for short) of ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}. Given an index pr1⋯rlqpr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q, one can take the product
(𝒰p​r1×⋯×𝒰rl​q,ℰp​r1⊞⋯⊞𝒰rl​q,𝒮p​r1⊞⋯⊞𝒮rl​q,ψp​r1×⋯×ψrl​q)({\mathcal{U}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{U}}_{r_{l}q},{\mathcal{E}}_{pr_{1}}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{U}}_{r_{l}q},{\mathcal{S}}_{pr_{1}}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{r_{l}q},\psi_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\psi_{r_{l}q})
(1.2)
which defines a D-chart of ℳ¯Floerp​r1×⋯×ℳ¯Floerrl​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{r_{l}q}.
Theorem 1.2.
(See Theorem 4.7)
The restriction of (𝒰p​q,ℰp​q,𝒮p​q,ψp​q)({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq},\psi_{pq}) to the stratum of 𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} indexed by pr1⋯rlqpr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q is isomorphic to the stabilization (Definition 2.3) of the product chart (1.2) by a complex orbifold vector bundle.
If the above data satisfy some further compatibility conditions spelled out in Definition 3.24, we call this system of derived orbifold charts of ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq} a (normally complex) derived orbifold lift of the flow category TFloerT^{{\rm Floer}}. The full statement of Theorem 4.7 exactly asserts the existence of an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift of TFloerT^{{\rm Floer}}.
As mentioned above, the construction of derived orbifold chart for a single moduli space ℳ¯Floerp​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq} is based on generalizing a recent result of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith [AMS21, Theorem 1.7]. We replace their framed JJ-holomorphic spheres by framed JJ-holomorphic cylinders and modify the perturbation method accordingly. The main difficulty is the compatibility mentioned above. To this end, we introduce the multi-layered thickening (see Section 5.3.3) to make sure that the thickened moduli space 𝒰p​r1×⋯×𝒰rl​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{U}}_{r_{l}q} is truly embedded in 𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}. To endow the space 𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} with a smooth structure, we follow the application of classical smoothing theory from [AMS21]. However, because 𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} is an orbifold with corners in general, the traditional smoothing theory does not suffice for our purpose. We develop a relative equivariant smoothing technique to construct the smoothing in an inductive fashion, which also makes the resulting smooth structures on various moduli spaces compatible.
Remark 1.3.
We expect that the construction of derived orbifold charts presented in this paper could be useful in the study of symplectic field theory (SFT) [EGH00] because the methods presented can be used to deal with punctures.
Remark 1.4.
The derived orbifold chart lift of the flow category TFloerT^{\rm Floer} should also be applied to construct certain Hamiltonian Floer homotopy theory for a complex-oriented generalized cohomology theory. As a first application, these geometric constructions should simplify the definition of Floer Morava KK-theory [AB21] because the algebraic gluing of local virtual fundamental chains should be largely simplified given these global charts.
Remark 1.5.
We would like to remark that there are some other technical issues one needs to resolve in order to carry out our perturbation scheme on the derived orbifold charts. For instance, to make sure an inductive scheme could work, one needs to guarantee that any prescribed construction on the “boundary statum” (1.2) can be extended to a neighborhood of it in (𝒰p​q,ℰp​q)({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}). To this end, a suitable collar structure is necessary and one has to keep track of the compatibility of such collar structures with the “difference bundle” appearing in Theorem 1.2. Considerations of this form partially account for the length of the later sections, because we will need a more refined geometric construction compared to the one in [AMS21].
Given a Morse function f:M→ℝf:M\to{\mathbb{R}} and a Riemannian metric gg on MM, if the pair (f,g)(f,g) satisfies certain properties stronger than the usual Morse–Smale condition, the work of Wehrheim [Weh12] shows that the moduli spaces of unpamatrized gradient flow lines of ff with respect to gg are compact smooth manifolds with corners. Therefore, one can associate (f,g)(f,g) with a flow category TMorseT^{\rm Morse} such that:
(1)
The objects are “capped” critical points of ff, see Section 4.2.
(2)
The morphism space ℳ¯x​yMorse\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{{\rm Morse}} is given by the moduli space of unparametrized gradient flow lines connecting the underlying critical points of xx and yy if the cappings are the same, otherwise it is the empty set.
(3)
The composition
ℳ¯x​zMorse×ℳ¯z​yMorse→ℳ¯x​yMorse\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xz}^{{\rm Morse}}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{zy}^{{\rm Morse}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{{\rm Morse}}
is defined to be the concatenation of gradient flow lines, which is a diffeomorphism onto its image.
To relate the two flow categories TFloerT^{\rm Floer} and TMorseT^{\rm Morse}, we introduce the PSS and SSP flow bimodules MPSSM^{\rm PSS} and MSSPM^{\rm SSP}. For instance, MPSSM^{\rm PSS} is described by the following data:
(1)
For x∈TMorsex\in T^{\rm Morse} and p∈TFloerp\in T^{\rm Floer}, we associate the pair (x,p)(x,p) with the moduli space of solutions to PSS equations ℳ¯PSSx​p\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp} connecting xx and pp.
(2)
The flow category TMorseT^{\rm Morse} acts on MPSSM^{\rm PSS} on the left, i.e., there is a map
ℳ¯Morsex​y×ℳ¯PSSy​p→ℳ¯PSSx​p\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Morse}_{xy}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{yp}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp}
satisfying an associativity condition. Geometrically, this map is again given by concatenating solutions to gradient flow line equations and Floer-type equations, and it is a homeomorphism onto its image.
(3)
The flow category TFloerT^{\rm Floer} acts on MPSSM^{\rm PSS} on the right in a similar fashion, i.e., there is a map
ℳ¯PSSx​q×ℳ¯Floerq​p→ℳ¯PSSx​p\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm PSS}_{xq}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm Floer}}_{qp}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp}
satisfying an associativity condition.
(4)
The right and left actions should satisfy certain associativity relation.
The flow bimodule MSSPM^{\rm SSP} is characterized similarly. Just like flow categories, one can define the notion of derived orbifold lift for flow bimodules (Definition 3.25). It turns out that we can indeed construct a derived orbifold lift for both MPSSM^{\rm PSS} and MSSPM^{\rm SSP} via geometric arguments.
Theorem 1.6.
(See Theorem 4.8)
Each of the flow bimodules MPSSM^{\rm PSS} and MSSPM^{\rm SSP} admits a derived orbifold lift (which extends the D-chart lift of TFloerT^{\rm Floer} and the trivial lift of TMorseT^{\rm Morse}).
In addition to these statements, the geometric underpinning of Theorem C also needs certain moduli spaces designed for interpolating between ΨSSP∘ΨPSS\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS} and the identity map on H∗​(M,Λ)H_{*}(M;\Lambda). Such a structure should be better thought of as a morphism between two flow bimodules “parametrized” by the interval [−1,1][-1,1], as exploited in detail in [Abo22]. We do not follow this viewpoint in this paper. Instead, we simply list out the necessary geometric input in Section 4 for the proof of Theorem C.
1.3. Fukaya–Ono–Parker perturbations
Now we discuss about the perturbation methods adapted in the course of the proof of Theorem A.
The perturbation scheme to be used in our construction was proposed by Fukaya–Ono [FO97] and further developed by B. Parker [Par13]. So this scheme will be referred to as the FOP perturbation scheme. We give a detailed introduction to its idea. For complete details, the reader could consult [BX22]. In a moduli problem related to pseudo-holomorphic curves, objects (such as stable maps) may have nontrivial automorphism groups; in the case of Hamiltonian Floer theory, the sphere bubbles in a stable Floer trajectory may be multiple covers carrying nontrivial automorphisms. This means that the moduli spaces have certain orbifold-type behavior. It is well-known that transversality could not always be achieved in the orbifold setting. To define invariants using abstract perturbations (see [FO99] [LT98a]), one needs to use “multi-valued perturbations” and count of transverse zeroes with ℚ{\mathbb{Q}}-valued weights. Such constructions result in ℚ{\mathbb{Q}}-valued invariants or chain complexes with ℚ{\mathbb{Q}}-coefficients. Another more algebraic reasoning to explain the appearance of ℚ{\mathbb{Q}} is that Poincaré duality for oriented orbifolds only holds over ℚ{\mathbb{Q}} (see the discussions of Pardon [Par16]). These two perspectives could account for the reasons why Gromov–Witten invariants and Hamiltonian Floer homology of a general symplectic manifold are only defined over the field of rational numbers.
Let us set up a finite-dimensional model to illustrate the failure of equivariant transversality and the proposal of Fukaya–Ono. Consider a compact smooth manifold UU acted on by a finite group Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}} and 𝑾{\bm{W}} is a representation of Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}. We may regard the orbifold U/ΓU/{\mathit{\Gamma}} as the moduli space and the orbifold vector bundle (U×𝑾)/Γ(U\times{\bm{W}})/{\mathit{\Gamma}} as the obstruction bundle. To simplify the exposition even further, let us assume dim​U=dim​𝑾{\rm dim}U={\rm dim}{\bm{W}}. In this case, a weighted count of the zeroes of an equivariant transverse maps S:U→𝑾S:U\to{\bm{W}} will give an Euler number, which is an invariant of the obstruction bundle over the orbifold U/ΓU/{\mathit{\Gamma}}. However, the equivariance of SS implies that S−1​(0)S^{-1}(0) may contain higher dimensional pieces; for example, when 𝑾{\bm{W}} contains no trivial subreprsentations, the fixed point locus UΓU^{\mathit{\Gamma}} must be contained in S−1​(0)S^{-1}(0). Hence ordinary transversality cannot be achieved equivariantly in general.
The existence of a normal complex structure allows one to consider a more delicate kind of section so that one form of equivariant transversality can hold. In the above finite-dimensional model, consider the normal bundle N​UΓ→UΓNU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to U^{\mathit{\Gamma}} over the Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}-fixed point set UΓU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}. Meanwhile, consider the decomposition
𝑾=𝑾̊Γ⊕𝑾ˇΓ{\bm{W}}=\mathring{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\oplus\check{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
where 𝑾̊Γ\mathring{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}} is the direct sum of trivial subrepresentations and 𝑾ˇΓ\check{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}} is the direct sum of nontrivial irreducible subrepresentations. A normal complex structure in this case is a Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}-equivariant complex structure on both N​UΓNU^{\mathit{\Gamma}} and 𝑾ˇΓ\check{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}. To illustrate the FOP perturbation scheme, assume for simplicity that N​UΓNU^{\mathit{\Gamma}} is trivial with fiber a complex Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}-representation 𝑽{\bm{V}} and 𝑾̊Γ={0}\mathring{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}=\{0\}. A section SS defined near UΓU^{\mathit{\Gamma}} can then be regarded as a map
f:UΓ→C∞​(𝑽,𝑾)Γ,f:U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to C^{\infty}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})^{\mathit{\Gamma}},
where C∞​(𝑽,𝑾)ΓC^{\infty}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})^{\mathit{\Gamma}} denotes the space of smooth Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}-equivariant maps from 𝑽{\bm{V}} to 𝑾{\bm{W}}. In [FO97] Fukaya–Ono proposed to consider sections corresponding to fiberwise polynomial maps
f:UΓ→PolydΓ​(𝑽,𝑾),f:U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}),
i.e., sections of EE whose restriction to each normal fiber is an equivariant complex polynomial map of degree at most dd. Assume U=UΓ×𝑽U=U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\times{\bm{V}} and denote the corresponding section by Sf:U→𝑾S_{f}:U\to{\bm{W}}. Then one has
Sf−1​(0)=UΓ∪(Sf−1​(0)∩(U∖UΓ)).S_{f}^{-1}(0)=U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\cup\big(S_{f}^{-1}(0)\cap(U\setminus U^{\mathit{\Gamma}})\big).
Although the zero locus of SfS_{f} still contains UΓU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}, Fukaya–Ono asserted that the count of the second component (modulo Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}), is an invariant, if dd is sufficiently large and ff is generic. If this is true, one could indeed define an integer-valued “Euler number” by counting the zeroes of SfS_{f} with trivial stabilizer.
A difficulty to implement Fukaya–Ono’s idea is that we need to introduce a new notion of transversality for those fiberwise polynomial maps. Certain delicate properties are needed for this transversality notion, especially when we change the cut-off degree dd for polynomial maps and when we change the isotropy groups. The crucial idea in the preprint [Par13] of Brett Parker which addresses these difficulties played an important role in the recent construction of the authors [BX22]. In short, one can define a canonical notion of transversality for those normally polynomial perturbations which behaves well when we change the degree dd and the isotropy group and which is satisfifed by generic such perturbations such that the integral counting is well-defined as proposed in [FO97].
Going back to the Floer-theoretic construction, given a derived orbifold lift of the flow category TFloerT^{\rm Floer}, we need to construct the FOP perturbation on all the thickened moduli spaces inductively. Although such a construction is cumbersome and somewhat routine, one novelty showing up is the multiplicativity of the FOP perturbation.
Theorem 1.7.
(See Corollary 2.8)
Let (𝒰i,ℰi,𝒮i)({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i}), i=1,2i=1,2 be normally complex derived orbifold charts (Definition 2.1) such that 𝒮i{\mathcal{S}}_{i} is a strongly transverse FOP section (Definition 2.7). Then the product section
𝒮1⊞𝒮2:𝒰1×𝒰2→ℰ1⊞ℰ2{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}:{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
is also a strongly transverse FOP section.
The upshot of the inductive construction of FOP perturbations can be summarized schematically as follows, after defining the relevant counts using 00-dimensional moduli spaces.
Theorem 1.8.
(See Theorem 3.49)
Using the derived orbifold lift of TFloerT^{\rm Floer}, one can define the chain complex C​F∗​(H,Λ)CF_{*}(H;\Lambda) from Theorem B after choosing a compatible family of strongly transverse FOP perturbations. Similarly, the chain maps ΨPSS\Psi^{\rm PSS}, ΨSSP\Psi^{\rm SSP}, and the homotopy
ΨSSP∘ΨPSS=I​d+O⁡(T)\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}=Id+O(T)
can be constructed by choosing a compatible family of strongly transverse FOP perturbations on the derived orbifold lifts of the relevant moduli spaces.
As a consequence, Theorem B and Theorem C hold.
Remark 1.9.
We would like to point out that the definition of FOP sections depend on an extra structure which is called a straightening (see Definition 3.31). Heuristically, it consists of a suitable Riemannian metric on 𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} and a special form of connection on ℰp​q{\mathcal{E}}_{pq} for each of the derived orbifold charts appearing in the derived orbifold lift. Again, there is an issue of compatibility with various structures on our derived orbifold lift. Although the relevant compatibility conditions will be shown to hold after a somewhat routine and cumbersome induction construction, pinning down the correct formulations seems to be a nontrivial task.
1.4. Discussions
We would like to comment on the implications of our result and some notable features of the techniques.
1.4.1. Towards stronger forms of the Arnold conjecture
One important quantity in the statement of Theorem A is the minimal Chern number. In the extremal case when N=1N=1, if the only torsion components of H∗​(M,ℤ)H_{*}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}) are ℤ/2{\mathbb{Z}}/2 and ℤ/3{\mathbb{Z}}/3, and both of them have odd degree, then they together contribute 22 to the quantity (1.1). In other words, our lower bound is only as strong as the lower bound from [AB21] in this special case. This might suggest that the sharp lower bound in the Arnold conjecture might need to incorporate certain symplectic information from the ambient symplectic manifold, as witnessed by the minimal Chern number in our statement.
On the other hand, it is possible that the methods developed in this paper might eventually resolve the strong Arnold conjecture for simply connected closed symplectic manifolds of dimension ≥6\geq 6. Indeed, following the bifurcation methods initiated in [Flo88], it should be possible to study the simple homotopy type of our integral Floer chain complex, which in turn is closely related to the stable Morse number of the ambient manifold. When the ambient manifold satisfies the condition at the beginning of this paragraph, the stable Morse number actually coincides with the Morse number.
1.4.2. Stable/normal complex structure
As emphasized in the exposition of FOP perturbations, the normal complex structure of derived orbifold charts plays a crucial role even in the definition of these polynomial-like perturbations. The closely related notion of stable complex structures also plays an important role in the construction of Floer homotopy type in [AB21], in which the stable complex structures are necessary for applying Poibcaré duality to the Morava K-theory of the classifying space of orbifolds. These structures are not necessary for the proof of Arnold conjecture over ℚ{\mathbb{Q}}. It is natural to expect more applications which make essential use of the normal/stable complex structures on the moduli spaces.
1.4.3. Algebraic structures and operations
Following the concept of “flow multimodules” as developed in [Abo22], one should be able to define multiplicative structure on the integral Floer homology. For instance, it should be possible to generalize the definition of quantum Steenrod operations on Hamiltonian Floer theory of semi-positive symplectic manifolds [Wil20] to all symplectic manifolds using the 𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}-reduction of our Hamiltonian Floer homology. Our construction of derived orbifold lifts should also be useful for regularizing moduli spaces of JJ-holomorphic curves originated from the algebraic structures.
1.5. Outline
The following describes the content of every section.
•
In Section 2, we review the differential topology related to the perturbation scheme used in this paper. Most notably, the multiplicativity of the FOP strongly transverse condition is derived in Corollary 2.14.
•
Section 3 is devoted to the discussions of flow categories, flow bimodules and their derived orbifold lifts. We show that for a flow category whose derived orbifold lift has a normal complex structure, it is possible to construct a family of compatible strongly transverse FOP perturbations so that one can define a chain complex over Λ\Lambda from these data if there is a further compatible orientation structure. The discussions culminate at Theorem 3.49. A similar result for flow bimodules is presented as well.
•
We bring back concrete symplectic geometry in Section 4. In particular, we describe the flow categories TFloerT^{\rm Floer}, TMorseT^{\rm Morse}, and flow bimodules MPSSM^{\rm PSS}, MSSPM^{\rm SSP}, and the structures of the interpolating moduli spaces in detail. After presenting the main geometric statements in Section 4.5 and the necessary input from a 11-parameter family of PSS/SSP moduli spaces (“the chain homotopy moduli spaces”) in Section 4.6, we prove our main theorem.
•
In Section 5, we show how to construct a compatible family of topological global Kuranishi charts for the moduli spaces ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}.
•
In Section 6, We discuss how to use (relative) equivariant smoothing theory to endow the global Kuranishi charts constructed in Section 5 with smooth structures. Moreover, we describe how to construct a normally complex lift of all derived orbifold lifts.
•
The parallel constructions for PSS, SSP type moduli spaces as in Section 5 and Section 6 are presented in Section 7. Because most of the arguments are only a matter of cosmetic modification, most of the proofs are only sketched.
Remark 1.10.
We were informed by Semon Rezchikov on September 14, 2022 that he had an independent approach towards similar results following our early work [BX22].
