2. Recap of the FOP natural transformation and multiplicativity
In this section we briefly review the construction of [BX22] concerning the Fukaya–Ono–Parker perturbations and prove an additional property of FOP perturbations regarding the multiplicativity. Some necessary ingredients from the theory of Whitney stratifications are provided in Appendix A.
2.1. Orbifolds and the canonical Whitney stratification on 𝒵d\mathcal{Z}_{d}
Now we describe the setup for FOP perturbations. In this section we consider effective22
2
A stabilization can make a non-effective orbifold effective. orbifolds only. An nn-dimensional orbifold is a locally compact, Hausdorff, and second countable topological space 𝒰{\mathcal{U}} equipped with an atlas of orbifold charts: each chart is of the form C=(U,Γ,ψ)C=(U,{\mathit{\Gamma}},\psi) where Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}} is a finite group with an effective linear action on ℝn{\mathbb{R}}^{n}, U⊂ℝnU\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n} is an invariant open subset, and ψ:U→𝒰\psi:U\to{\mathcal{U}} is a Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}-invariant continuous map such that the induced map U/Γ→𝒰U/{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to{\mathcal{U}} is a homeomorphism onto an open subset of 𝒰{\mathcal{U}}. There is a compatibility requirement for overlapping charts which we will not recall here. In notation the map ψ\psi is often suppressed and we identify its image with U/ΓU/{\mathit{\Gamma}}. Similarly, over an orbifold 𝒰{\mathcal{U}}, an orbifold vector bundle consists of an orbifold ℰ{\mathcal{E}}, a continuous map πℰ:ℰ→𝒰\pi_{\mathcal{E}}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}, and an atlas of bundle charts of ℰ{\mathcal{E}}. A bundle chart consists of a chart C=(U,Γ)C=(U,{\mathit{\Gamma}}) of the base 𝒰{\mathcal{U}}, a Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}-equivariant vector bundle π:E→U\pi:E\to U, and a homeomorphism E/Γ≅πℰ−1​(U/Γ)E/{\mathit{\Gamma}}\cong\pi_{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}(U/{\mathit{\Gamma}}) which is compatible with the projections. We denote by the triple (Γ,E,U)({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U) a bundle chart. We ofter use charts which are centered at certain points of the orbifold. For each point x∈𝒰x\in{\mathcal{U}}, an orbifold chart centered at xx is a chart Cx=(Ux,Γx,ψx)C_{x}=(U_{x},{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},\psi_{x}) where Ux⊂ℝnU_{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n} is an invariant open neighborhood of the origin, such that ψx​(0)=x\psi_{x}(0)=x. A bundle chart for ℰ→𝒰{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}} centered at xx is then denoted by (Γx,Ex,Ux)({\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},E_{x},U_{x}) where (Γx,Ux)({\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},U_{x}) is an orbifold chart centered at xx and Ex→UxE_{x}\to U_{x} is a Γx{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}-equivariant vector bundle.
We need a few other frequently used notations. Let (Γ,E,U)({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U) be a bundle chart. For each subgroup G⊂ΓG\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}}, denote by UG⊂UU^{G}\subset U the fixed point locus of the induced GG-action and denote by N​UGNU^{G} the normal bundle of UG↪UU^{G}\hookrightarrow U. Then the fibers of N​UGNU^{G} are representations of GG whose decompositions into the direct sum of irreducible representations contain no trivial summand. We can also decompose the restriction
E|UG=E̊G⊕EˇGE|_{U^{G}}=\mathring{E}^{G}\oplus\check{E}^{G}
where E̊G⊂E\mathring{E}^{G}\subset E is the subbundle whose fibers are the maximal trivial subrepresentations and EˇG\check{E}^{G} is the complement, whose fibers are direct sums of nontrivial irreducible representations.
2.1.1. Derived orbifold charts
We recall the notion of derived orbifold charts introduced in [Par21, Section 5] and used in [BX22], as well as a few related concepts. We will soon generalize this notion to the case of orbifolds with faces but this generalization is straightforward.
Definition 2.1.
(1)
An (effective) derived orbifold chart (D-chart for short) is a triple (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) where 𝒰{\mathcal{U}} is an effective orbifold, ℰ→𝒰{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}} is an orbifold vector bundle, and 𝒮:𝒰→ℰ{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}} is a continuous section. We say the triple (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is compact if 𝒮−1​(0){\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0) is compact.
(2)
A (smooth) perturbation of a compact derived orbifold chart (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is a smooth section 𝒮′:𝒰→ℰ{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}} such that there exists a precompact open neighborhood 𝒟{\mathcal{D}} of 𝒮−1​(0){\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0) and a continuous norm on ℰ{\mathcal{E}} such that
‖𝒮−𝒮′‖C0​(𝒰∖𝒟)<infx∈𝒰∖𝒟|𝒮⁡(x)|.\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}({\mathcal{U}}\setminus{\mathcal{D}})}<\inf_{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}}|{\mathcal{S}}(x)|.
In particular, (𝒮′)−1​(0)({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0) is still contained in 𝒟{\mathcal{D}} and hence (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮′)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}) is also a compact derived orbifold chart.
(3)
A normal complex structure on a derived orbifold chart (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) consists of, for each bundle chart (Γ,E,U)({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U) and for each subgroup G⊆ΓG\subseteq{\mathit{\Gamma}}, a GG-invariant complex structure IGI_{G} on N​UGNU^{G} and a GG-invariant complex structure JGJ_{G} on EˇG\check{E}^{G}. Moreover, these complex structures are compatible in the following sense.
•
Within the same chart, for each pair of subgroups H⊂G⊂ΓH\subset G\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}} for which we have HH-equivariant inclusions
N​UH|UG⊂N​UG,\displaystyle\ NU^{H}|_{U^{G}}\subset NU^{G},\
EˇH|UG⊂EˇG\displaystyle\ \check{E}^{H}|_{U^{G}}\subset\check{E}^{G}
we require that they are complex linear with respect to the complex structures IGI_{G}, JGJ_{G} and IH,JHI_{H},J_{H}.
•
The system of invariant complex structures are compatible with chart embeddings.
(4)
A straightening of a derived orbifold chart (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) consists of a Riemannian metric on 𝒰{\mathcal{U}} and a connection on ℰ{\mathcal{E}} satisfying the following conditions.
•
For each chart (U,E,Γ)(U,E,{\mathit{\Gamma}}), the pullback Riemannian metric gUg_{U} is “straightened.” Namely, for each subgroup G⊂ΓG\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}}, near UGU^{G}, the ambient Riemannian metric gUg_{U} agrees with the bundle metric on N​UGNU^{G} induced by gUg_{U} via the exponential map along the normal directions.
•
For each chart (U,E,Γ)(U,E,{\mathit{\Gamma}}), the pullback connection ∇E\nabla^{E} on EE is “straightened.” Namely, for each subgroup G⊂ΓG\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}}, we can identify a neighborhood of UGU^{G} with a neighborhood of the zero section of N​UGNU^{G} using the exponential map associated with gUg_{U}. After identifying E|N​UGE|_{NU^{G}} with the pullback of E|UGE|_{U^{G}} using the projection N​UG→UGNU^{G}\to U^{G} and the parallel transport along normal geodesics using ∇E\nabla^{E}, the connection ∇E\nabla^{E} agrees with the pullback connection of the restriction of ∇E\nabla^{E} to UGU^{G}.
Lemma 2.2.
[BX22, Lemma 3.15, Lemma 3.20] For a compact derived orbifold chart (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) there exists a straightening in a neighborhood of 𝒮−1​(0){\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0).
There are several natural relations between derived orbifold charts.
Definition 2.3.
(1)
An open embedding from a derived orbifold chart (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) to (𝒰′,ℰ′,𝒮′)({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}) consists of an open embedding ϕ:𝒰→𝒰′\phi:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime} of orbifolds and a bundle isomorphism ϕ^:ℰ→ℰ′|ϕ⁡(𝒰)\widehat{\phi}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}|_{\phi({\mathcal{U}})} covering ϕ\phi such that ϕ^∘𝒮=𝒮′∘ϕ\widehat{\phi}\circ{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\circ\phi and (𝒮′)−1​(0)⊂ϕ⁡(𝒰)({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\subset\phi({\mathcal{U}}).
(2)
A germ of open embeddings from a derived orbifold chart (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) to (𝒰′,ℰ′,𝒮′)({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}) is an equivalence class of open embeddings from an open neighborhood of 𝒮−1​(0){\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0) with the restrictions of ℰ{\mathcal{E}} and 𝒮{\mathcal{S}} over it, to (𝒰′,ℰ′,𝒮′)({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}), where the equivalence relation is induced by shrinking the neighborhood. Such a germ is called to induce a germ equivalence.
(3)
The product of derived orbifold charts (𝒰i,ℰi,𝒮i)({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i}), i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k, is the derived orbifold chart
(𝒰1×⋯×𝒰k,ℰ1⊞⋯⊞ℰk,𝒮1⊞⋯⊞𝒮k).({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{U}}_{k},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{k},{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{k}).
(4)
Let 𝒞=(𝒰,ℰ,𝒮){\mathcal{C}}=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) be a derived orbifold chart and πℱ:ℱ→𝒰\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{U}} be another orbifold vector bundle. The stabilization of 𝒞{\mathcal{C}} by ℱ{\mathcal{F}} is the chart
Stabℱ​(𝒞)=(ℱ,πℱ∗​ℰ⊕πℱ∗​ℱ,πℱ∗​𝒮⊕τℱ){\rm Stab}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{C}})=({\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}\oplus\tau_{\mathcal{F}})
where τℱ:ℱ→πℱ∗​ℱ\tau_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}} is the tautological section.
Remark 2.4.
The stabilization operation intertwines with many other constructions. For example, suppose ϕ:𝒞→𝒞′\phi:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime} is an open embedding and ℱ′→𝒰′{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime} is a orbifold vector bundle. Denote by ℱ{\mathcal{F}} the pullback bundle ϕ∗​ℱ\phi^{*}{\mathcal{F}}. Then there is an obvious extension of ϕ\phi which defines an open embedding from Stabℱ​(𝒞){\rm Stab}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{C}}) into Stabℱ′​(𝒞′){\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}). On the other hand, if 𝒞{\mathcal{C}} is equipped with a straightening, one can endow the stabilization Stabℱ​(𝒞){\rm Stab}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{C}}) with a straightening naturally once ℱ{\mathcal{F}} is equipped with a bundle metric and a compatible connection.
2.1.2. Equivariant polynomial maps
To describe the use of FOP sections we need to recall some basic properties of equivariant polynomial maps. Let GG be a finite group and 𝑽,𝑾{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}} be finite dimensional complex GG-representations. Let PolyG​(𝑽,𝑾){\rm Poly}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}) be the space of equivariant complex polynomial maps from VV to WW, and for each nonnegative integer dd, let PolydG​(𝑽,𝑾)⊂PolyG​(𝑽,𝑾){\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\subset{\rm Poly}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}) be the subspace of maps with degree at most dd. There is a natural GG-equivariant evaluation map
ev:𝑽×PolydG​(𝑽,𝑾)→𝑾{\rm ev}:{\bm{V}}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\to{\bm{W}}
whose zero locus is denoted by
Z:=ZdG:=ZdG​(𝑽,𝑾).Z:=Z_{d}^{G}:=Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}).
(2.1)
This is a complex algebraic variety.
The application of FOP sections crucially relies on the existence of certain Whitney stratifications on the above variety. By a classical theorem of Whitney [Whi65], any complex algebraic subvariety inside a smooth variety admits a canonical Whitney stratification (see Theorem A.4) whose strata are smooth algebraic submanifolds. However, for the variety ZZ (2.1), this canonical Whitney stratification may not respect the group action. One needs to use a “more symmetric” Whitney stratification with a few nice properties—this was the observation of B. Parker [Par13]. The necessity of having such nice properties comes from the consideration that we need to consider the Whitney stratifications on the variety ZZ for different cut-off degrees dd and different groups GG. In our previous work [BX22, Theorem 4.3] we proved the following result showing the existence of certain canonical “symmetric” Whitney stratifications which have the desired nice properties.
Theorem 2.5.
There exists a unique Whitney stratification on ZdG​(𝐕,𝐖)Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}) subject to the following conditions.
(1)
For each subgroup H⊂GH\subset G, let 𝑽H∗⊂𝑽{\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\subset{\bm{V}} be the subset of points whose stabilizer is exactly HH. Then for each x∈ZdG​(𝑽,𝑾)∩(𝑽H∗×PolydG​(𝑽,𝑾))x\in Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\cap({\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})), the germ through xx is contained in 𝑽H∗×PolydG​(𝑽,𝑾){\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}).
(2)
The Whitney stratification is the minimal one among all which satisfy the above condition.
Moreover, this Whitney stratification enjoys the following additional properties.
(1)
It is GG-invariant.
(2)
It is induced from a Whitney prestratification (see Definition A.1 and A.2) on ZZ whose strata are all algebraic submanifolds.
(3)
It is invariant under all GG-equivariant diffeomorphisms of 𝑽×PolydG​(𝑽,𝑾){\bm{V}}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}) which preserve ZZ.
The nice behaviors of the canonical Whitney stratification allow us to extend our consideration to bundles. Let BB be a smooth manifold acted on trivially by a finite group GG. Let V,W→BV,W\to B be smooth GG-equivariant complex vector bundles with fibers isomorphic to representations 𝑽{\bm{V}} and 𝑾{\bm{W}} respectively. Such datum defines a locally trivial bundle
PolydG​(V,W)→B{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)\to B
whose fiber at x∈Bx\in B is the space PolydG​(Vx,Wx){\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x}). Then there is a subbundle
𝒵dG​(V,W)⊂V⊕PolydG​(V,W){\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W)\subset V\oplus{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
whose fiber at xx is the zero locus associated with ev:Vx×PolydG​(Vx,Wx)→Wx{\rm ev}:V_{x}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x})\to W_{x}. Using the invariance property of the canonical Whitney stratification of ZdG​(𝑽,𝑾)Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}) under GG-equivariant diffeomorphisms, the canonical Whitney stratification on the fibers can be “patched together” to define a canonical Whitney stratification on the fiber bundle 𝒵dG​(V,W){\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W) which is “locally trivial.”
Another statement relevant to us, which was originally proved by Fukaya–Ono, says that when dd is sufficiently large, the variety ZZ is a union of smooth pieces. For a proof, the readers could refer to [BX22, Proposition 4.9].
Theorem 2.6.
For dd sufficiently large, for each subgroup H⊂GH\subset G, the locus
ZdG​(𝑽,𝑾)H:=ZdG​(𝑽,𝑾)∩(𝑽H∗∩PolydG​(𝑽,𝑾))Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})_{H}:=Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\cap({\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\cap{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}))
is a smooth algebraic submanifold of complex dimension
dimℂPolydG​(𝑽,𝑾)+dimℂ𝑽̊H−dimℂ𝑾̊H.\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})+\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathring{\bm{V}}^{H}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathring{\bm{W}}^{H}.
2.1.3. FOP sections and strong transversality
Now we recall the notion of normally complex sections (which we call by FOP sections, owing credit to Fukaya–Ono and B. Parker), which is Parker’s generalization of the notion of normally polynomial sections.
We first consider the case for a single chart. Let BB be a smooth manifold, GG be a finite group trivially acting on BB, and πV:V→B\pi_{V}:V\to B, πW:W→B\pi_{W}:W\to B be GG-equivariant complex vector bundles. Choose a positive integer dd. Then there is a vector bundle
PolydG​(V,W)→B{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)\to B
whose fiber over each b∈Bb\in B is the vector space PolydG​(Vx,Wx){\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x}) of GG-equivariant polynomial maps with degree at most dd. Let Vϵ⊂VV_{\epsilon}\subset V be an open GG-invariant disk subbundle with respect to an auxiliary bundle metric. Consider smooth sections of the pullback bundle πV∗​W→Vϵ\pi_{V}^{*}W\to V_{\epsilon}.
Definition 2.7.
Let s:Vϵ→πV∗​Ws:V_{\epsilon}\to\pi_{V}^{*}W be a smooth GG-equivariant section.
(1)
ss is called a normally polynomial section of degree at most dd if its restriction to each fiber Vx∩VϵV_{x}\cap V_{\epsilon} coincides with the restriction of an element of PolydG​(Vx,Wx){\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x}).
(2)
ss is called a normally complex section of degree at most dd if for each (x,v)∈Vϵ(x,v)\in V_{\epsilon}, there exists a smooth GG-equivariant bundle map f:Vϵ→PolydG​(V,W)f:V_{\epsilon}\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W) such that for points (x′,v′)(x^{\prime},v^{\prime}) near (x,v)(x,v), one has
s⁡(x′,v′)=f⁡(x′,v′)​(v′).s(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})=f(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})(v^{\prime}).
The map ff is called a local lift of ss near (x,v)(x,v). We also call a normally complex section an FOP section.
(3)
An FOP section ss is called strongly transverse at (x,v)∈Vϵ(x,v)\in V_{\epsilon} if for any local lift f:V→PolydG​(V,W)f:V\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W) near (x,v)(x,v), the graph of ff, as a submanifold of the total space of Vϵ⊕PolydG​(V,W)V_{\epsilon}\oplus{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W), is transverse to the canonical Whitney stratification of 𝒵dG​(V,W){\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W) near (x,v)(x,v), i.e., the graph of ff is transverse to all the strata of the canonical Whitney (pre)stratification of 𝒵dG​(V,W){\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W).
With the above preparations, now we consider the global situation for a normally complex derived orbifold chart.
Definition 2.8.
Let (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) be a compact normally complex derived orbifold chart equipped with a straightening.
(1)
A section 𝒮′:𝒰→ℰ{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}} is called an FOP section if for each bundle chart (Γ,E,U)({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U) for which 𝒮′{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime} lifts to a Γ{\mathit{\Gamma}}-equivariant section S:U→ES:U\to E, the following condition is true. Over UΓU^{\mathit{\Gamma}} we can decompose E|UΓ=E̊Γ⊕EˇΓE|_{U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}=\mathring{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\oplus\check{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}. The straightening induces an identification of a tubular neighborhood of UΓU^{\mathit{\Gamma}} with a disk bundle Nϵ​UΓN_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}} inside the normal bundle N​UΓ→UΓNU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to U^{\mathit{\Gamma}} as well as an equivariant bundle isomorphism
E|Nϵ​UΓ≅πN​UΓ∗​E̊Γ⊕πN​UΓ∗​EˇΓ.E|_{N_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}\cong\pi_{NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}^{*}\mathring{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\oplus\pi_{NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}^{*}\check{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}.
Then with respect to this splitting we can decompose
S|Nϵ​UΓ=(S̊,Sˇ).S|_{N_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}=(\mathring{S},\check{S}).
We require that Sˇ:Nϵ​UΓ→πN​UΓ∗​EˇΓ\check{S}:N_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to\pi_{NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}^{*}\check{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}} is an FOP section.
(2)
An FOP perturbation of (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is a smooth perturbation 𝒮′:𝒰→ℰ{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}} (see Definition 2.1) such that 𝒮′{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime} is an FOP section near its zero locus.
(3)
An FOP section 𝒮′:𝒰→ℰ{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}} is called strongly transverse at x∈𝒰x\in{\mathcal{U}} if the following conditions are satisfied. Let (Γx,Ux,Ex)({\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},U_{x},E_{x}) be a bundle chart centered at xx. By shrinking the chart we assume that UxU_{x} is identified with a disk bundle Nϵ​UxΓxN_{\epsilon}U_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}} of the normal bundle N​UxΓxNU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}} of UxΓxU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}} and 𝒮′{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime} lifts to an equivariant section Sx:Ux→ExS_{x}:U_{x}\to E_{x}. With respect to the splitting (induced from the straightening)
Ex≅πN​UxΓx∗​E̊x⊕πN​UxΓx∗​EˇxE_{x}\cong\pi_{NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}}^{*}\mathring{E}_{x}\oplus\pi_{NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}}^{*}\check{E}_{x}
we write Sx=(S̊x,Sˇx)S_{x}=(\mathring{S}_{x},\check{S}_{x}). Then we require that there exists an local lift f:Nϵ​UxΓx→PolydΓx​(N​UxΓx,EˇxΓx)f:N_{\epsilon}U_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}) of Sˇx\check{S}_{x} such that the induced bundle map
(S̊x,graph​f):Nϵ​UxΓx→E̊xΓx⊕(N​UxΓx⊕PolydΓx​(N​UxΓx,Eˇx))(\mathring{S}_{x},{\rm graph}f):N_{\epsilon}U_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\to\mathring{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\oplus(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\oplus{\rm Poly}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}))
is transverse to the subbundle {0}⊕𝒵dΓx​(N​UxΓx,EˇxΓx)\{0\}\oplus{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}) with respect to the canonical Whitney stratification at the point 0∈Ux0\in U_{x}.
Remark 2.9.
The strong transversality condition of FOP sections presented as above a priori depends on the choices of bundle charts and the cut-off degree dd appearing in PolydΓx​(N​UxΓx,EˇxΓx){\rm Poly}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}). The most important output from [BX22, Section 4] is showing that the canonical Whitney stratification on 𝒵dG​(V,W){\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W) is compatible with the change of the cut-off degree dd and the group GG, when interpreted suitably. The upshot is, once fixing a straightening datum of (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}), the strong transversality condition is in fact intrinsic. The readers could refer to [BX22] for details, and we continue our discussions with such background in mind.
It is a general fact that generic smooth maps are transverse to a given Whitney stratified object. As a consequence, a generic FOP section is strongly transverse. We formulate this fact as the following Proposition.
Proposition 2.10.
[BX22, Proposition 6.4]
Let (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) be a compact normally complex derived orbifold chart equipped with a straightening. Fix a continuous norm on ℰ{\mathcal{E}}. Fix a precompact open neighborhood 𝒟⊂𝒰{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{U}} of 𝒮−1​(0){\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0).
(1)
(Absolute version) Given ϵ>0\epsilon>0, there exists a smooth section 𝒮ϵ:𝒰→ℰ{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}} satisfying the following conditions.
(a)
𝒮ϵ{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon} is an FOP section in a neighborhood of 𝒟¯\overline{\mathcal{D}} and it is strongly transverse near 𝒟¯\overline{\mathcal{D}}.
(b)
‖𝒮−𝒮ϵ‖C0​(𝒟)<ϵ\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}({\mathcal{D}})}<\epsilon.
(2)
(Relative version) More generally, let K⊂𝒰K\subset{\mathcal{U}} be a compact subset, 𝒰′⊂𝒰{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}} be an open neighborhood of KK. Suppose we are given a section 𝒮1′:𝒰′→ℰ|𝒰′{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{E}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}} which is an FOP section and strongly transverse near K∩𝒟¯K\cap\overline{\mathcal{D}}. Then there exists a smooth perturbation 𝒮′:𝒰→ℰ{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}} which is an FOP section and strongly transverse near 𝒟¯\overline{\mathcal{D}}, such that 𝒮′{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime} coincides with 𝒮1′{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime} near KK. In addition, if ϵ>0\epsilon>0 is sufficiently small and ‖𝒮−𝒮1′‖C0<ϵ\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}}<\epsilon, then we can choose 𝒮′{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime} such that ‖𝒮−𝒮′‖C0<2​ϵ\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}}<2\epsilon.
The relative version of the above proposition is often referred to as a “CUDV” type statement. This means that a good perturbation has been constructed on an open neighborhood UU of a closed subset CC and we would like to find a good perturbation on a neighborhood of C∪DC\cup D where DD is another closed subset, while we want to maintain the original perturbation near CC and do not change anything outside an open neighborhood VV of D∖CD\setminus C.
Moreover, as proposed by Fukaya–Ono, the isotropy free part of the zero locus of a strongly transverse FOP section should induce a homology class.
Proposition 2.11.
Let (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) be an oriented (i.e., both 𝒰{\mathcal{U}} and ℰ{\mathcal{E}} are oriented) and compact normally complex derived orbifold chart equipped with a straightening. Let 𝒰∗⊂𝒰{\mathcal{U}}^{*}\subset{\mathcal{U}} be the manifold part, i.e., the open and dense subset of points whose isotropy groups are trivial. Then the following is true.
(1)
Let 𝒮′{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime} be a strongly transverse FOP perturbation. Then the set (𝒮′)−1​(0)∩𝒰∗({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}^{*} is an oriented smooth submanifold of 𝒰∗{\mathcal{U}}^{*} of real dimension being dim​𝒰−rank​ℰ{\rm dim}{\mathcal{U}}-{\rm rank}{\mathcal{E}} and the inclusion map (𝒮′)−1​(0)∩𝒰∗↪𝒰({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}^{*}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{U}} is an oriented pseudocycle, hence represents an integral homology class. 33
3
In [BX22] we extend the notion of pseudocycles in manifolds to general Thom–Mather stratified spaces (including orbifolds) and proved that they represent integral homology classes.
(2)
The resulting homology class, called the FOP Euler class, denoted by
χFOP​(𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)∈H∗​(𝒰,ℤ),\chi^{\rm FOP}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})\in H_{*}({\mathcal{U}};{\mathbb{Z}}),
is independent of the choice of strongly transverse FOP perturbations and is independent of the choice of straightening, and hence is an invariant of the normally complex derived orbifold chart.
Remark 2.12.
In fact there exist a collection of homology classes associated to a derived orbifold chart indexed by a finite group and a pair of complex representations. The FOP Euler class in Proposition 2.11 is the leading one in this collection.
2.2. FOP sections and products
In this subsection we prove that the product of strongly transverse FOP sections is still strongly transverse. This is a necessary ingredient for the inductive construction of perturbations in Floer theory. Moreover, we show that the natural transformation ℱ​O​Pγ{\mathcal{F}OP}_{\gamma} from the stably complex derived orbifold bordism Ω¯∗ℂ,der\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},{\rm der}} to the integral homology constructed by [BX22, Theorem 1.4] is multiplicative.
2.2.1. Products of transverse FOP sections
We fix our notations. Let GiG_{i}, i=1,2i=1,2 be finite groups. Let 𝑽i,𝑾i{\bm{V}}_{i},{\bm{W}}_{i} be complex GiG_{i}-representations. Then 𝑽1⊕𝑽2{\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2} and 𝑾1⊕𝑾2{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2} are G1×G2G_{1}\times G_{2} representations under the product action. Choose nonnegative integers d,d1,d2d,d_{1},d_{2} such that d≥d1,d2d\geq d_{1},d_{2}. Consider the space
PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2).{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}).
It has a subspace
Polyd1G1​(𝑽1,𝑾1)×Polyd2G2​(𝑽2,𝑾2).{\rm Poly}_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1})\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2}).
One also has the inclusion of ZZ-varieties
Zd1G1​(𝑽1,𝑾1)×Zd2G2​(𝑽2,𝑾2)⊂ZdG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2).Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1})\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2})\subset Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}).
Abbreviate the three ZZ-varieties as Zd1G1Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}, Zd2G2Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}, and ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} respectively.
Proposition 2.13.
When d1,d2d_{1},d_{2} are sufficiently large, the inclusion
ϕ:(𝑽1×Polyd1G1​(𝑽1,𝑾1))×(𝑽2×Polyd2G2​(𝑽2,𝑾2))↪(𝑽1⊕𝑽2)×PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2)\phi:\big({\bm{V}}_{1}\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1})\big)\times\big({\bm{V}}_{2}\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2})\big)\\
\hookrightarrow({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})
is transverse to all the strata of the canonical Whitney stratification on ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}. Moreover, the inclusion pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification on the target to the canonical (product) Whitney stratification on the domain.
The proof is given in Subsection 2.3.
Corollary 2.14.
Let (𝒰i,ℰi,𝒮i)({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i}), i=1,2i=1,2 be normally complex derived orbifold charts such that 𝒮i{\mathcal{S}}_{i} is a strongly transverse FOP section. Then the product section
𝒮1⊞𝒮2:𝒰1×𝒰2→ℰ1⊞ℰ2{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}:{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
is also a strongly transverse FOP section.
Proof.
As transversality is defined locally, it suffices to restrict our consideration to local charts. Let (Ui,Ei,Gi)(U_{i},E_{i},G_{i}) be a bundle chart of ℰi{\mathcal{E}}_{i} centered at xi∈𝒰ix_{i}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i} such that 𝒮i{\mathcal{S}}_{i} is pulled back to a GiG_{i}-equivariant section
Si:Ui→Ei.S_{i}:U_{i}\to E_{i}.
Suppose N​UiGiNU_{i}^{G_{i}} is trivial with fiber 𝑽i{\bm{V}}_{i} and EiE_{i} is trivial with fiber 𝑾i{\bm{W}}_{i}. Decompose 𝑾i=𝑾̊iGi⊕𝑾ˇiGi{\bm{W}}_{i}=\mathring{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}}\oplus\check{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}}. Then near N​UiGiNU_{i}^{G_{i}} we can write
Si=(S̊i,Sˇi).S_{i}=(\mathring{S}_{i},\check{S}_{i}).
By the definition of FOP sections, there exists a local lift of Sˇi\check{S}_{i} near the origin
fi:UiGi×𝑽i→PolydiGi​(𝑽i,𝑾ˇiGi).f_{i}:U_{i}^{G_{i}}\times{\bm{V}}_{i}\to{\rm Poly}_{d_{i}}^{G_{i}}({\bm{V}}_{i},\check{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}}).
By assumption, S̊i\mathring{S}_{i} is transverse to 0∈𝑾̊i0\in\mathring{\bm{W}}_{i} (in the usual sense). Denote U=U1×U2U=U_{1}\times U_{2}, 𝑾=𝑾1⊕𝑾2{\bm{W}}={\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}. Then
𝑾̊G1×G2=𝑾̊1G1⊕𝑾̊2G2.\mathring{\bm{W}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}=\mathring{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{1}}\oplus\mathring{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}}.
Hence S̊=(S̊1,S̊2)\mathring{S}=(\mathring{S}_{1},\mathring{S}_{2}) is transverse to 0∈𝑾̊G1×G20\in\mathring{\bm{W}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} and
S̊−1​(0)=S̊1−1​(0)×S̊2−1​(0).\mathring{S}^{-1}(0)=\mathring{S}_{1}^{-1}(0)\times\mathring{S}_{2}^{-1}(0).
Hence we may assume that 𝑾̊1G1=0\mathring{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{1}}=0, 𝑾̊2G2=0\mathring{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}}=0. Next, the graph of fif_{i} is transverse to UiGi×ZdiGi​(𝑽i,𝑾ˇiGi)U_{i}^{G_{i}}\times Z_{d_{i}}^{G_{i}}({\bm{V}}_{i},\check{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}}). Notice that
graph⁡(f)=graph⁡(f1×f2)=graph⁡(f1)×graph⁡(f2).{\rm graph}(f)={\rm graph}(f_{1}\times f_{2})={\rm graph}(f_{1})\times{\rm graph}(f_{2}).
It follows that the graph of ff is transverse to the product Whitney stratification on UG1×G2×Zd1G1​(𝑽1,𝑾ˇ1G2)×Zd2G2​(𝑽2,𝑾ˇ2G2)U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},\check{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{2}})\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},\check{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}}). If we view ff as a map
f:UG1×G2×𝑽1×𝑽2→Polymax⁡(d1,d2)G1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾ˇ1G1⊕𝑾ˇ2G2),f:U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times{\bm{V}}_{1}\times{\bm{V}}_{2}\to{\rm Poly}_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},\check{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{1}}\oplus\check{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}}),
it is easy to see that the intersection between graph⁡(f){\rm graph}(f) and UG1×G2×Zmax⁡(d1,d2)G1×G2U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} is contained in UG1×G2×Zd1G1×Zd2G2U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}. By Proposition 2.13, as the inclusion Zd1G1×Zd2G2↪Zmax⁡(d1,d2)G1×G2Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}\hookrightarrow Z_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} respects the canonical Whitney stratifications, we see that graph⁡(f){\rm graph}(f) is transverse to all the strata of UG1×G2×Zmax⁡(d1,d2)G1×G2U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}. By definition, this means that S=S1×S2S=S_{1}\times S_{2} is strongly transverse at x=(x1,x2)x=(x_{1},x_{2}).
∎
2.2.2. Multiplicativity of the FOP natural transformation
A consequence of previous discussions is that the FOP natural transformation defined in [BX22] is natural with respect to products. Although this result will not be used in the setting of Floer theory, we include it here.
Let us recall the relevant notations. An isotropy type is a triple (G,𝑽,𝑾)(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}) where GG is a finite group, 𝑽,𝑾{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}} are finite-dimensional complex GG-representations which do not contain trivial GG-summands. A stable isotropy type is an equivalence classes of isotropy types with respect to the equivalence relation generated by
(G,𝑽,𝑾)∼(G,𝑽⊕𝑹,𝑾⊕𝑹)(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\sim(G,{\bm{V}}\oplus{\bm{R}},{\bm{W}}\oplus{\bm{R}})
where 𝑹{\bm{R}} is a nontrivial irreducible complex representation of GG. A stable isotropy type is denoted by γ\gamma or [G,𝑽,𝑾][G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}] if (G,𝑽,𝑾)(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}) represents it. To discuss products, we define the multiplication of (stable) isotropy types in the obvious way:
[G1,𝑽1,𝑾1]×[G2,𝑽2,𝑾2]:=[G1×G2,𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2].[G_{1},{\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1}]\times[G_{2},{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2}]:=[G_{1}\times G_{2},{\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}].
The pseudocycles defined by strongly transverse FOP sections induce natural transformations of generalized homology theories. First, for a topological space YY one can define the stably complex derived orbifold bordism group
Ω¯(Y)ℂ,der∗\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y)
generated by isomorphism classed of quadruples (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮,f)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f) where (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is a compact stably complex derived orbifold chart and f:𝒰→Yf:{\mathcal{U}}\to Y is a continuous map, modulo the equivalence relations generated by
(1)
(Restriction) (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮,f)∼(𝒰′,ℰ′,𝒮′,f′)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f)\sim({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},f^{\prime}) if 𝒰′⊂𝒰{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}} is an open neighborhood of 𝒮−1​(0){\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0) and ℰ′=ℰ|𝒰′{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{E}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}, 𝒮′=𝒮|𝒰′{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{S}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}, and f′=f|𝒰′f^{\prime}=f|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}.
(2)
(Stabilization) (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮,f)∼(𝒰′,ℰ′,𝒮′,f′)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f)\sim({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},f^{\prime}) if 𝒰′{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime} is equal to the total space of a vector bundle πℱ:ℱ→𝒰\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{U}}, ℰ′=πℱ∗​ℰ⊕πℱ∗​ℱ{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}, 𝒮′=πℱ∗​𝒮⊕τℱ{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}\oplus\tau_{\mathcal{F}} where τℱ:ℱ→πℱ∗​ℱ\tau_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}} is the tautological section, and f′=f∘πℱf^{\prime}=f\circ\pi_{\mathcal{F}}.
(3)
(Cobordism) (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮,f)∼(𝒰′,ℰ′,𝒮′,f′)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f)\sim({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},f^{\prime}) if there is a bordism between them extending the stable complex structures.
A stable complex structure on (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is roughly a lift of the virtual bundle T​𝒰−ℰT{\mathcal{U}}-{\mathcal{E}} to a complex virtual bundle. Disjoint union of derived orbifold charts induces the group structure on Ω¯(Y)ℂ,der∗\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y). For details of the relevant terminologies, the readers could refer to [BX22, Section 7]. For each stable isotropy type γ\gamma represented by (G,𝑽,𝑾)(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}), denote nγ=dimℝ​𝑽−dimℝ​𝑾n_{\gamma}={\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}{\bm{V}}-{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}{\bm{W}}. Then in [BX22] we constructed a natural transformation of generalized homology theories made of linear maps (see [BX22, Theorem 1.4])
ℱ𝒪𝒫γY:Ω¯(Y)ℂ,der∗→H∗−nγ(Y;ℤ).\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma}^{Y}:\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y)\to H_{*-n_{\gamma}}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}}).
The main consequence of the fact that strong transversality of FOP perturbations is preserved after taking products regarding the stably complex derived bordism theory is the following theorem.
Theorem 2.15.
Let Y1Y_{1} and Y2Y_{2} be topological spaces and γ1,γ2\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} be stable isotropy types. Then the follow diagram is commutative:
Ω¯(Y1)ℂ,der∗×Ω¯(Y2)ℂ,der∗\textstyle{\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y_{1})\times\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}ℱ​𝒪​𝒫γ1Y1×ℱ​𝒪​𝒫γ2Y2\scriptstyle{\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{1}}^{Y_{1}}\times\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{2}}^{Y_{2}}}H∗−nγ1​(Y1,ℤ)×H∗−nγ2​(Y2,ℤ)\textstyle{H_{*-n_{\gamma_{1}}}(Y_{1};{\mathbb{Z}})\times H_{*-n_{\gamma_{2}}}(Y_{2};{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Ω¯(Y1×Y2)ℂ,der∗\textstyle{{\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{\mathbb{C},{\rm der}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}ℱ​𝒪​𝒫γ1×γ2Y1×Y2\scriptstyle{\mathcal{FOP}^{Y_{1}\times Y_{2}}_{\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}}}H∗−nγ1−nγ2​(Y1×Y2,ℤ).\textstyle{H_{*-n_{\gamma_{1}}-n_{\gamma_{2}}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2};{\mathbb{Z}}).}
(2.2)
Here the left vertical arrow is the map induced by product of derived orbifold charts:
(𝒰1,ℰ1,𝒮1,f1)×(𝒰2,ℰ2,𝒮2,f2)↦(𝒰1×𝒰2,ℰ1⊞ℰ2,𝒮1⊞𝒮2,f1×f2)({\mathcal{U}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{1},{\mathcal{S}}_{1},f_{1})\times({\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{2},f_{2})\mapsto({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2},f_{1}\times f_{2})
and the right vertical arrow is the Eilenberg–Zilber map.
Proof.
Let (𝒰i,ℰi,𝒮i,fi)({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i},f_{i}), i=1,2i=1,2 be a representative of an element of Ω¯(Yi)ℂ,der∗\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y_{i}). By the definition of ℱ​𝒪​𝒫γiYi\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{i}}^{Y_{i}}, we choose straightenings on (𝒰i,ℰi)({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i}) and choose strongly transverse FOP sections 𝒮i′:𝒰i→ℰi{\mathcal{S}}_{i}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{i} which is C0C^{0}-close to 𝒮i{\mathcal{S}}_{i}. Then 𝒮i′{\mathcal{S}}_{i}^{\prime} defines a pseudocycle in 𝒰i{\mathcal{U}}_{i} which is contained in the closure of the stratum 𝒰i,γi⊂𝒰i{\mathcal{U}}_{i,\gamma_{i}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{i}, i.e., the set of points xi∈𝒰ix_{i}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i} whose stabilizers are isomorphic to GiG_{i} and for a bundle chart (Gi,Ei,Ui)(G_{i},E_{i},U_{i}) centered at xix_{i}, the stable isotropy type defined by (Gi,(N​UGi)xi,(Eˇi)xi)(G_{i},(NU^{G_{i}})_{x_{i}},(\check{E}_{i})_{x_{i}}) lies in the class γ\gamma. Then
ℱ​𝒪​𝒫γiYi​([𝒰i,ℰi,𝒮i,fi])=(fi)∗​[(𝒮i′)−1​(0)∩𝒰i,γi¯].\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{i}}^{Y_{i}}([{\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i},f_{i}])=(f_{i})_{*}[\overline{({\mathcal{S}}_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap\mathcal{U}_{i,\gamma_{i}}}].
Now consider the product chart (𝒰1×𝒰2,ℰ1⊞ℰ2,𝒮1⊞𝒮2,f1×f2)({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2},f_{1}\times f_{2}). The chosen straightenings produce a straightening on the product, with respect to which the product 𝒮1′⊞𝒮2′{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime} is an FOP section. Corollary 2.14 implies that 𝒮1′⊞𝒮2′{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime} is also a strongly transverse FOP section and (𝒮1′⊞𝒮2′)−1​(0)=(𝒮1′)−1​(0)×(𝒮2′)−1​(0)({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)=({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\times({\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0). Restricting the product 𝒰1,γ1×𝒰2,γ2{\mathcal{U}}_{1,\gamma_{1}}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2,\gamma_{2}}, one has, as sets
(𝒮1′⊞𝒮2′)−1​(0)∩(𝒰1×𝒰2)γ1×γ2=((𝒮1′)−1​(0)∩𝒰1,γ1)×((𝒮2′)−1​(0)∩𝒰2,γ2).({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2})_{\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}}=\big(({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}_{1,\gamma_{1}}\big)\times\big(({\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}_{2,\gamma_{2}}\big).
As the product of pseudocycles is still a pseudocycle and the homology classes represented by pseudocycles respect such product structures, therefore the commutativity of the diagram (2.2) follows. ∎
2.3. Proof of Proposition 2.13
First we consider the canonical Whitney stratification on Zd1G1×Zd2G2Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}. Abbreviate
𝒴i=𝑽i×PolydiGi​(𝑽i,𝑾i).{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}={\bm{V}}_{i}\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{i}}^{G_{i}}({\bm{V}}_{i},{\bm{W}}_{i}).
𝒴i{\mathcal{Y}}_{i} is endowed with the action prestratification, i.e.,
𝒴i=⨆Hi∈𝔄i𝑽i,Hi∗{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}=\bigsqcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}}{\bm{V}}_{i,H_{i}}^{*}
We explain the notations. For each subgroup Hi⊂GiH_{i}\subset G_{i}, 𝑽i,Hi∗⊂𝑽i{\bm{V}}_{i,H_{i}}^{*}\subset{\bm{V}}_{i} is the set of points whose stabilizers are exactly HiH_{i}. The symbol 𝔄i{\mathfrak{A}}_{i} denotes the set of all subgroups of GiG_{i} for which 𝑽i,Hi∗≠∅{\bm{V}}_{i,H_{i}}^{*}\neq\emptyset. Then on the product 𝒴:=𝒴1×𝒴2{\mathcal{Y}}:={\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2} which has the G1×G2G_{1}\times G_{2}-action, the strata of the action prestratification is indexed exactly by 𝔄1×𝔄2{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\times{\mathfrak{A}}_{2} and
𝒴=⨆(H1,H2)∈𝔄1×𝔄2𝑽1,H1∗×𝑽2,H2∗.{\mathcal{Y}}=\bigsqcup_{(H_{1},H_{2})\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\times{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}}{\bm{V}}_{1,H_{1}}^{*}\times{\bm{V}}_{2,H_{2}}^{*}.
The strata of this prestratification are all algebraic submanifolds. Hence by [BX22, Theorem A.21], the variety Zd1G1×Zd2G2Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}} has a canonical Whitney stratification, which is the minimal Whitney stratification respecting the action prestratification; by Proposition A.6 of Appendix A, this Whitney stratification is the product of the canonical Whitney stratifications on Zd1G1Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}} and Zd2G2Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}.
Now we prove Proposition 2.13. As the canonical Whitney stratification on ZdGZ_{d}^{G} respects the inclusion ZdG↪Zd′GZ_{d}^{G}\hookrightarrow Z_{d^{\prime}}^{G} for d≤d′d\leq d^{\prime} (see [BX22, Theorem 4.12]), one may assume that d1=d2=dd_{1}=d_{2}=d. The inclusion map sends ZdG1×ZdG2Z_{d}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d}^{G_{2}} into ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}.
The proof of the following lemma is analogous to that of [BX22, Lemma 4.14].
Lemma 2.16.
There exists a map
ψ:(𝑽1⊕𝑽2)×PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2)→𝒴1×𝒴2\psi:({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
satisfying the following conditions.
(1)
ψ∘ϕ\psi\circ\phi is the identity map on 𝒴1×𝒴2{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}.
(2)
For each (v,P)∈(𝑽1⊕𝑽2)×PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2)(v,P)\in({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}), ev⁡(ψ⁡(v,P))=ev⁡(v,P){\rm ev}(\psi(v,P))={\rm ev}(v,P).
(3)
ϕ∘ψ\phi\circ\psi is transverse to ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} and pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification to itself.
Proof.
For each P∈PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2)P\in{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}), denote its 𝑾1{\bm{W}}_{1}-component by P1P_{1} and its 𝑾2{\bm{W}}_{2}-component by P2P_{2}. Then we can regard P1P_{1} as a G2G_{2}-invariant polynomial map
P1∈PolydG2​(𝑽2,PolydG1​(𝑽1,𝑾1))P_{1}\in{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1}))
and regard P2P_{2} as a G1G_{1}-invariant polynomial map
P2∈PolydG1​(𝑽1,PolydG2​(𝑽2,𝑾2)).P_{2}\in{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2})).
Then for v=(v1,v2)∈𝑽1⊕𝑽2v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2}, define
ψ⁡(v,P)=ψ⁡(v1,v2,P1,P2)=((v1,P1​(⋅,v2)),(v2,P2​(v1,⋅)))∈𝒴1×𝒴2.\psi(v,P)=\psi(v_{1},v_{2},P_{1},P_{2})=\Big((v_{1},P_{1}(\cdot,v_{2})),(v_{2},P_{2}(v_{1},\cdot))\Big)\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}.
Then it is easy to verify that ψ∘ϕ=Id\psi\circ\phi={\rm Id} and that
ev⁡(ψ⁡(v,P))=ev⁡(v,P).{\rm ev}(\psi(v,P))={\rm ev}(v,P).
(2.3)
Now we prove the last property. Consider the manifold B=PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2)B={\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}) over which there are the trivial bundles V=B×(𝑽1⊕𝑽2)V=B\times({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2}) and W=B×(𝑾1⊕𝑾2)W=B\times({\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}). Then there are two bundle maps
f¯1,f¯2:V→PolydG1×G2​(V,W)≅B×PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2)\underline{\smash{f}}_{1},\underline{\smash{f}}_{2}:V\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V,W)\cong B\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})
where (here the first variable is the fiber coordinate and the second variable is the base coordinate)
f¯1​(v,P)=(P,P),\displaystyle\ \underline{\smash{f}}_{1}(v,P)=(P,P),\
f¯2​(v,P)=(ϕ¯​(ψ⁡(v,P)),P)\displaystyle\ \underline{\smash{f}}_{2}(v,P)=(\underline{\smash{\phi}}(\psi(v,P)),P)
where ϕ¯​(v,P)=P\underline{\smash{\phi}}(v,P)=P. Then (2.3) implies that
graph⁡(f¯1−f¯2)∈𝒵dG1×G2​(V,W).{\rm graph}(\underline{\smash{f}}_{1}-\underline{\smash{f}}_{2})\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V,W).
As the identity map of (𝑽1⊕𝑽2)×PolydG1×G2​(𝑽1⊕𝑽2,𝑾1⊕𝑾2)({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}) is transverse to ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}, which means that the graph of f¯1\underline{\smash{f}}_{1} is transverse to 𝒵dG1×G2{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}, by a lemma of B. Parker (see [Par13, Lemma 4.10], also [BX22, Lemma 4.7]), the graph of f¯2\underline{\smash{f}}_{2} is also transverse to 𝒵dG1×G2{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}, implying that ϕ∘ψ\phi\circ\psi is transverse to ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}. Moreover, as the identity map pulls back any Whitney stratification to itself, by [BX22, Lemma 4.8], the map ϕ∘ψ\phi\circ\psi also pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification on ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} to itself.
∎
Proof of Proposition 2.13.
As ϕ∘ψ\phi\circ\psi is transverse to ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}, it follows that ϕ\phi is transverse to ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} along the image of ψ\psi. As ψ\psi is surjective, it follows that ϕ\phi is transverse to ZdG1×G2Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}} everywhere. Moreover, as ψ∘ϕ\psi\circ\phi is the identity, it follows that ψ\psi is transverse to ZdG1×ZdG2Z_{d}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d}^{G_{2}} along the image of ϕ\phi. Moreover, as ψ=ψ∘ϕ∘ψ\psi=\psi\circ\phi\circ\psi, which implies that the image of d​ψd\psi at any point is equal to the image of d​ψd\psi at some point in Im⁡(ϕ){\rm Im}(\phi). Hence ψ\psi is transverse to ZdG1×ZdG2Z_{d}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d}^{G_{2}} everywhere.
The proof of the claim that ϕ\phi resp. ψ\psi pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification to the canonical one is similar to the proof of [BX22, Theorem 4.12], which relies crucially on a property of minimal Whitney stratifications (see [BX22, Lemma A.11]).
∎
3. Abstract constructions of chain complexes and maps over the integers
In this section we provide an abstract recipe of constructing chain complexes associated to flow categories and chain maps associated to flow bimodules. We explain the list of necessary structures on flow categories and flow bimodules which allow one to use FOP perturbations to define the algebraic counts over the integers. This section also serves as a source of notations. In Subsection 3.1 we set up the notations for partially ordered sets and abstract stratified spaces. In Subsection 3.2 we introduce the abstract notion of topological flow categories and flow bimodules. In Subsection 3.4 we define the notion of derived orbifold lifts of flow categoreis and bimodules which are abstract frameworks for regularizing the moduli spaces. In Subsection 3.5 we lift certain auxiliary structures which will be necessary to carry out the FOP perturbation scheme. In Subsection 3.6 we consider the important notion of stable normal complex structures. In Subsection 3.7 and Subsection 3.8 we explain the recipe of inductively constructing FOP perturbations associated to derived orbifold lifts of flow categories and flow bimodules and the recipe of extracting chain complexes and chain maps from the countings.
3.1. Stratified spaces
3.1.1. Partially ordered sets
Many objects in Floer theory are indexed by certain partially ordered sets. We abbreviate the phrase “partially ordered set” by the word poset. In this paper posets are always countable. We use different symbols such as ≤\leq, ⪯\preceq, etc. to denote the partial order relations. For a poset 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}, let 𝔸max⊆𝔸{\mathbb{A}}^{\max}\subseteq{\mathbb{A}} be the subset of maximal elements. A poset 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} has a canonical Alexandrov topology: a subset U⊂𝔸U\subset{\mathbb{A}} is open if α∈U\alpha\in U and α≤β\alpha\leq\beta imply that β∈U\beta\in U.
The product of finitely many posets carries a canonically induced partial order. Indeed, if 𝔸1,…,𝔸k{\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{k} are posets, then the relation
(α1,…,αk)≤(β1,…,βk)​if and only if​αi≤βi​∀i=1,…,k(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\leq(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})\ \text{if and only if}\ \alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}\ \forall i=1,\ldots,k
is a partial order on 𝔸1×⋯×𝔸k{\mathbb{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{A}}_{k}.
We often consider posets with a well-defined “depth” function. We introduce the following notion of homogeneous posets. In fact all moduli spaces considered in this paper are stratified by the following kind of posets.
Definition 3.1.
A poset 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} is called homogeneous if for each α∈𝔸\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}, the length of a maximal sequence of elements α=α0<α1<⋯<αk\alpha=\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{k} such that αk∈𝔸max\alpha_{k}\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\max} is finite and only depends on α\alpha. This length is called the depth or codimension of α\alpha, denoted by depth⁡(α){\rm depth}(\alpha). In particular,
α∈𝔸max⟺depth⁡(α)=0.\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\max}\Longleftrightarrow{\rm depth}(\alpha)=0.
A poset map between homogeneous posets is a called a homogeneous poset map if it preserves the depth.
Definition 3.2.
Given a homogeneous poset 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}, the set of adjacent faces of α∈𝔸\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}} is defined to be
𝔽α:={β∈𝔸|α≤β​ and ​depth​(β)=1}.{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}:=\{\beta\in{\mathbb{A}}|\alpha\leq\beta\text{ and }{\rm depth}(\beta)=1\}.
It is straightforward to check that homogeneous posets also admit finite products with depth function being
depth⁡(α1,…,αk)=depth⁡(α1)+⋯+depth⁡(αk).{\rm depth}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})={\rm depth}(\alpha_{1})+\cdots+{\rm depth}(\alpha_{k}).
We introudce the following “boundary stratum” notation. Given a poset 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} and an element α∈𝔸\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}, denote
∂α𝔸:={α′∈𝔸|α′≤α}.\partial^{\alpha}{\mathbb{A}}:=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{A}}\ |\ \alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha\}.
More generally, if 𝔹⊂𝔸{\mathbb{B}}\subset{\mathbb{A}} is a subset, denote
∂𝔹𝔸:=⋃β∈𝔹∂β𝔸.\partial^{\mathbb{B}}{\mathbb{A}}:=\bigcup_{\beta\in{\mathbb{B}}}\partial^{\beta}{\mathbb{A}}.
It has the induced partial order. If 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} is a homogeneous poset, then ∂α𝔸\partial^{\alpha}{\mathbb{A}} is also homogeneous with depth function being shifted by depth⁡(α){\rm depth}(\alpha). Moreover, for each nonnegative integer kk, denote
∂[k]𝔸:=∂depth−1​(k)𝔸\partial^{[k]}{\mathbb{A}}:=\partial^{{\rm depth}^{-1}(k)}{\mathbb{A}}
which is still homogeneous with the depth function shifted by kk.
3.1.2. Stratified spaces
We introduce our notion of stratified topological spaces. We emphasize here that stratified spaces always refer to a poset. Moreover, the notion of stratified spaces should not be confused with the notion of prestratified spaces discussed in Appendix A.
Definition 3.3.
Let 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} be a (countable) poset. An 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-stratified space (𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-space for short) is a locally compact, Hausdorff and second countable topological space XX endowed with a continuous map
s:X→𝔸s:X\to{\mathbb{A}}
with respect to the Alexandrov topology on 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} such that the range of ss is finite. In particular, we can write
X=⨆α∈𝔸Xα,where​Xα:=s−1​(α)X=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}}X_{\alpha},\ {\rm where}\ X_{\alpha}:=s^{-1}(\alpha)
satisfying the following conditions.
(1)
Each XαX_{\alpha} (called a stratum) is locally closed (which can be empty44
4
For example, in a moduli space of stable Floer cylinders, the subset of smooth Floer cylinders, which should be the top stratum, could be empty.).
(2)
All but finitely many strata are empty.
(3)
For each α∈𝔸\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}, the subset
∂αX:=⨆β≤αXβ\partial^{\alpha}X:=\bigsqcup_{\beta\leq\alpha}X_{\beta}
is a closed set (which may contain the closure of XαX_{\alpha} properly). Note that this condition follows from the continuity of ss.
We introduce the following notions for stratified spaces.
Definition 3.4.
(1)
A map from an 𝔸1{\mathbb{A}}_{1}-space X1X_{1} to an 𝔸2{\mathbb{A}}_{2}-space X2X_{2} is a commutative diagram
X1\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}f\scriptstyle{f}s1\scriptstyle{s_{1}}X2\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}s2\scriptstyle{s_{2}}𝔸1\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}i\scriptstyle{i}𝔸2\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{2}}
where i:𝔸1→𝔸2i:{\mathbb{A}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{2} is a poset map and ff is a continuous map. If 𝔸1{\mathbb{A}}_{1} and 𝔸2{\mathbb{A}}_{2} are both homogeneous (see Definition 3.1), then we require that ii is a homogeneous map. We usually call such a map a stratified map to emphasize that it respects the stratifications.
(2)
A stratified map f:X1→X2f:X_{1}\to X_{2} as above is called an embedding if ff is a homeomorphism onto its image and i:𝔸1→𝔸2i:{\mathbb{A}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{A}}_{2} is an injection. An open embedding is an embedding with an open image.
(3)
A stratified map f:X1→X2f:X_{1}\to X_{2} is called a homeomorphism if ff is a homeomorphism of topological spaces and the underlying poset map is an isomorphism.
(4)
The product of 𝔸i{\mathbb{A}}_{i}-spaces XiX_{i} (i=1,…,ki=1,\ldots,k) is the product topological space X1×⋯×XkX_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{k} stratified by the product poset 𝔸1×⋯×𝔸k{\mathbb{A}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{A}}_{k}. It is easy to see
∂(α1,…,αk)(X1×⋯×Xk)=∂α1X1×⋯×∂αkXk.\partial^{(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})}(X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{k})=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}X_{1}\times\cdots\times\partial^{\alpha_{k}}X_{k}.
(5)
Let GG be a topological group. A GG-action on an 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-space XX is a continuous GG-action on XX which preserves each stratum. In this case, the ∂α𝔸\partial^{\alpha}{\mathbb{A}}-space ∂αX\partial^{\alpha}X has an induced GG-action.
3.2. Topological flow categories and bimodules
The concept of flow categories was introduced by Cohen–Jones–Segal [CJS95]. We need a variant of the original construction similar to [Par16, Section 7] and [Abo22, Section 7,8].
Setup 3.5.
Let NN be a nonnegative integer, Π\Pi be an infinite cyclic group, and ω:Π→ℤ\omega:\Pi\to{\mathbb{Z}} be a group injection.
Let 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} be a countable poset equipped with the following extra data: a free Π\Pi-action and two functions (called the action and the index)
𝒜𝒫:𝒫→ℝ,\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{P}}\to{\mathbb{R}},\
ind𝒫:𝒫→ℤ/2​N.\displaystyle\ {\rm ind}^{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{P}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2N.
Assume the following conditions.
(1)
The Π\Pi-action is order-preserving. Namely, for all p,q∈𝒫p,q\in{\mathcal{P}} and a∈Πa\in\Pi
p≤q⟺a⋅p≤a⋅q.p\leq q\Longleftrightarrow a\cdot p\leq a\cdot q.
(2)
For all p∈𝒫p\in{\mathcal{P}} and a∈Πa\in\Pi,
𝒜𝒫​(a⋅p)=𝒜𝒫​(p)+ω⁡(a){\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}(a\cdot p)={\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)+\omega(a)
(3.1)
and
ind𝒫​(a⋅p)=ind𝒫​(p).{\rm ind}^{\mathcal{P}}(a\cdot p)=\rm{ind}^{\mathcal{P}}(p).
(3.2)
(3)
For all p,q∈𝒫p,q\in{\mathcal{P}},
p<q⟹𝒜𝒫​(p)<𝒜𝒫​(q).p<q\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)<{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}(q).
(4)
The quotient set 𝒫¯:=𝒫/Π\underline{\smash{\mathcal{P}}}:={\mathcal{P}}/\Pi is finite.
The conditions that ω:Π→ℤ\omega:\Pi\to{\mathbb{Z}} is injective and that 𝒫¯\underline{\smash{\mathcal{P}}} is finite imply that 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} is “locally finite-dimensional,” namely, for any pair of elements p<qp<q of 𝒫{\mathcal{P}}, there are at most finitely many elements lying between them.
Notation 3.6.
Given a pair of elements p<qp<q in 𝒫{\mathcal{P}}, we define a poset
𝔸p​q𝒫:={α=pr1⋯rlq|p<r1<⋯<rl<q,r1,…,rl∈𝒫}{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}:=\big\{\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\ |\ p<r_{1}<\cdots<r_{l}<q,\ r_{1},\ldots,r_{l}\in{\mathcal{P}}\big\}
whose partial order is induced by inclusion
ps1⋯smq≤pr1⋯rlq⟺{r1⋯rl}⊆{s1,…,sm}.ps_{1}\cdots s_{m}q\leq pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\Longleftrightarrow\{r_{1}\cdots r_{l}\}\subseteq\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{m}\}.
The poset 𝔸p​q𝒫{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}} has a unique maximal element p​qpq and is homogeneous whose depth function is
depth(pr1⋯rlq)=l.{\rm depth}(pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q)=l.
The following is a formal characterization of the feature that boundary strata of moduli spaces consist of broken trajectories. Namely, given a triple p​r​q∈𝔸p​q𝒫prq\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}, it is easy to see that there is an isomorphism of homogeneous posets
𝔸p​r𝒫×𝔸r​q𝒫≅∂p​r​q𝔸p​q𝒫{\mathbb{A}}_{pr}^{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{rq}^{\mathcal{P}}\cong\partial^{prq}{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}
(3.3)
under the concatenation of strings.
Now we introduce the notion of flow categories under the setting of Setup 3.5.
Definition 3.7.
Let 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} be as in Setup 3.5. A flow category T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} over 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} is a topologically enriched category55
5
Namely, the set of morphisms are topological spaces and composition maps are continuous. with the set of objects given by 𝒫{\mathcal{P}}, with morphism spaces Tp​qT_{pq} satisfying the following conditions.
(1)
Tp​q≠∅T_{pq}\neq\emptyset only if p≤qp\leq q in 𝒫{\mathcal{P}}.66
6
In the Morse or Floer case, it is indeed true that Tp​q≠∅T_{pq}\neq\emptyset if and only if p≤qp\leq q.
(2)
Tp​pT_{pp} is the singleton.
(3)
Tp​qT_{pq} is a compact 𝔸p​q𝒫{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}-space.
(4)
Given a triple p<r<qp<r<q in 𝒫{\mathcal{P}}, the composition map factors through a stratified homeomorphism
Tp​r×Tr​q\textstyle{T_{pr}\times T_{rq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}∂p​r​qTp​q\textstyle{\partial^{prq}T_{pq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​r𝒫×𝔸r​q𝒫\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pr}^{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{rq}^{\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}∂p​r​q𝔸p​q𝒫\textstyle{\partial^{prq}{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}}
where the underlying poset isomorphism is the map (3.3). We require that whenever p<r<s<qp<r<s<q, the following associativity diagram holds:
Tp​r×Tr​s×Ts​q{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{pr}\times T_{rs}\times T_{sq}}Tp​s×Ts​q{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{ps}\times T_{sq}}Tp​r×Tr​q{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{pr}\times T_{rq}}Tp​q.{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{pq}.}
(5)
Π\Pi defines a strict action on T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}}: for any a∈Πa\in\Pi and p,q∈𝒫p,q\in{\mathcal{P}}, there is a stratified homeomorphism
Tp​q\textstyle{T_{pq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}ϕa\scriptstyle{\phi_{a}}Ta⋅p​a⋅q\textstyle{T_{a\cdot p\ a\cdot q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​q𝒫\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸a⋅p​a⋅q𝒫\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{a\cdot p\ a\cdot q}^{\mathcal{P}}}
where the underlying poset map is the natural isomorphism. Moreover, when a1,a2∈Πa_{1},a_{2}\in\Pi, we require that the equation ϕa1⋅a2=ϕa1∘ϕa2\phi_{a_{1}\cdot a_{2}}=\phi_{a_{1}}\circ\phi_{a_{2}} holds and ϕ0\phi_{0} is the identity map for a=0∈Πa=0\in\Pi.
Lemma 3.8.
Given α=pr1⋯rlq∈𝔸𝒫p​q\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\in\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pq}, the space ∂αTp​q\partial^{\alpha}T_{pq} is homeomorphic to Tp​r1×⋯×Trl​qT_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times T_{r_{l}q} as 𝔸𝒫p​r1×⋯×𝔸𝒫rl​q\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{r_{l}q}-spaces.
Proof.
We prove the statement by induction on depth⁡(α){\rm depth}(\alpha). For depth⁡(α)=0{\rm depth}(\alpha)=0, this is tautology, and for depth⁡(α)=1{\rm depth}(\alpha)=1, the assertion follows from Definition 3.7. Suppose the lemma holds for all depth⁡(α)≤l−1{\rm depth}(\alpha)\leq l-1. Now suppose α=pr1⋯rlq\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q. Consider the homeomorphism between 𝔸p​r1𝒫×𝔸r1​q𝒫\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pr_{1}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{r_{1}q} spaces Tp​r1×Tr1​q→∂p​r1​qTp​qT_{pr_{1}}\times T_{r_{1}q}\to\partial^{pr_{1}q}T_{pq}. Restricting the homeomorphism along the closed stratum 𝔸𝒫p​r1×∂r1⋯rlq𝔸𝒫r1​q\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pr_{1}}\times\partial^{r_{1}\cdots r_{l}q}\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{r_{1}q} and using the induction hypothesis, we obtain a homeomorphism of 𝔸𝒫p​r1×⋯×𝔸𝒫rl​q\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{A}^{\mathcal{P}}_{r_{l}q}-spaces
Tp​r1×⋯×Trl​q→∂αTp​q.T_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times T_{r_{l}q}\to\partial^{\alpha}T_{pq}.
By associativity, if we construct such a homeomorphism by decomposing α\alpha as pr1⋯rkpr_{1}\cdots r_{k} and rk⋯rlqr_{k}\cdots r_{l}q for some 1≤k≤l1\leq k\leq l, the resulting homeomorphism between the stratified spaces is the same.
∎
Definition 3.7 does not impose any regularity conditions or tangential structures on the morphism spaces. The purpose of such a definition is to single out the stratification structures and we will introduce several enhancements by putting various structures on the morphism spaces in the sequel.
3.2.1. Flow bimodules
We first introduce the posets indexing strata in flow bimodules.
Notation 3.9.
Suppose 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} and 𝒫′{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime} are two posets as in Setup 3.5 equipped with own action and index functions
(𝒜𝒫,ind𝒫):𝒫→ℝ×(ℤ/2​N),\displaystyle\ ({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}},{\rm ind}^{\mathcal{P}}):{\mathcal{P}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times({\mathbb{Z}}/2N),\
(𝒜𝒫′,ind𝒫′):𝒫′→ℝ×(ℤ/2​N).\displaystyle\ ({\mathcal{A}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}},{\rm ind}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}):{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times({\mathbb{Z}}/2N).
For p∈𝒫p\in{\mathcal{P}} and p′∈𝒫′p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}, define a poset
𝔸p​p′:={α=pq1⋯qkqk′′⋯q1′p′|p<q1<⋯<qk,qk′′<⋯<q1′<p′}{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}}:=\big\{\alpha=pq_{1}\cdots q_{k}q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\cdots q^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}\ |\ p<q_{1}<\cdots<q_{k},\ q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}<\cdots<q^{\prime}_{1}<p^{\prime}\big\}
The partial order is again induced by inclusion:
pq1⋯qkq′k′⋯q′1p′≤pq~1⋯q~k~q~′k~′⋯q~′1p′⟺{q~1,…,q~k~}⊆{q1,…,qk}and{q~′1⋯q~′k~′}⊆{q′1⋯q′k′}.pq_{1}\cdots q_{k}q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\cdots q^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}\leq p\tilde{q}_{1}\cdots\tilde{q}_{\tilde{k}}\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{\tilde{k}^{\prime}}\cdots\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}\\
\Longleftrightarrow\{\tilde{q}_{1},\dots,\tilde{q}_{\tilde{k}}\}\subseteq\{q_{1},\dots,q_{k}\}\ {\rm and}\ \{\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{\tilde{k}^{\prime}}\}\subseteq\{q^{\prime}_{1}\cdots q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\}.
𝔸p​p′{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}} is a homogeneous poset with a unique maximal element p​p′pp^{\prime} and depth function
depth(pq1⋯qkqk′′⋯q1′p′)=k+k′.{\rm depth}(pq_{1}\cdots q_{k}q_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}\cdots q_{1}^{\prime}p^{\prime})=k+k^{\prime}.
There are similar characterizations of “broken configurations.” If p<qp<q are elements in 𝒫{\mathcal{P}}, it is easy to see that there is a natural isomorphism of homogeneous posets
𝔸p​q𝒫×𝔸q​p′≅∂p​q​p′𝔸p​p′{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{qp^{\prime}}\cong\partial^{pqp^{\prime}}{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}}
by concatenation of words. Such an isomorphism makes the following diagram commute:
𝔸p​q1𝒫×𝔸q1​q2𝒫×𝔸q2​p′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pq_{1}}^{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathbb{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}_{q_{1}q_{2}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{q_{2}p^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​q2𝒫×𝔸q2​p′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pq_{2}}^{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{q_{2}p^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​q1𝒫×𝔸q1​p′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pq_{1}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{q_{1}p^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​p′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}}}
Similarly, if q′<p′q^{\prime}<p^{\prime} in 𝒫′{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}, one has
𝔸p​q′×𝔸q′​p′𝒫′≅∂p​q′​p′𝔸p​p′,{\mathbb{A}}_{pq^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}\cong\partial^{pq^{\prime}p^{\prime}}{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}},
which satisfies a similar commutative relation as above, and in this case the poset 𝔸𝒫′{\mathbb{A}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}} acts on the right. Moreover, these two types of isomorphisms are compatible in the following sense. Namely, the following diagram is commutative for which the arrows are induced by the obvious concatenation of words.
𝔸p​q𝒫×𝔸q​q′×𝔸q′​p′𝒫′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{qq^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​q𝒫×𝔸q​p′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{qp^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​q′×𝔸q′​p′𝒫′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pq^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝔸p​p′\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}}}
Definition 3.10.
Let T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} and T𝒫′T^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}} be flow categories over 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} and 𝒫′{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime} respectively. A flow bimodule MM from T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} to T𝒫′T^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}} consists of the following data.
(1)
A compact 𝔸p​p′{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}}-space Mp​p′M_{pp^{\prime}} (which could be empty) for all p∈𝒫p\in{\mathcal{P}} and p′∈𝒫′p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}.
(2)
For p<qp<q, a homeomorphism of stratified spaces
Tp​q𝒫×Mq​p′ ∂p​q​p′Mp​p′ 𝔸p​q𝒫×𝔸q​p′ ≅ ∂p​q​p′𝔸p​p′ .\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.90326pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.90326pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise M_{qp^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern As spelled out in [Abo22, Section 8], one can define “higher” homotopies between flow bimodules parametrized cubes [0,1]n[0,1]^{n} for any n∈ℤ>0n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0} and construct a cubically enriched category of flow bimodules. Such notions are useful for proving invariance of Floer homology/homotopy groups, but establishing such invariance is beyond the scope of this paper.
3.3. Outer-collaring
In Floer theory one often needs to inductively construct structures (such as perturbations) on infinitely many moduli spaces. Each moduli space is an orbifold with corners in a suitable sense. One wishes to construct the structures such that near the boundary or corner they are of the product type. This requires various compatible collar structures near the boundary and corners. The existence of a compatible system of collar structures is difficult to construct directly. Instead, following [FOOO20, Chapter 17], we take a short-cut by constructing the collars “outside.”
We will first consider the outer-collaring construction for individual stratified spaces, flow categories, and bimodules. They are operated in the topological category. This discussion is not immediately used in this section. Later we will also discuss outer-collaring of stratified objects in the smooth category.
3.3.1. Outer-collaring of stratified spaces
We first discuss the outer-collaring of a single moduli space which may appear in either a flow category or a bimodule. We consider the first case (flow category) and the second case is completely similar. Abbreviate 𝔸p​q𝒫{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}} by 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} where 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} satisfies conditions of Setup 3.5 and p,q∈𝒫p,q\in{\mathcal{P}}. Let XX be an 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-space whose strata are indexed by words α=pr1⋯rlq\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q. Choose r≥0r\geq 0 and we will define a new 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-space denoted by X⊞rX^{\boxplus r}. As a set,
X⊞r=(⨆α∈𝔸∂αX×[−r,0]𝔽α)/∼,X^{\boxplus r}=\left(\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}}\partial^{\alpha}X\times[-r,0]^{{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}}\right)/\sim,
where 𝔽α⊂𝔸{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathbb{A}} is the set of adjacent faces and where the equivalence relation ∼\sim is generated by the following relation: if α≤β\alpha\leq\beta (which implies the inclusion of the sets of adjacent faces 𝔽β⊂𝔽α{\mathbb{F}}_{\beta}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}), we identify
(x,(ti)i∈𝔽β)∈∂βX×[−r,0]𝔽β(x,(t_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{F}}_{\beta}})\in\partial^{\beta}X\times[-r,0]^{{\mathbb{F}}_{\beta}}
with
(y,(sj)j∈𝔽α)∈∂αX×[−r,0]𝔽α(y,(s_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}})\in\partial^{\alpha}X\times[-r,0]^{{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}}
if x=y∈∂αXx=y\in\partial^{\alpha}X, sj=0s_{j}=0 when j∉𝔽βj\notin{\mathbb{F}}_{\beta} and tj=sjt_{j}=s_{j} when j∈𝔽βj\in{\mathbb{F}}_{\beta}. We call X⊞rX^{\boxplus r} the outer collaring of XX of width rr (see Figure 1).
XX∂β2X\partial^{\beta_{2}}X∂β1X\partial^{\beta_{1}}X∂αX×[−r,0]2\partial^{\alpha}X\times[-r,0]^{2}∂β1X×[−r,0]\partial^{\beta_{1}}X\times[-r,0]∂β2X×[−r,0]\partial^{\beta_{2}}X\times[-r,0]
Figure 1. The local picture of an outer collaring of a prestratified space for α<β1,β2\alpha<\beta_{1},\beta_{2}.
One has a natural identification X=X⊞0X=X^{\boxplus 0} and the natural inclusion X⊞r↪X⊞r′X^{\boxplus r}\hookrightarrow X^{\boxplus r^{\prime}} when r≤r′r\leq r^{\prime}. Moreover, X⊞rX^{\boxplus r} has a structure of an 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-space as follows. For each β∈𝔸\beta\in{\mathbb{A}}, define the boundary stratum
∂βX⊞r=⋃α≤β(∂αX×{(tj)j∈𝔽α|tj=−r∀j∈𝔽β}).\partial^{\beta}X^{\boxplus r}=\bigcup_{\alpha\leq\beta}\Big(\partial^{\alpha}X\times\{(t_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}}\ |\ t_{j}=-r\ \forall j\in{\mathbb{F}}_{\beta}\}\Big).
Then the corresponding stratum for β∈𝔸\beta\in{\mathbb{A}} is given by
Xβ⊞r=∂βX⊞r∖⋃α<β∂αX⊞r.X_{\beta}^{\boxplus r}=\partial^{\beta}X^{\boxplus r}\setminus\bigcup_{\alpha<\beta}\partial^{\alpha}X^{\boxplus r}.
It is easy to see that the induced map X⊞r→𝔸X^{\boxplus r}\to{\mathbb{A}} is a stratification.
The following lemma shows that the outer-collaring construction respects products.
Lemma 3.12.
(1)
For any α∈𝔸\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}, we have (∂αX)⊞r=∂αX⊞r(\partial^{\alpha}X)^{{\boxplus r}}=\partial^{\alpha}X^{\boxplus r} as ∂α𝔸\partial^{\alpha}{\mathbb{A}}-spaces.
(2)
If XiX_{i} are 𝔸i{\mathbb{A}}_{i}-spaces for i=1,2i=1,2, then X1⊞r×X2⊞r=(X1×X2)⊞rX_{1}^{\boxplus r}\times X_{2}^{\boxplus r}=(X_{1}\times X_{2})^{{\boxplus r}} as 𝔸1×𝔸2{\mathbb{A}}_{1}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{2}-spaces.
Proof.
These statements are true by inspecting the definition.
∎
