Here the horizontal arrows are induced from the collar structure and the vertical arrows are restrictions of the open chart embeddings associated to the stabilization and scaffolding.
Finally we spell out the meaning of compatible straightenings.
Definition 3.35.
Consider a system of D-chart presentations equipped with a collar structure and a scaffolding which are compatible. Then a straightening (see Definition 3.31) is compatible with the collar structure and the scaffolding if the following conditions are satisfied.
(1)
For each pair α<β\alpha<\beta, near ∂α𝒰β\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}, using the corner coordinates induced from the map θβ​αcollar\theta_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm collar}, near ∂α𝒰β\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}, one has
gβ=gβ|∂α𝒰β+∑i∈𝔽α∖𝔽βd​ti2.g_{\beta}=g_{\beta}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}}+\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}\setminus{\mathbb{F}}_{\beta}}dt_{i}^{2}.
(3.14)
Moreover, with respect to the bundle isomorphism θ^β​αcollar\widehat{\theta}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm collar}, near ∂α𝒰β\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta} one has
∇ℰβ=(π^β​αcollar)∗​∇ℰβ|∂α𝒰β.\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}}=\left(\widehat{\pi}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm collar}\right)^{*}\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}}.
(2)
For each pair α≤β\alpha\leq\beta, the domain embedding
ιβ​α:𝒰α↪∂α𝒰β\iota_{\beta\alpha}:{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}
is isometric and totally geodesic. The normal bundle of this embedding has an induced metric and metric connection. Via the stabilization map 𝜽β​α{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha} which embeds ℱβ​α{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha} into ℰβ{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}, the bundle ℱβ​α{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha} then carries a metric and metric connection.
(3)
The bundle embedding ℱβ​α↪ℰβ{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta} preserves the connection.
(4)
The stabilization map on the domain is isometric.
(5)
Via the stabilization map (on the bundle), the connection on ℰβ|∂α𝒰β{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}} near the embedding image is the pullback connection, i.e., the direct sum of the pullback connections on ℱβ​α{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha} and ℰα{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}.
3.6. Normal complex structure and orientations on flow categories
In order to carry out the FOP perturbations and define counts over ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}, we need to introduce normal complex structures on the derived orbifold lifts and certain orientation structures.
Definition 3.36.
Let T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} be a flow category over 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} equipped with a derived orbifold lift 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}}, with the collection of derived orbifold presentations
{Cp​q=(𝒰p​q,ℰp​q,𝒮p​q,ψp​q)}p≤q.\big\{C_{pq}=({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq},\psi_{pq})\big\}_{p\leq q}.
Moreover, assume that 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} has a scaffolding
𝔉𝒫=((ℱβ​α,𝜽β​α)α≤β)p<q.{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}=\Big(\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha},{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha}\big)_{\alpha\leq\beta}\Big)_{p<q}.
Then a normal complex structure (or normally complex lift) on the pair 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} and 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}} consists of the following data.
(1)
A normal complex structure on each of the derived orbifold chart (𝒰p​q,ℰp​q,𝒮p​q)({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}).
(2)
A complex structure on each of the orbifold vector bundles ℱβ​α→𝒰α{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}.
(3)
For any pair p,q∈𝒫p,q\in{\mathcal{P}} with Tp​q𝒫≠∅T^{\mathcal{P}}_{pq}\neq\emptyset and any pair of strata α≤β\alpha\leq\beta in 𝔸p​q𝒫{\mathbb{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pq}, the underlying map of the germ equivalence (3.8)
θβ​α:Stabℱβ​α​(𝒰α)≃∂α𝒰β\theta_{\beta\alpha}:{\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}}({\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha})\simeq\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}
respects the normal complex structures.
(4)
Under the same assumptions as the previous item, the map
θ^β​α:πβ​α∗​ℰα⊕πβ​α∗​ℱβ​α→ℰβ|∂αUβ\widehat{\theta}_{\beta\alpha}:\pi^{*}_{\beta\alpha}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\oplus\pi^{*}_{\beta\alpha}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta}|_{\partial^{\alpha}U_{\beta}}
intertwines with the normal complex structures.
(5)
The splitting of orbifold vector bundles (3.9) over 𝒰α{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}, the bundle isomorphism (3.10), and the splitting (3.11) respect the normal complex structures.
(6)
These (normal) complex structures admit a strict Π\Pi-action in the obvious sense.
Definition 3.37.
Let T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}}, 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}}, and 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}} be as in Definition 3.36. Suppose we are given a normal complex structure on the pair 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} and 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}. Then a collar structure compatibile with 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}} (see Definition 3.34) is said to be compatible with the normal complex structure if the collar maps intertwine with the normal complex structures.
Remark 3.38.
Note that the complex structure on ℱβ​α{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha} and the normal complex structure on 𝒰α{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha} induce a normal complex structure on Stabℱβ​α​(𝒰α){\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}}({\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}). Moreover, due to the behavior of orbifold charts near the boundary and corners, ∂α𝒰β\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta} indeed has a normal complex structure.
Remark 3.39.
We could define the notion of normal complex structure in a more intrinsic way which does not rely on the choice of scaffoldings.
Remark 3.40.
In our applications, the notion of normal complex structure is mostly useful for considering the space 𝒰α{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}, as the orbifold vector bundles ℰα{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha} and ℱβ​α{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha} would have fiberwise complex structures in practice.
The normal complex structure is concerned with the tangential structure on the normal directions to strata specified by the isomorphism classes of the isotropy groups on an orbifold. In particular, an unorientable manifold has a (trivial) normal complex structure when being viewed as an orbifold. To define algebraic counts, we need to define orientation structures on the flow categories and flow bimodules, which is an extra structure beyond the normal complex structure.
Definition 3.41.
Let VV be a vector space over ℝ{\mathbb{R}}. The orientation line 𝔬V\mathfrak{o}_{V} of VV is defined to be the free ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-module of rank 11 given by HdimV​(V,V∖{0},ℤ)H_{\dim V}(V,V\setminus\{0\};{\mathbb{Z}}) with ℤ/2{\mathbb{Z}}/2-grading the mod 22 reduction of dimV\dim V. More generally, if (V+,V−)(V^{+},V^{-}) is a virtual vector space, its orientation line 𝔬(V+,V−)\mathfrak{o}_{(V^{+},V^{-})} is defined to be the tensor product of orientation lines 𝔬V+⊗𝔬V−∨\mathfrak{o}_{V^{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{V^{-}}^{\vee}.
Definition 3.42.
An 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-stratified derived orbifold chart C=(𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)C=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is called orientable if both 𝒰{\mathcal{U}} and ℰ{\mathcal{E}} are orientable. Given a connected component of an orientable derived orbifold chart, the orientation line 𝔬C\mathfrak{o}_{C} of (𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is defined to be the orientation line of the virtual vector space
(Tx​𝒰,ℰx)(T_{x}{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}}_{x})
for arbitrary x∈𝒰x\in{\mathcal{U}} lying in the top stratum of 𝒰{\mathcal{U}}.
If C=(𝒰,ℰ,𝒮)C=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}) is an orientable 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-stratified derived orbifold chart, given an index α∈𝔸\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}, the ∂α𝔸\partial^{\alpha}{\mathbb{A}}-stratified derived orbifold chart ∂αC=(∂α𝒰,ℰ|∂α𝒰,𝒮|∂α𝒰)\partial^{\alpha}C=(\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathcal{S}}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}}) is also orientable. Moreover, it is easy to see that there is a natural isomorphism
𝔬∂αC→∼𝔬C⊗(𝔬ℝ∨)⊗depth⁡(α).\mathfrak{o}_{\partial^{\alpha}C}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{C}\otimes(\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee})^{\otimes{\rm depth}(\alpha)}.
Definition 3.43.
Suppose T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}}, 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} and 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}} are the same in Definition 3.36 An orientation of such a normally complex derived orbifold lift is given by:
(1)
A virtual vector space (Vp+,Vp−)(V_{p}^{+},V_{p}^{-}) for each p∈𝒫p\in{\mathcal{P}}. Define 𝔬p:=𝔬(Vp+,Vp−)\mathfrak{o}_{p}:=\mathfrak{o}_{(V_{p}^{+},V_{p}^{-})}.
(2)
An isomorphism of orientation lines
𝔬Cp​q→∼𝔬p∨⊗𝔬q.\mathfrak{o}_{C_{pq}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{p}^{\vee}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{q}^{.}
(3.15)
(3)
For p<r<qp<r<q, an isomorphism of orientation lines
𝔬Cp​r⊗𝔬Cr​q⊗(𝔬ℱp​r​q,p​q⊗𝔬ℱp​r​q,p​q∨)→∼𝔬∂p​r​qCp​q.\mathfrak{o}_{C_{pr}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{C_{rq}}\otimes(\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{F}_{prq,pq}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{F}_{prq,pq}}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{\partial^{prq}C_{pq}}.
(3.16)
(4)
The above isomorphisms are preserved by the Π\Pi-action.
Note that if the above derived orbifold lift is given an orientation, then for any α≤β\alpha\leq\beta in 𝔸p​qFloer{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}, there is an isomorphism
𝔬Cα→∼𝔬∂αCβ.\mathfrak{o}_{C_{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{\partial^{\alpha}C_{\beta}}.
Definition 3.44.
Given a flow category T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} endowed with a derived orbifold lift 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}}, a scaffolding 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}, and a compatible collar structure, if they are further endowed with a normal complex structure and orientation, we call such a datum an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift and abbreviate it as 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}}.
If we have a flow bimodule MM between T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} and T𝒫′T^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}} such that these three objects admit a compatible derived orbifold lift, as well as a compatible scaffolding structure, one can spell out the meaning of a normal complex structure and an orientation on MM similarly to Definition 3.36.
Definition 3.45.
Let T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} (resp. T𝒫′T^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}) be flow categories endowed with a derived orbifold lift 𝔇𝒫={Cp​q𝒫}p≤q{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}}=\{C^{\mathcal{P}}_{pq}\}_{p\leq q} (resp. 𝔇𝒫′={Cp′​q′𝒫′}p′≤q′{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=\{C^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}}\}_{p^{\prime}\leq q^{\prime}}) and a scaffolding
𝔉𝒫=((ℱβ​α𝒫,𝜽β​α)α≤β)p<q​ resp. ​𝔉𝒫′=((ℱβ′​α′𝒫′,𝜽β′​α′)α′≤β′)p′<q′.{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}=\Big(\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}},{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha}\big)_{\alpha\leq\beta}\Big)_{p<q}\text{ resp. }{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}=\Big(\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\big)_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\beta^{\prime}}\Big)_{p^{\prime}<q^{\prime}}.
Moreover, both the pair (𝔇𝒫,𝔉𝒫)({\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}) and the pair (𝔇𝒫′,𝔉𝒫′)({\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}) have a normal complex structure in the sense of Definition 3.36. Suppose MM is a flow bimodule from T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} to T𝒫′T^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}} with a derived orbifold lift extending 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} and 𝔇𝒫′{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}} (Definition 3.25)
𝔇M=({Cp​p′M=(𝒰p​p′M,ℰp​p′M,𝒮p​p′M,ψp​p′M)}p∈𝒫,p′∈𝒫′,𝜾p​p′M){\mathfrak{D}}^{M}=\Big(\big\{C^{M}_{pp^{\prime}}=({\mathcal{U}}^{M}_{pp^{\prime}},{\mathcal{E}}^{M}_{pp^{\prime}},{\mathcal{S}}^{M}_{pp^{\prime}},\psi^{M}_{pp^{\prime}})\big\}_{p\in{\mathcal{P}},p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}},{\bm{\iota}}^{M}_{pp^{\prime}}\Big)
and a scaffolding
𝔉M=({ℱβ~​α~M,𝜽β~​α~M}α~≤β~)α~,β~∈𝔸p​p′M{\mathfrak{F}}^{M}=\big(\big\{{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{\beta}\tilde{\alpha}}^{M},{\bm{\theta}}_{\tilde{\beta}\tilde{\alpha}}^{M}\big\}_{\tilde{\alpha}\leq\tilde{\beta}}\big)_{\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pp^{\prime}}^{M}}
compatible with 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}} and 𝔉𝒫′{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}} (Definition 3.30). Then a normal complex structure (or normally complex lift) on this datum extending or compatible with the given ones on (𝔇𝒫,𝔉𝒫)({\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}) and (𝔇𝒫′,𝔉𝒫′)({\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}) consists of the following data.
(1)
A normal complex structure on each of the derived orbifold chart (𝒰p​p′M,ℰp​p′M,𝒮p​p′M)({\mathcal{U}}^{M}_{pp^{\prime}},{\mathcal{E}}^{M}_{pp^{\prime}},{\mathcal{S}}^{M}_{pp^{\prime}}).
(2)
A complex structure on the each of the orbifold vector bundles ℱβ~​α~M→𝒰αM{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{\beta}\tilde{\alpha}}^{M}\to{\mathcal{U}}^{M}_{\alpha}.
(3)
The germ equivalence θα~,β~M\theta^{M}_{\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}} and the bundle map covering it θ^α~,β~M\widehat{\theta}^{M}_{\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}} all intertwine with the normal complex structures.
(4)
Splittings or isomorphism of the form (3.9), (3.10), and (3.11) all respect the (normal) complex structures.
(5)
The respective (normal) complex structures are identified under the Π\Pi-action.
Definition 3.46.
Under the same setting as in Definition 3.45, suppose we are given a compatible normal complex structure on the pair 𝔇M{\mathfrak{D}}^{M} and 𝔉𝒫{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}. Then a collar structure compatibile with 𝔉M{\mathfrak{F}}^{M} (see Definition 3.34) is said to be compatible with the normal complex structure if the collar maps intertwine with the normal complex structures.
Definition 3.47.
Suppose (T𝒫,𝔇𝒫,𝔉𝒫)(T^{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}), (T𝒫′,𝔇𝒫′,𝔉𝒫′)(T^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}), and (M,𝔇M,𝔉M)(M,{\mathfrak{D}}^{M},{\mathfrak{F}}^{M}) be the same as in Definition 3.45. Moreover, assume that (𝔇𝒫,𝔉𝒫)({\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}}) and (𝔇𝒫′,𝔉𝒫′)({\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}) are equipped with an orientation in the sense of Definition 3.43. Then an orientation on the given normally complex lift of 𝔇M{\mathfrak{D}}^{M} and 𝔉M{\mathfrak{F}}^{M} is given by
(1)
An isomorphism of orientation lines
𝔬Cp​p′M→∼𝔬p∨⊗𝔬p′.\mathfrak{o}_{C_{pp^{\prime}}^{M}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{p}^{\vee}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{p^{\prime}}.
(3.17)
(2)
For p<qp<q and p′p^{\prime}, an isomorphism of orientation lines
𝔬Cp​q𝒫⊗𝔬Cq​p′M⊗(𝔬ℱp​q​p′,p​qM⊗𝔬ℱp​q​p′,p​qM∨)→∼𝔬∂p​q​p′Cp​p′M.\mathfrak{o}_{C^{\mathcal{P}}_{pq}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{C^{M}_{qp^{\prime}}}\otimes(\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{F}^{M}_{pqp^{\prime},pq}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{F}^{M}_{pqp^{\prime},pq}}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{\partial^{pqp^{\prime}}C_{pp^{\prime}}^{M}}.
(3.18)
(3)
For pp and q′<p′q^{\prime}<p^{\prime}, an isomorphism of orientation lines
𝔬Cp​q′M⊗𝔬Cq′​p′𝒫′⊗(𝔬ℱp​q′​p′,p​qM⊗𝔬ℱp​q′​p′,p​qM∨)→∼𝔬∂p​q′​p′Cp​p′M.\mathfrak{o}_{C^{M}_{pq^{\prime}}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{C^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}}}\otimes(\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{F}^{M}_{pq^{\prime}p^{\prime},pq}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\mathcal{F}^{M}_{pq^{\prime}p^{\prime},pq}}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{\partial^{pq^{\prime}p^{\prime}}C_{pp^{\prime}}^{M}}.
(3.19)
(4)
The above isomorphisms are preserved by the Π\Pi-action.
For a flow bimodule MM from T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} to T𝒫′T^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}} as in Definition 3.45 (with 𝔇M{\mathfrak{D}}^{M}, 𝔉M{\mathfrak{F}}^{M} and a compatible collar structure), if it is further endowed with a orientation structure, we say it has an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift and abbreviate as 𝔇M{\mathfrak{D}}^{M}.
3.7. Constructing FOP perturbations
We extend the notion of FOP perturbations from the case of a single derived orbifold chart to the case of a derived orbifold lift of a flow category. The case of a flow bimodule is similar.
Definition 3.48 (Perturbations on a derived orbifold lift).
Given a derived orbifold lift 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} of a flow category T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} (see Definition 3.24).
(1)
A perturbation on 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} consists of a system of smooth perturbations
𝔖′=(𝒮p​q′:𝒰p​q→ℰp​q)p<q{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}=\Big({\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}\Big)_{p<q}
of the compact derived orbifold chart (𝒰p​q,ℰp​q,𝒮p​q)({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}) (see Definition 2.1) such that the diagram (3.6) still commutes if we replace all 𝒮p​q{\mathcal{S}}_{pq} by 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}.
(2)
Suppose the derived orbifold lift is equipped with a scaffolding (see Definition 3.28). Then we say that a perturbation 𝔖′{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime} respects the scaffolding if the following is true. For each pair of strata α≤β\alpha\leq\beta of 𝔸p​q𝒫{\mathbb{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}_{pq}, the datum 𝔖′{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime} induces a perturbation 𝒮α′{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime} of CαC_{\alpha} and a perturbation 𝒮β′{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{\prime} of CβC_{\beta} by taking the product. Then we require
𝒮β′|∂α𝒰β=Stabℱβ​α​(𝒮α′).{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{\prime}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}}={\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}}({\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime}).
(3)
Suppose the derived orbifold lift is equipped with a collar structure. Then we say that a perturbation 𝔖′{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime} respects the collar structure if for each p​qpq and each stratum α∈𝔸p​q𝒫\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}}, the following diagram commutes.
(πp​q,αcollar)∗​(ℰp​q|∂α𝒰p​q)\textstyle{(\pi_{pq,\alpha}^{\rm collar})^{*}({\mathcal{E}}_{pq}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}ϑp​q,αcollar\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{pq,\alpha}^{\rm collar}}ℰ∂α𝒰p​q\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}}}∂α𝒰p​q×[0,ϵ)𝔽α\textstyle{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\times[0,\epsilon)^{{\mathbb{F}}_{\alpha}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}(πp​q,αcollar)∗​(𝒮p​q′|∂α𝒰p​q)\scriptstyle{(\pi_{pq,\alpha}^{\rm collar})^{*}({\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}})}θp​q,αcollar\scriptstyle{\theta_{pq,\alpha}^{\rm collar}}𝒰p​q\textstyle{{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝒮p​q′\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}}
(4)
Suppose the derived orbifold lift is normally complex and is equipped with a package of compatible structures (scaffolding, collar structure, and straightening). Then a perturbation 𝔖′{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime} is called an FOP perturbation if respects the scaffolding, respects the collar structure, and for each p​qpq, with respect to the straightening, the restriction of 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} to the interior of each stratum ∂α𝒰p​q\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} is an FOP section of ℰp​q{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}.88
8
Indeed being an FOP perturbation only needs to refer to the straightening and the normal complex structure.
(5)
Under the assumptions of the last item, an FOP perturbation is called strongly transverse if the restriction of each 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} to the interior of each stratum of 𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} is strongly transverse (see Definition 2.7).
Theorem 3.49.
Given a flow category T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}}, a derived orbifold lift 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} with a normally complex structure, together with a package of compatible extra structures (scaffolding, collaring, and straightening), there exists a strongly transverse FOP perturbation. Moreover, we can make the perturbation Π\Pi-invariant, i.e.,
𝒮p​q′=𝒮a⋅p​a⋅q′​∀a∈Π​p,q∈𝒫{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}={\mathcal{S}}_{a\cdot p\ a\cdot q}^{\prime}\ \forall a\in\Pi\ p,q\in{\mathcal{P}}
after identifying Cp​qC_{pq} with Ca⋅p​a⋅qC_{a\cdot p\ a\cdot q}.
Proof.
The construction is based on the same induction strategy as defining the Hamiltonian Floer homology (as well as continuation maps etc.) using abstract perturbations as in [FO99] and [LT98b]. The package of additional structures is necessary because the FOP perturbation scheme is more rigid than the traditional smooth or continuous multivalued perturbation scheme. We start with a pair p<qp<q with minimal (nonzero) energy 𝒜𝒫​(q)−𝒜𝒫​(p){\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}(q)-{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p). Then by definition, 𝔸p​q𝒫{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\mathcal{P}} is a poset with a single element and hence the chart Cp​q=(𝒰p​q,ℰp​q,𝒮p​q)C_{pq}=({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}) is a derived orbifold chart with no boundary or corners. By the absolute version of Proposition 2.10, with respect to the normal complex structure and the straightening, there exists a strongly transverse FOP perturbation 𝒮p​q′:𝒰p​q→ℰp​q{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq} which can be arbitrarily close (measured in C0C^{0}) to 𝒮p​q{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}. In particular, 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} has a compact zero locus. We can also make such perturbations Π\Pi-invariant.
Now for a given pair p<qp<q, we state our induction hypothesis: for pair r<sr<s with p≤r<s≤qp\leq r<s\leq q and (p,q)≠(r,s)(p,q)\neq(r,s), we have constructed a strongly transverse FOP section with respect to the straightening and normal complex structure
𝒮r​s′:𝒰r​s→ℰr​s{\mathcal{S}}_{rs}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{rs}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{rs}
satisfying the compatibility condition for a perturbation (with respect to the collar structure and scaffolding, see Definition 3.48). We would like to construct a strongly transverse FOP perturbation 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} which extends the existing ones and which still satisfies the compatibility conditions.
For each proper stratum α=pr1⋯rlq\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q of p​qpq, the product of 𝒮p​r1′{\mathcal{S}}_{pr_{1}}^{\prime}, …\ldots, 𝒮rl​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{r_{l}q}^{\prime} provides a section
𝒮α′:=𝒮p​r1′×⋯×𝒮rl​q′:𝒰α→ℰα.{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{S}}_{pr_{1}}^{\prime}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{S}}_{r_{l}q}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}.
Then via the stabilization map
𝜽p​q,α:Stabℱp​q,α​(Cα)≃∂αCp​q{\bm{\theta}}_{pq,\alpha}:{\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{pq,\alpha}}(C_{\alpha})\simeq\partial^{\alpha}C_{pq}
one obtains a section
𝒮p​q,α′:∂α𝒰p​q→ℰp​q|∂α𝒰p​q{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime}:\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}}
which is the stabilization of 𝒮α′{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime}. We check the following conditions.
(1)
The collection of 𝒮p​q,α′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime} agree on overlaps. Indeed, for each pair of stratum α≤β\alpha\leq\beta, by the compatibility condition (see item (2) of Definition 3.48) satisfied by the existing perturbations (which is assumed as induction hypothesis), one has
𝒮β′|∂α𝒰β=Stabℱβ​α​(𝒮α′).{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{\prime}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}}={\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}}({\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime}).
Then by the definition of scaffolding (see Definition 3.28 and (3.13)), one has
𝒮p​q,β′|∂α𝒰p​q=Stabℱp​q,β​(𝒮β′|∂α𝒰β)=Stabℱp​q,β​(Stabℱβ​α​(𝒮α′))=Stabℱp​q,α​(𝒮α′)=𝒮p​q,α′.{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\beta}^{\prime}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}}={\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{pq,\beta}}({\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{\prime}|_{\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{\beta}})={\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{pq,\beta}}({\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}}({\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime}))\\
={\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{pq,\alpha}}({\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime})={\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime}.
(2)
The collection of 𝒮p​q,α′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime} respect the collar structure. This is a consequence of the induction hypothesis and the fact that the scaffolding respects the collar structure (see Definition 3.34).
(3)
Each 𝒮p​q,α′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime} is an FOP perturbation. This is a consequence of the compatibility condition on the additional structures (see Definition 3.35) which guarantees that the stabilization of an FOP section is still an FOP section.
(4)
Each 𝒮p​q,α′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime} is strongly transverse within the interior of ∂α𝒰p​q\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}. Notice that
Int𝒰α=Int𝒰p​r1×⋯×Int𝒰rl​q.{\rm Int}{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}={\rm Int}{\mathcal{U}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\rm Int}{\mathcal{U}}_{r_{l}q}.
Corollary 2.14 implies that the restriction of 𝒮α′{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}^{\prime} to the interior of 𝒰α{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha} is an FOP section. Moreover, as strong transversality is preserved under stabilization, we know that 𝒮p​q,α′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime} is strongly transverse within Int​∂α𝒰p​q=Stabℱp​q,α​(Int​𝒰α){\rm Int}\partial^{\alpha}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}={\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{pq,\alpha}}({\rm Int}{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}).
Therefore, we can extend the collection of 𝒮p​q,α′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq,\alpha}^{\prime} to a neighborhood of ∂𝒰p​q\partial{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} using the collar structure, simply by pulling back the existing perturbations using the projection maps. Hence we have obtained a section 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} of ℰp​q{\mathcal{E}}_{pq} defined in an open neighborhood of ∂𝒰p​q\partial{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}. The conditions on the collar structure imply that the extension is well-defined. The compatibility between straightenings and the collar structure implies that the extension is still an FOP section. As we can extend strongly transverse FOP sections in the standard “CUDV” fashion (see the relative version of Proposition 2.10), one can construct a strongly FOP perturbation 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} which extends the existing ones near the boundary. The compactness assumption on 𝒮p​q−1​(0){\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{-1}(0) implies that one can make the perturbed zero locus (𝒮p​q′)−1​(0)({\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime})^{-1}(0) compact. The inductive construction can then be carried on. Moreover, the Π\Pi-equivariance of the perturbation can be maintained in the induction process.
∎
3.8. Chain complexes and maps over the integers
We recall the notion of Novikov coefficient ring.
Definition 3.50.
(1)
The integral Novikov ring is the ring of formal Laurent series in a single variable TT with integer coefficients, i.e.
Λ:=ℤ[[T]][T−1]={𝔵=∑i=−m∞aiTi,wherem∈ℤandai∈ℤ}.\Lambda:={\mathbb{Z}}[[T]][T^{-1}]=\Big\{{\mathfrak{x}}=\sum_{i=-m}^{\infty}a_{i}T^{i},\ {\rm where}\ m\in{\mathbb{Z}}\ {\rm and}\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\Big\}.
(2)
The valuation on Λ\Lambda is the map
val:Λ→ℤ,val⁡(∑ai​Ti)=min⁡{i|ai≠0}.{\rm val}:\Lambda\to{\mathbb{Z}},\ {\rm val}(\sum a_{i}T^{i})={\rm min}\left\{i\ |\ a_{i}\neq 0\right\}.
Denote
Λ0:={𝔵∈Λ|val⁡(𝔵)=0}.\Lambda_{0}:=\{{\mathfrak{x}}\in\Lambda\ |\ {\rm val}({\mathfrak{x}})=0\}.
and
Λ+:={𝔵∈Λ|val⁡(𝔵)>0}.\Lambda_{+}:=\{{\mathfrak{x}}\in\Lambda\ |\ {\rm val}({\mathfrak{x}})>0\}.
3.8.1. The chain complexes
Now we start to build the chain complexes and chain maps. First, associated to a poset 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} as in Setup 3.5, one can define a ℤ/2​N{\mathbb{Z}}/2N-graded free Λ\Lambda-module as follows. Define
C∗𝒫:={∑p∈𝒫mpp|mp∈ℤ,∀c>0,#{p|mp≠0,𝒜𝒫(p)<c}<∞}.C_{*}^{\mathcal{P}}:=\left\{\sum_{p\in{\mathcal{P}}}m_{p}p\ |\ m_{p}\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ \forall c>0,\#\{p\ |\ m_{p}\neq 0,{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)<c\}<\infty\right\}.
This is a free abelian group graded by the index function ind𝒫:𝒫→ℤ/2​N{\rm ind}^{\mathcal{P}}:{\mathcal{P}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2N. Define a Λ\Lambda-module structure on C∗C_{*} by
Ta​(∑p∈𝒫mp​p):=∑p∈𝒫mp​(a⋅p).T^{a}\Big(\sum_{p\in{\mathcal{P}}}m_{p}p\Big):=\sum_{p\in{\mathcal{P}}}m_{p}(a\cdot p).
Conditions of Setup 3.5 implies that C∗C_{*} is a ℤ/2​N{\mathbb{Z}}/2N-graded free Λ\Lambda-module whose rank is equal to the cardinality of 𝒫/Π{\mathcal{P}}/\Pi.
Isolated zeroes in the free locus of each oriented derived orbifold chart induce integer counts. More precisely, fix a strongly transverse FOP perturbation
𝔖′:=(𝒮p​q′:𝒰p​q→ℰp​q)p<q.{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}:=\Big({\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}\Big)_{p<q}.
For each pair p<qp<q, we can write
𝒰p​q=⨆i=−∞+∞𝒰p​q[i]{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}=\bigsqcup_{i=-\infty}^{+\infty}{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}^{[i]}
where 𝒰p​q[i]⊂𝒰p​q{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}^{[i]}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{pq} is the open and closed subset of points whose local virtual dimension is ii. It follows from (3.5) that
𝒰p​q[i]≠∅⟹i≡ind𝒫​(p)−ind𝒫​(q)−1​mod​ 2​N.{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}^{[i]}\neq\emptyset\Longrightarrow i\equiv{\rm ind}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)-{\rm ind}^{\mathcal{P}}(q)-1\ {\rm mod}\ 2N.
Then let np​qn_{pq} be the count (with signs) of zeroes of 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} in the free locus of the zero-dimensional component 𝒰p​q[0]{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}^{[0]}. The sign of an isolated zero point of 𝒮p​q′{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime} is uniquely determined by the sign read off from the isomorphism (3.15). The compactness assumption (see item (3) of Definition 3.7) implies that np​qn_{pq} is finite. Moreover, by the finiteness of 𝒫/Π{\mathcal{P}}/\Pi and the compactness condition, these counts define a ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-linear map
d𝒫:C∗𝒫→C∗−1𝒫.d^{\mathcal{P}}:C_{*}^{\mathcal{P}}\to C_{*-1}^{\mathcal{P}}.
The Π\Pi-equivariance of the perturbation implies that
np​q=na⋅p​a⋅q,∀a∈ℤ​a​n​d​p,q∈𝒫n_{pq}=n_{a\cdot p\ a\cdot q},\ \forall a\in{\mathbb{Z}}\ and\ p,q\in{\mathcal{P}}
which further implies that d𝒫d^{\mathcal{P}} is Λ\Lambda-linear. By looking at 1-dimensional components of all derived orbifold charts, using the coherence of the orientations (3.16), one can see that d𝒫d^{\mathcal{P}} is a differential map, i.e., d𝒫∘d𝒫=0d^{\mathcal{P}}\circ d^{\mathcal{P}}=0. Therefore, one obtains a chain complex
(C∗𝒫,d𝒫)(C_{*}^{\mathcal{P}},d^{\mathcal{P}})
of Λ\Lambda-modules. One hence obtains the homology
H∗𝒫=⨁i∈ℤ/2​NHi𝒫H_{*}^{\mathcal{P}}=\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N}H_{i}^{\mathcal{P}}
which has a natural Λ\Lambda-module structure. One can show that the chain homotopy equivalence class of the chain complex does not depend on the choice of the perturbations. We omit the details because such a fact is not needed in our application.
3.8.2. The chain maps
Now suppose we have two topological flow categories, T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} over 𝒫{\mathcal{P}} and T𝒫′T^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}} over 𝒫′{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime} and a flow bimodule MM from T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} to T𝒫′T^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}.
Theorem 3.51.
Assume the following conditions.
(1)
There is an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} resp. 𝔇𝒫′{\mathfrak{D}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}} of T𝒫T^{\mathcal{P}} resp. T𝒫′T^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}.
(2)
There is an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift 𝔇𝒫​𝒫′{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}} of MM which extends 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} and 𝔇𝒫′{\mathfrak{D}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}.
(3)
On these derived orbifold lifts there exist compatible collar structures, scaffoldings, and straightenings.
Suppose we are given a strongly transverse FOP perturbation on 𝔇𝒫{\mathfrak{D}}^{\mathcal{P}} and a strongly transverse FOP perturbation on 𝔇𝒫′{\mathfrak{D}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}. Then there exists a strongly transverse FOP perturbation on 𝔇𝒫​𝒫′{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}} which extends the existing ones which is Π\Pi-invariant.
Proof.
This theorem is essentially a relative version of Theorem 3.49 and the proof is the same as we can always extend strongly transverse FOP perturbations from local to global using the standard “CUDV” fashion (see the relative version of Proposition 2.10).
∎
Now under the assumptions of Theorem 3.51, the existing strongly transverse FOP perturbations induce chain complexes
(C∗𝒫,d𝒫),\displaystyle\ (C_{*}^{\mathcal{P}},d^{\mathcal{P}}),\
(C∗𝒫′,d𝒫′).\displaystyle\ (C_{*}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}},d^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}).
We would like to define a chain map using the extended FOP perturbations on 𝔇𝒫​𝒫′{\mathfrak{D}}_{{\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}. Indeed, for p∈𝒫p\in{\mathcal{P}} and p′∈𝒫′p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}, consider the perturbation
𝒮p​p′′:𝒰p​p′→ℰp​p′.{\mathcal{S}}_{pp^{\prime}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pp^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pp^{\prime}}.
Let np​p′n_{pp^{\prime}} be the count (with signs read off from the isomorphism between orientation lines (3.17)) of zeros of 𝒮p​p′′{\mathcal{S}}_{pp^{\prime}}^{\prime} in the free locus of all components of 𝒰p​p′{\mathcal{U}}_{pp^{\prime}} with local virtual dimension zero. The compactness condition on Mp​p′M_{pp^{\prime}} (see Definition 3.10) implies that np​p′n_{pp^{\prime}} is finite. Moreover, (3.7) implies that
np​p′≠0⟹ind𝒫​(p)≡ind𝒫′​(p′)​mod​ 2​N.n_{pp^{\prime}}\neq 0\Longrightarrow{\rm ind}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\equiv{\rm ind}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}(p^{\prime})\ {\rm mod}\ 2N.
Then we formally define
ΨM:C∗𝒫→C∗𝒫′\Psi^{M}:C_{*}^{\mathcal{P}}\to C_{*}^{{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}
by linearly extending
ΨM​(p)=∑p′∈𝒫′np​p′​p′.\Psi^{M}(p)=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}n_{pp^{\prime}}p^{\prime}.
We claim that the above is a legitimate element of C∗𝒫′C_{*}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}. Indeed, this is a consequence of the condition (3.4) of Definition 3.10 and the finiteness of 𝒫′/Π{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}/\Pi. Hence ΨM\Psi^{M} is a well-defined morphisms of graded abelian group. Moreover, by the Π\Pi-equivariance of all structures, one has
np​p′=na⋅p​n⋅p′,∀a∈Π.n_{pp^{\prime}}=n_{a\cdot p\ n\cdot p^{\prime}},\ \forall a\in\Pi.
Hence ΨM\Psi^{M} is Λ\Lambda-linear. Lastly, by the description of codimension one stratum of all bimodule moduli spaces and the coherence of orientations (3.18) and (3.19), similar to the case of d𝒫∘d𝒫=0d^{\mathcal{P}}\circ d^{\mathcal{P}}=0, one has that ΨM\Psi^{M} is a chain map, i.e.,
ΨM∘d𝒫=d𝒫′∘ΨM.\Psi^{M}\circ d^{\mathcal{P}}=d^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}}\circ\Psi^{M}.
Remark 3.52.
We would like to remark that the definitions of d𝒫d^{\mathcal{P}} and ΨM\Psi^{M} from counting points in moduli spaces of virtual dimension 00, and the usual proof for (d𝒫)2=0(d^{\mathcal{P}})^{2}=0 and d𝒫′∘ΨM=ΨM∘d𝒫d^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}\circ\Psi^{M}=\Psi^{M}\circ d^{\mathcal{P}} from counting the boundary points of moduli spaces of virtual dimension 11 works in our setting, because the pseudocycle condition on the free locus of (𝒮p​q′)−1​(0)({\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime})^{-1}(0) guarantees that the topological boundary ∂((𝒮p​q′)−1​(0))free\partial(({\mathcal{S}}_{pq}^{\prime})^{-1}(0))^{\rm free} has codimension at least 22 so the desired compactness property, therefore the finiteness of algebraic counts holds.
4. Proof of the integral Arnold conjecture
In this section, we define the topological flow category associated with a non-degenerate Hamiltonian and the topological flow category constructed from a Morse–Smale function. Then we define two bimodules between these two flow categories, which are respectively the space-level lifts of the well-known Piunikhin–Salamon–Schwarz map (the PSS map) and its inverse (the SSP map). We state in Theorem 4.7 the most important technical result in this paper, namely, the existence of derived orbifold lifts of the aforementioned objects, whose proof is contained in later sections. Finally, we explain how to use the perturbation scheme developed in Section 2 and Section 3 to prove Theorem A.
The following is the standing assumption of our discussions later on.
Hypothesis 4.1.
(1)
The symplectic manifold (M,ω)(M,\omega) is integral, i.e., the de Rham cohomology class [ω]∈Hd​R2​(M)≅H2​(M,ℝ)[\omega]\in H_{dR}^{2}(M)\cong H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}) lies in the image of H2​(M,ℤ)→H2​(M,ℝ)H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}}).
(2)
HH is a nondegenerate Hamiltonian on (M,ω)(M,\omega).
(3)
All 1-periodic orbits of HH are embedded and any two of them are disjoint.
(4)
The symplectic actions (4.1) of all capped 1-periodic orbits are integral.
(5)
JJ is a time-independent ω\omega-compatible almost complex structure such that for all Floer trajectories with smooth domains (i.e., no sphere bubbles are attached and the trajectories are not broken) u:Θ→Xu:\Theta\to X, the linearized operator is surjective.
We explain why it suffices to prove the Arnold conjecture under these assumptions in order to establish it in full generality.
(1)
As we explained in the introduction of this paper, the case of Arnold conjecture for which [ω][\omega] is rational implies the general case, and this is equivalent to the case when [ω]∈H2​(M,ℤ)[\omega]\in H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}) by suitably rescaling ω\omega and HH using a common integer-valued factor.
(2)
Because any C2C^{2}-small perturbation of a nondegenerate Hamiltonian HH will not change the number of periodic orbits, we can freely perturb HH to guarantee that all 2​π2\pi-periodic orbits are nondegenerate.
(3)
As the 2-dimensional case of the integral Arnold conjecture is known, we can restrict ourselves to the case when dim​M≥4{\rm dim}M\geq 4. Then one can slightly perturb HH so that distinct periodic orbits do not intersect and all periodic orbits are embedded.
(4)
Given a nondegenerate Hamiltonian HH (whose capped 1-periodic orbits are all discrete), by adding a C2C^{2}-small, tt-independent function f:M→ℝf:M\to{\mathbb{R}} whose restriction to a neighborhood of the image of each periodic orbit is a constant does not change the set of capped 1-periodic orbits but will shift their symplectic actions by constants. Then we can add a sufficiently small function ff which makes the symplectic actions of all capped periodic orbits rational. Then the symplectic actions can be made integral by a further rescaling.
(5)
For any given JJ, one can slightly perturb HH to achieve transversality for all smooth Floer trajectories. Moreover, the perturbed Hamiltonian can be chosen to agree with HH up to second order on each 1-periodic orbits of HH (see [FHS95, Theorem 5.1]).
4.1. Hamiltonian Floer flow categories
4.1.1. The moduli spaces of Floer trajectories
We first review the basics about the moduli spaces of stable Floer trajectories. Let (M,ω)(M,\omega) be a compact symplectic manifold and H=(Ht)t∈S1H=(H_{t})_{t\in S^{1}} be a smooth 1-periodic Hamiltonian on MM. A capped 1-periodic orbit is a pair p=([u],p¯)p=([u],\underline{\smash{p}}) where p¯:S1→M\underline{\smash{p}}:S^{1}\to M is a 1-periodic orbit of the Hamiltonian vector field XHtX_{H_{t}} and [u][u] is an equivalence class of maps u:𝔻→Mu:{\mathbb{D}}\to M such that (we view 𝔻{\mathbb{D}} as the unit disk in ℂ{\mathbb{C}})
u​(e2​π​𝒊​t)=p¯​(t);u(e^{2\pi{\bm{i}}t})=\underline{\smash{p}}(t);
two such maps u1,u2:𝔻→Mu_{1},u_{2}:{\mathbb{D}}\to M are equivalent if
∫𝔻u1∗​ω=∫𝔻u2∗​ω.\int_{\mathbb{D}}u_{1}^{*}\omega=\int_{{\mathbb{D}}}u_{2}^{*}\omega.
Define the symplectic action of a capped orbit p=([u],p¯)p=([u],\underline{\smash{p}}) to be
𝒜H​(p)=∫𝔻u∗​ω+∫S1Ht​(p¯​(t))​𝑑t.{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p)=\int_{\mathbb{D}}u^{*}\omega+\int_{S^{1}}H_{t}(\underline{\smash{p}}(t))dt.
(4.1)
We denote by Per⁡(H){\rm Per}(H) the set of contractible 1-periodic orbits of HH, whose elements are denoted by p¯,q¯\underline{\smash{p}},\underline{\smash{q}}, etc. Denote by Per~​(H)\widetilde{{\rm Per}}(H) the set of capped 1-periodic orbits of HH, whose elements are denoted by p,qp,q, etc. It follows from the integrality assumption on the symplectic class that Per~​(H)\widetilde{{\rm Per}}(H) is a ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-covering of Per⁡(H){\rm Per}(H).
Choose an ω\omega-compatible almost complex structure JJ on MM. Let Θ=ℝ×S1\Theta={\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1} be the infinite cylinder with standard coordinates z=s+𝒊​tz=s+{\bm{i}}t. The Floer equation is the first-order equation for smooth maps u:Θ→Mu:\Theta\to M
∂u∂s+J⁡(∂u∂t−XHt​(u))=0.\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+J\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-X_{H_{t}}(u)\right)=0.
(4.2)
The associated energy for a map u:Θ→Mu:\Theta\to M is defined to be
EH​(u):=12​∫Θ(|∂u∂s|2+|∂u∂t−XHt​(u)|2)​𝑑s​𝑑t.E_{H}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Theta}\left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}\right|^{2}+\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-X_{H_{t}}(u)\right|^{2}\right)dsdt.
Any solution to (4.2) with finite energy necessarily converges as s→±∞s\to\pm\infty to periodic orbits of HH. Then one can use a pair of (capped) orbits to label solutions. Let ℳp¯​q¯Floer{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\smash{p}}\underline{\smash{q}}}^{\rm Floer} be the set of solutions to (4.2) which converge to p¯\underline{\smash{p}} resp. q¯\underline{\smash{q}} as s→−∞s\to-\infty resp. s→+∞s\to+\infty, modulo the obvious time translation. Denote by
ℳp​qFloer⊂ℳp¯​q¯Floer{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{\underline{\smash{p}}\underline{\smash{q}}}^{\rm Floer}
the subset of equivalence classes of solutions uu such that the concatenation of pp and uu is equivalent to qq as capped 1-periodic orbits. Then for any solution uu to (4.2) representing an element of ℳp​qFloer{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}, it is standard that
EH​(u)=𝒜H​(q)−𝒜H​(p).E_{H}(u)={\mathcal{A}}_{H}(q)-{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p).
(4.3)
As a result the Floer differential increases the symplectic action.
The moduli spaces in general are not compact with respect to the Cloc∞C^{\infty}_{\rm loc}-topology. Indeed, the space ℳp​qFloer{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer} admits a natural compactification called the Gromov–Floer compactification, denoted by ℳ¯p​qFloer\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}, incorporating both bubbling of holomorphic spheres and breaking of Floer trajectories, see e.g. [FO99, Section 18]. It is standard knowledge that ℳ¯p​qFloer\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer} is a compact Hausdorff topological space.99
9
It is possible that ℳp​qFloer=∅{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}=\emptyset while ℳ¯p​qFloer≠∅\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}\neq\emptyset.
4.1.2. The flow category
We now package the collection of Floer moduli spaces into a flow category. First we see how this system of moduli spaces fits into Setup 3.5. Let N∈ℤ≥0N\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0} be the minimal Chern number of (M,ω)(M,\omega). The integral symplectic form ω\omega defines a homomorphism
ω:π2​(M)→ℤ.\omega:\pi_{2}(M)\to{\mathbb{Z}}.
Define
Π:=π2​(M)/ker​ω.\Pi:=\pi_{2}(M)/{\rm ker}\omega.
Then Π\Pi is an infinite cyclic group and ω\omega induces an injection
ω:Π→ℤ\omega:\Pi\to{\mathbb{Z}}
Then we define
𝒫Floer:=Per~​(H).{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}:=\widetilde{\rm Per}(H).
Define the partial order by the existence of nonempty Floer moduli spaces, i.e.,
p≤q⟹ℳ¯p​qFloer≠∅.p\leq q\Longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}\neq\emptyset.
It has a free Π\Pi-action defined by taking the connected sum between a representative of a capped 1-periodic orbit and a representative of an element a∈Πa\in\Pi. Define
𝒜𝒫Floer:=𝒜H,\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{A}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}}:={\mathcal{A}}_{H},\
ind𝒫Floer:= Conley–Zehnder index.\displaystyle\ {\rm ind}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}}:=\text{ Conley--Zehnder\ index}.
Then 𝒫Floer{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer} satisfies conditions of Setup 3.5.
Notation 4.2.
As a convention, objects labelled by 𝒫Floer{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer} are often also labelled by Floer{\rm Floer}. For example, we abbreviate
𝒜Floer:=𝒜𝒫Floer,\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}:={\mathcal{A}}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}},\
indFloer:=ind𝒫Floer.\displaystyle\ {\rm ind}^{\rm Floer}:={\rm ind}^{{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}}.
One can then describe the stratifications on Floer moduli spaces. As in Notation 3.6, for each pair p,q∈𝒫Floerp,q\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}, one has the homogeneous poset
𝔸p​qFloer:={α=pr1⋯rlq|p<r1<⋯<rl<q}.{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}:=\big\{\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\ |\ p<r_{1}<\cdots<r_{l}<q\big\}.
Then the moduli space ℳ¯p​qFloer\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer} an 𝔸p​qFloer{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}-stratified topological space. Indeed, for each α=pr1⋯rlq\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q, one has
∂αℳ¯p​qFloer≅ℳ¯p​r1Floer×⋯×ℳ¯rl​qFloer\partial^{\alpha}\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}\cong\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pr_{1}}^{\rm Floer}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{r_{l}q}^{\rm Floer}
as the α\alpha-stratum is the subset of “broken trajectories” of a type described by α\alpha.
Definition 4.3.
The Hamiltonian Floer flow category (associated to HH and JJ), denoted by TFloerT^{\rm Floer}, is the topological flow category over 𝒫Floer{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer} whose morphism space between p,q∈Per~​(H)p,q\in\widetilde{{\rm Per}}(H) is the moduli space ℳ¯p​qFloer\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}, and whose composition maps are the natural inclusions
ℳ¯p​rFloer×ℳ¯r​qFloer→ℳ¯p​qFloer,∀p≤r≤q.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pr}^{\rm Floer}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{rq}^{\rm Floer}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer},\ \forall p\leq r\leq q.
4.2. Morse flow category
We describe the definition of the more classical Morse flow category and explain how it fits into the general framework of this paper. We declare that in this paper Morse flows are always the ascending flow, i.e., the flow generated by the gradient vector field. Suppose (f,g)(f,g) is a Morse–Smale pair on MM, namely, ff is a Morse function and gg is a Riemannian metric such that the unstable manifold of any critical point intersects transversely with any stable manifold. We use x¯,y¯\underline{\smash{x}},\underline{\smash{y}} to denote the critical points of ff. To ensure the moduli spaces of (unparametrized) gradient flow lines to have smooth structures, we assume that near each x¯∈crit⁡(f)\underline{\smash{x}}\in{\rm crit}(f), there exists a coordinate chart (x1,…,x2​n)(x_{1},\dots,x_{2n}) such that f=±x12±⋯±x2​n2f=\pm x_{1}^{2}\pm\cdots\pm x_{2n}^{2} and g=dx1⊗dx1+⋯dx2​n⊗dx2​ng=dx_{1}\otimes dx_{1}+\cdots dx_{2n}\otimes dx_{2n}. Then by [Weh12], for any pair of cricial points x¯,y¯∈crit⁡(f)\underline{\smash{x}},\underline{\smash{y}}\in{\rm crit}(f), the moduli space
ℳ¯Morsex¯​y¯\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Morse}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}
of unparametrized broken flow lines connecting xx and yy is a smooth manifold with faces. It is standard knowledge that upon choosing orientations on the unstable manifolds of all critical points, one can count rigid Morse flow lines (with signs) and define a ℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-graded chain complex over ℤ{\mathbb{Z}} and its homology coincides with H∗​(M,ℤ)H_{*}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}).
We want to fit the Morse flow category into the abstract framework. Define
𝒫Morse:=Π×crit⁡(f){\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}:=\Pi\times{\rm crit}(f)
with a partial order defined by
(a,x¯)≤(b,y¯)⟺a=bandℳ¯≠Morsex¯​y¯∅.(a,\underline{\smash{x}})\leq(b,\underline{\smash{y}})\Longleftrightarrow a=b\ {\rm and}\ \overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm Morse}\neq\emptyset.
Π\Pi naturally acts freely on 𝒫Morse{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}. The action function is defined by
𝒜Morse​(a,x¯)=ω⁡(a)+ϵ​f​(x¯){\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Morse}(a,\underline{\smash{x}})=\omega(a)+\epsilon f(\underline{\smash{x}})
where ϵ\epsilon is a sufficiently small positive number. The index function is defined by
indMorse​(a,x¯)≡12​dim​M−Morse​index​of​x¯​mod​ 2​N.{\rm ind}^{\rm Morse}(a,\underline{\smash{x}})\equiv\frac{1}{2}{\rm dim}M-{\rm Morse\ index\ of\ }\underline{\smash{x}}\ {\rm mod}\ 2N.
One can easily check that the triple (𝒫Morse,𝒜Morse,indMorse)({\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse},{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Morse},{\rm ind}^{\rm Morse}) satisfies conditions of Setup 3.5. Then following Notation 3.6 one obtains a collection of homogeneous posets 𝔸x​yMorse{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm Morse} for all x,y∈𝒫Morsex,y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}. Define
ℳ¯=Morsex​y{ℳ¯x¯​y¯Morse,if​x=(a,x¯)​and​y=(a,y¯),∅,otherwise.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm Morse}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm Morse},&\ {\rm if}\ x=(a,\underline{\smash{x}})\ {\rm and}\ y=(a,\underline{\smash{y}}),\\
\emptyset,&\ {\rm otherwise}.\end{array}\right.
Then each ℳ¯x​yMorse\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm Morse} is stratified by the poset 𝔸x​yMorse{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm Morse}. We can then define the flow category TMorse:=TMorse​(f,g)T^{\rm Morse}:=T^{\rm Morse}(f,g) associated with (f,g)(f,g) as follows.
Definition 4.4.
The Morse flow category TMorseT^{\rm Morse} is the topological flow category over 𝒫Morse{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse} whose morphism spaces are Tx​yMorse=ℳ¯x​yMorseT_{xy}^{\rm Morse}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm Morse} and whose composition maps are the natural inclusions
ℳ¯x​zMorse×ℳ¯z​yMorse≅∂x​z​yℳ¯x​yMorse↪ℳ¯x​yMorse.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xz}^{\rm Morse}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{zy}^{\rm Morse}\cong\partial^{xzy}\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm Morse}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm Morse}.
It is obvious that TMorseT^{\rm Morse} is a strict Π\Pi-equivariant flow category.
As transversality is already achieved for the Morse flow category and there is no orbifold behavior, one does not need to use derived orbifold lift nor normal complex structures to define the resulting chain complex. However we would like to formally put this case into the general framework of the previous section as it will be necessary when we connect the Morse flow category to objects where transversality fails and orbifold behavior appears.
First, following the abstract outer-collaring recipe, one can construct an outer-collaring of the Morse flow category. Fix the width r=1r=1. The outer-collared Morse flow category (TMorse)+(T^{{\rm Morse}})^{+} has morphism spaces being (ℳ¯)Morsex​y+(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{{\rm Morse}})^{+}. A priori this is only a topological flow category. However, one can equip the morphism spaces and composition maps with smooth structure. Indeed, by [Weh12, Theorem 1.4] which proves the associativity of gluing maps for the special kind of Morse–Smale pair (f,g)(f,g) (see also [Qin18]), the original Morse flow category has compatible “interior” collars. Then the outer-collaring construction does not alter the feature and put a smooth structure on each space (ℳ¯)Morsex​y+(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm Morse})^{+} such that the composition maps are smooth.
Second, as transversality is already achieved and the moduli spaces are manifolds but not orbifolds, the collection of outer-collared moduli spaces together with the zero obstruction bundle and the zero Kuranishi map form a derived orbifold lift of (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+}, denoted by 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}. The outer-collaring provides a collar structure on this lift. There is also the trivial scaffolding and the trivial straightening, which are obviously compatible with the collar structure. In the manifold case, there is only the trivial normal complex structure. Lastly, it is a classical knowledge that upon choosing orientations on all unstable manifolds the Morse moduli spaces inherit coherent orientations. Here the orientation line 𝔬x{\mathfrak{o}}_{x} associated with a capped critical point x=(a,x¯)x=(a,\underline{\smash{x}}) is defined to be the orientation line of the tangent space of the stable submanifold of ∇gf\nabla_{g}f at x¯\underline{\smash{x}}. We summarize these observations as follows.
Lemma 4.5.
A choice of orientations on all unstable manifolds of ∇gf\nabla^{g}f makes 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse} an oriented and normally complex drived orbifold lift of (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+} equipped with a compatible package of additional structures. Moreover, the 00-perturbation is a strongly transverse FOP perturbation on 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse} which defines the ℤ/2​N{\mathbb{Z}}/2N-graded chain complex (C∗​(f,Λ),dMorse)(C_{*}(f;\Lambda),d^{\rm Morse}) of Λ\Lambda-modules, whose homology is isomorphic to the Λ\Lambda-module
Hn−∗(2​N)(M;Λ)=⨁i∈ℤ/2​NHn−i(2​N)(M;ℤ)⊗ℤΛ.H^{(2N)}_{n-*}(M;\Lambda)=\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N}H^{(2N)}_{n-i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda.
Here
Hi(2​N)​(M,ℤ):=⨁j≡i​mod​ 2​NHj​(M,ℤ).H_{i}^{(2N)}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}):=\bigoplus_{j\equiv i\ {\rm mod}\ 2N}H_{j}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}).
4.3. PSS and SSP bimodules
4.3.1. Moduli spaces
Now we describe the moduli spaces which allow us to interpolate between Floer theory and Morse theory. We first set up a convention: PSS moduli spaces are defined by objects with input from Morse critical points and output from 1-periodic (capped) Hamiltonian orbits and SSP moduli spaces parametrizes objects in the reversed direction.
We only describe PSS moduli spaces in detail. The case of SSP moduli spaces is similar. To define the equation and hence the moduli space, we make the following choices.
(1)
An integer CPSS∈ℤC^{\rm PSS}\in{\mathbb{Z}} satisfying
inf(t,x)∈S1×MHt​(x)>CPSS.\inf_{(t,x)\in S^{1}\times M}H_{t}(x)>C^{\rm PSS}.
(4.4)
(2)
A smooth, non-decreasing cut-off function ρ:ℝ→[0,1]\rho:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1] such that
ρ⁡(s)={0,s≤0,1,s≥1.\rho(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0,&\ s\leq 0,\\
1,&\ s\geq 1.\end{array}\right.
(4.5)
Define a 22-parameter family of functions
Hs,tPSS​(x)=(1−ρ⁡(s))​CPSS+ρ⁡(s)​Ht​(x).H_{s,t}^{{\rm PSS}}(x)=(1-\rho(s))C^{\rm PSS}+\rho(s)H_{t}(x).
Then over the cylinder Θ\Theta one has the vector-field-valued 11-form
XHPSS⊗d​tX_{H}^{\rm PSS}\otimes dt
which is defined by the Hamiltonian vector field of Hs,tPSS​(x)H_{s,t}^{{\rm PSS}}(x).
Note that it vanishes for s≤0s\leq 0. The PSS equation is
∂u∂s+J⁡(∂u∂t−XHPSS​(u))=0,where​u∈C∞​(Θ,M).\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+J\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-X_{H}^{\rm PSS}(u)\right)=0,\ {\rm where}\ u\in C^{\infty}(\Theta,M).
(4.6)
The energy of a solution is defined to be and is computed as
EPSS​(u):=∫Θ‖∂su‖2​𝑑s​𝑑t=∫Θω⁡(∂su,∂tu−XHPSS​(u))​𝑑s​𝑑t=∫Θu∗​ω+∫Θd​Hs,tPSS​(∂su)​𝑑s​𝑑t=∫Θu∗​ω+∫Θ∂∂s​(Hs,tPSS​(u))​ds​dt−∫Θ∂Hs,tPSS∂s​ds​dt.\begin{split}E^{\rm PSS}(u):=&\ \int_{\Theta}\|\partial_{s}u\|^{2}dsdt\\
=&\ \int_{\Theta}\omega(\partial_{s}u,\partial_{t}u-X_{H}^{\rm PSS}(u))dsdt\\
=&\ \int_{\Theta}u^{*}\omega+\int_{\Theta}dH_{s,t}^{\rm PSS}(\partial_{s}u)dsdt\\
=&\ \int_{\Theta}u^{*}\omega+\int_{\Theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}(H_{s,t}^{\rm PSS}(u))dsdt-\int_{\Theta}\frac{\partial H^{\rm PSS}_{s,t}}{\partial s}dsdt.\end{split}
If the energy is finite, then by Gromov’s removal of singularity theorem, uu extends to a JJ-holomorphic map near −∞-\infty and hence automatically determines a cap for the periodic orbit at +∞+\infty. Hence one can use a capped 1-periodic orbit p∈Per~​(H)p\in\widetilde{{\rm Per}}(H) to label the moduli space of PSS solutions. It is similar to the case of Floer trajectories that ℳ∙pPSS{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\bullet p}^{\rm PSS} admits a natural compactification consisting of “stable PSS trajectories,” i.e. configurations with both cylindrical and spherical components. Denote the compactification by
ℳ¯∙pPSS.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\bullet p}^{\rm PSS}.
(4.7)
For each map uu representing an element of ℳ¯∙pPSS\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\bullet p}^{\rm PSS}, one has the following energy identity
EPSS​(u)=𝒜H​(p)−CPSS−∫Θρ′​(s)​(H⁡(u)−CPSS)​𝑑s​𝑑t.E^{\rm PSS}(u)={\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p)-C^{\rm PSS}-\int_{\Theta}\rho^{\prime}(s)(H(u)-C^{\rm PSS})dsdt.
An important consequence of (4.4) is that
ℳ¯≠PSS∙p∅⟹𝒜H(p)−CPSS>0.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\bullet p}^{\rm PSS}\neq\emptyset\Longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p)-C^{\rm PSS}>0.
(4.8)
We introduce
d∙p:=𝒜H​(p)−CPSSd_{\bullet p}:={\mathcal{A}}_{H}(p)-C^{\rm PSS}
and call it the topological energy of a solution uu.
Now we bring in the Morse theory of a Morse–Smale pair (f,g)(f,g). Given x¯∈crit⁡(f)\underline{\smash{x}}\in{\rm crit}(f), consider the unstable manifold Wu​(x¯)⊂MW^{u}(\underline{\smash{x}})\subset M of the flow of ∇gf\nabla^{g}f and its compactification Wu​(x¯)¯\overline{W^{u}(\underline{\smash{x}})}. Then define
ℳ¯:=PSSx¯​pev∙−1(Wu​(x¯)¯),\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}p}^{\rm PSS}:={\rm ev}_{\bullet}^{-1}(\overline{W^{u}(\underline{\smash{x}})}),
where ev∙:ℳ¯∙pPSS→M{\rm ev}_{\bullet}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\bullet p}^{\rm PSS}\to M is the evaluation map at −∞-\infty.
Moreover, if x=(a,x¯)∈𝒫Morsex=(a,\underline{\smash{x}})\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse} and p∈𝒫Floerp\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}, then define
ℳ¯x​pPSS:=ℳ¯x¯​(−a)⋅pPSS.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xp}^{\rm PSS}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\ (-a)\cdot p}^{\rm PSS}.
The stratifications on the PSS moduli spaces fit into the general framework of bimodules described in the previous section. Recall that to x∈𝒫=𝒫Morsex\in{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse} and p∈𝒫′=𝒫Floerp\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer} one associates a homogeneous poset
𝔸x​p:=𝔸x​pPSS:={xy1⋯ykql⋯q1p|x<y1<⋯<yk,ql<⋯<q1<p}{\mathbb{A}}_{xp}:={\mathbb{A}}_{xp}^{\rm PSS}:=\{xy_{1}\cdots y_{k}q_{l}\cdots q_{1}p\ |\ x<y_{1}<\cdots<y_{k},q_{l}<\cdots<q_{1}<p\}
which has the natural partial order induced from inclusion and the depth function
depth(xy1⋯ykql⋯q1p)=k+l.{\rm depth}(xy_{1}\cdots y_{k}q_{l}\cdots q_{1}p)=k+l.
The PSS moduli space ℳ¯x​pPSS\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xp}^{\rm PSS} is stratified by 𝔸x​pPSS{\mathbb{A}}_{xp}^{\rm PSS} with corresponding strata
(ℳ¯)PSSx​pxy1⋯ykql⋯q1≅ℳ¯×Morsex​y1⋯×ℳ¯×Morseyk−1​ykℳ¯×PSSyk​qlℳ¯×Floerql​ql−1⋯×ℳ¯Floerq1​p\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xp}^{\rm PSS}\right)_{xy_{1}\cdots y_{k}q_{l}\cdots q_{1}}\cong\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy_{1}}^{\rm Morse}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{y_{k-1}y_{k}}^{\rm Morse}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{y_{k}q_{l}}^{\rm PSS}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{q_{l}q_{l-1}}^{\rm Floer}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{q_{1}p}^{\rm Floer}
i.e., the subset of configurations with breakings described by the word xy1⋯ykql⋯q1pxy_{1}\cdots y_{k}q_{l}\cdots q_{1}p. Gromov compactness shows that there are at most finitely many nonempty stratum.
To define the SSP moduli spaces, let us choose an integer CSSP∈ℤC^{\rm SSP}\in{\mathbb{Z}} such that
sup(t,x)∈S1×MHt​(x)<CSSP.\sup_{(t,x)\in S^{1}\times M}H_{t}(x)<C^{\rm SSP}.
(4.9)
Using the same cut-off function as in (4.5), we can write down another 22-parameter family of functions
Hs,tSSP​(x)=(1−ρ⁡(s))​Ht​(x)+ρ⁡(s)​CSSP.H_{s,t}^{{\rm SSP}}(x)=(1-\rho(s))H_{t}(x)+\rho(s)C^{{\rm SSP}}.
(4.10)
Denote the associated vector-field-valued 11-form on Θ\Theta by XHSSP⊗d​tX_{H}^{{\rm SSP}}\otimes dt, then the SSP equation is written as
∂u∂s+J⁡(∂u∂t−XHSSP​(u))=0,where​u∈C∞​(Θ,M).\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+J\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-X_{H}^{\rm SSP}(u)\right)=0,\ {\rm where}\ u\in C^{\infty}(\Theta,M).
Because XHSSP​d​tX_{H}^{{\rm SSP}}dt vanishes for s≥0s\geq 0, any solution uu extends to a JJ-holomorphic map near +∞+\infty, which determines a cap for the periodic orbit at −∞-\infty. Given p∈Per~​(H)p\in\widetilde{{\rm Per}}(H), we cam similarly consider “stable SSP trajectories” which constitute a compact moduli space
ℳ¯p∙SSP.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{p\bullet}^{{\rm SSP}}.
For any representative uu of a point in ℳ¯p∙SSP\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{p\bullet}^{{\rm SSP}}, its topological energy is defined to be
dp∙:=CSSP−𝒜H(p).d_{p\bullet}:=C^{\rm SSP}-\mathcal{A}_{H}(p).
Due to the choice (4.9), we see that dp∙>0d_{p\bullet}>0 as long as ℳ¯p∙SSP≠∅\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{p\bullet}^{{\rm SSP}}\neq\emptyset. Now suppose x¯∈crit⁡(f)\underline{\smash{x}}\in{\rm crit}(f), let Ws​(x¯)⊂MW^{s}(\underline{\smash{x}})\subset M be the stable submanifold of ∇gf\nabla^{g}f and denote by Ws​(x¯)¯\overline{W^{s}(\underline{\smash{x}})} its compactification. Introduce the moduli space
ℳ¯p​x¯SSP:=ev∙−1​(Ws​(x¯)¯),\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{{\rm SSP}}_{p\underline{\smash{x}}}:={\rm ev}_{\bullet}^{-1}(\overline{W^{s}(\underline{\smash{x}})}),
where ev∙:ℳ¯p∙SSP→M{\rm ev}_{\bullet}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{p\bullet}^{{\rm SSP}}\to M is the evaluation map at +∞+\infty. If x=(a,x¯)∈𝒫Morsex=(a,\underline{\smash{x}})\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse} and p∈𝒫Floerp\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}, then define
ℳ¯p​xSSP:=ℳ¯a⋅p​x¯SSP.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{px}^{\rm SSP}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{a\cdot p\ \underline{\smash{x}}}^{\rm SSP}.
Similar to the PSS case, the homogeneous posets associated with SSP spaces are denoted by
𝔸p​xSSP:={pq1⋯qlyk⋯y1x|p<q1<⋯<ql,yk<⋯<y1<x}{\mathbb{A}}_{px}^{\rm SSP}:=\{pq_{1}\cdots q_{l}y_{k}\cdots y_{1}x|\ p<q_{1}<\cdots<q_{l},y_{k}<\cdots<y_{1}<x\}
for p∈𝒫Floerp\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer} and x∈𝒫Morsex\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}, endowed with depth function
depth(pq1⋯qlyk⋯y1x)=k+l.{\rm depth}(pq_{1}\cdots q_{l}y_{k}\cdots y_{1}x)=k+l.
ℳ¯p​xSSP\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{px}^{\rm SSP} is stratified by 𝔸p​xSSP{\mathbb{A}}_{px}^{\rm SSP} with corresponding strata
(ℳ¯)SSPp​xpq1⋯qlyk⋯y1x≅ℳ¯×Floerp​q1⋯×ℳ¯×Floerql−1​qlℳ¯×SSPql​ykℳ¯×Morseyk​yk−1⋯×ℳ¯.Morsey1​x\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{px}^{\rm SSP}\right)_{pq_{1}\cdots q_{l}y_{k}\cdots y_{1}x}\cong\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq_{1}}^{\rm Floer}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{q_{l-1}q_{l}}^{\rm Floer}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{q_{l}y_{k}}^{\rm SSP}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{y_{k}y_{k-1}}^{\rm Morse}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{y_{1}x}^{\rm Morse}.
4.3.2. The bimodule structure
The PSS and SSP moduli spaces can be packaged into two flow bimodules (see Definition 3.10). Recall that one has the Floer flow category TFloerT^{\rm Floer} and the Morse flow category TMorseT^{\rm Morse}. Essentially by the way we compactify the PSS resp. SSP moduli spaces we see that one can define a flow bimodule MPSSM^{\rm PSS} from TMorseT^{\rm Morse} to TFloerT^{\rm Floer} and a flow bimodule MSSPM^{\rm SSP} from TFloerT^{\rm Floer} to TMorseT^{\rm Morse}. We explain the specific terms for the PSS bimodule; the SSP case is completely symmetric.
Proposition 4.6.
For x∈𝒫Morsex\in{\mathcal{P}}^{{\rm Morse}} and p∈𝒫Floerp\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}, define MPSSx​p:=ℳ¯x​pPSSM^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xp}^{\rm PSS}. Then together with the natural inclusion of the boundary strata
ℳ¯Morsex​y×ℳ¯y​pPSS→ℳ¯x​pPSS,\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm Morse}}_{xy}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{yp}^{\rm PSS}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xp}^{\rm PSS},
ℳ¯x​qPSS×ℳ¯q​pFloer→ℳ¯x​pPSS,\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xq}^{\rm PSS}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{qp}^{\rm Floer}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xp}^{\rm PSS},
the spaces Mx​pPSSM^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp} define a flow bimodule from TMorseT^{\rm Morse} to TFloerT^{\rm Floer}.
Proof.
For any x∈𝒫Morsex\in{\mathcal{P}}^{{\rm Morse}} and p∈𝒫Morsep\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse} the moduli space Mx​pPSSM^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp} is compact due to Gromov compactness and the bound on the topological energy. For such xx and pp, indeed the difference of energies 𝒜Morse​(x)−𝒜Floer​(p){\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Morse}(x)-{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(p) is uniformly bounded. The associaticity of inclusions of boundary strata follows from the construction, so is the strict Π\Pi-equivariance property.
∎
We refer to such a bimodule as the PSS bimodule, and the version for the SSP spaces as SSP bimodule, denoted by MPSSM^{\rm PSS} and MSSPM^{\rm SSP} respectively.
4.4. The pearly bimodule
We consider the moduli space of (parametrized) JJ-holomorphic maps u:ℂ​ℙ1→Mu:\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to M with two marked points z−=0z_{-}=0 and z+=∞z_{+}=\infty. Given a homology class A∈H2​(M,ℤ)A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}), let ℳ(M,J;A)pearl0,2{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\rm pearl}(M,J;A) be the moduli space of (parametrized) JJ-holomorphic maps representing the class AA whose domains are smooth. It has a Gromov compactification ℳ¯(M,J;A)pearl0,2\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\rm pearl}(M,J;A). Each element of this compactification is represented by a stable map whose domain has a distinguished component whose parametrization is fixed. Moreover, by intersecting with the unstable manifold of x¯∈crit​f\underline{\smash{x}}\in{\rm crit}f at z−z_{-} and with the stable manifold of y¯∈crit​f\underline{\smash{y}}\in{\rm crit}f at z+z_{+}, we have a moduli space
ℳ¯(M,J;A)pearlx¯​y¯\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm pearl}(M,J;A)
For a∈Πa\in\Pi, define
ℳ¯(a)pearlx¯​y¯:=⋃ω⁡(A)=ω⁡(a)ℳ¯(M,J;A)pearlx¯​y¯.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm pearl}(a):=\bigcup_{\omega(A)=\omega(a)}\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm pearl}(M,J;A).
Then given x,y∈𝒫Morsex,y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse} which can be written as (a,x¯)(a,\underline{\smash{x}}) and (b,y¯)(b,\underline{\smash{y}}) where a,b∈Πa,b\in\Pi and x¯,y¯∈crit​f\underline{\smash{x}},\underline{\smash{y}}\in{\rm crit}f, define
ℳ¯:=pearlx​yℳ¯(b−a)pearlx¯​y¯.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm pearl}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm pearl}(b-a).
The pearly moduli spaces are stratified by configurations which have broken Morse trajectories either on the incoming edge or on the out-going edge. Indeed, for 𝒫=𝒫′=𝒫Morse{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}, as in Notation 3.6 there is a system of homogeneous posets indexed by pairs of x,y∈𝒫Morsex,y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}. More explicitly, given x,y∈𝒫Morsex,y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}, define
𝔸x​ypearl:={xx1⋯xkyl⋯y1y|xi=(a,x¯i),yj=(b,y¯j),x¯<x¯1<⋯<x¯k,y¯l<⋯<y¯1<y¯}.{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm pearl}:=\left\{xx_{1}\cdots x_{k}y_{l}\cdots y_{1}y\ \left|\ \begin{array}[]{c}x_{i}=(a,\underline{\smash{x}}_{i}),\ y_{j}=(b,\underline{\smash{y}}_{j}),\\
\underline{\smash{x}}<\underline{\smash{x}}_{1}<\cdots<\underline{\smash{x}}_{k},\ \underline{\smash{y}}_{l}<\cdots<\underline{\smash{y}}_{1}<\underline{\smash{y}}\end{array}\right.\right\}.
Note that all the xix_{i}’s resp. yjy_{j}’s are marked with the same class a∈Πa\in\Pi resp. b∈Πb\in\Pi, because otherwise, the morphism space Txi​xi+1MorseT^{\rm Morse}_{x_{i}x_{i+1}} or Tyj​yj+1MorseT^{\rm Morse}_{y_{j}y_{j+1}} is empty by the construction in Section 4.2. Given α=xx1⋯xkyl⋯y1y∈𝔸x​ypearl\alpha=xx_{1}\cdots x_{k}y_{l}\cdots y_{1}y\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm pearl}, the corresponding stratum in the pearly moduli space is
(ℳ¯)pearlx​yα≅ℳ¯×Morsex​x1⋯×ℳ¯×Morsexk−1​xkℳ¯×pearlxk​ylℳ¯yl​yl−1Morse×⋯×ℳ¯.Morsey1​y\Big(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm pearl}\Big)_{\alpha}\cong\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xx_{1}}^{\rm Morse}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{x_{k-1}x_{k}}^{\rm Morse}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{x_{k}y_{l}}^{\rm pearl}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{y_{l}y_{l-1}}^{\rm Morse}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{y_{1}y}^{\rm Morse}.
There are again only finitely many nonempty strata, thanks to Gromov compactness. Using the formulation of flow bimodules, we can see that the collection of pearly moduli spaces and the product structures of various strata provide a flow bimodule from TMorseT^{\rm Morse} to TMorseT^{\rm Morse}, which we call the pearly bimodule and denote it by MpearlM^{\rm pearl}. The strict Π\Pi-action on MpearlM^{\rm pearl} follows from the construction. The outer-collaring (of width 1) of MpearlM^{\rm pearl} provides a bimodule (Mpearl)+(M^{\rm pearl})^{+} from (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+} to (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+}.
4.5. Main Theorems
Using the notations introduced above, we can state the output of Sections 5, 6, and 7.
Theorem 4.7.
Denote by (TFloer)+(T^{\rm Floer})^{+} the outer-collaring (see Section 3.3.2) of the Hamiltonian Floer flow category TFloerT^{\rm Floer}. Then (TFloer)+(T^{\rm Floer})^{+} admits a derived orbifold lift (Definition 3.24)
𝔇Floer=({Cp​qFloer=(𝒰p​qFloer,ℰp​qFloer,𝒮p​qFloer,ψp​qFloer)}p<q,{𝜾β​αFloer}α≤β),{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}=\Big(\big\{C^{\rm Floer}_{pq}=({\mathcal{U}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq},\psi^{\rm Floer}_{pq})\big\}_{p<q},\big\{{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm Floer}\big\}_{\alpha\leq\beta}\Big),
with a collar structure (Definition 3.26)
{θ^β​αcollar,Floer}α≤β,\{\widehat{\theta}_{\beta\alpha}^{{\rm collar},{\rm Floer}}\}_{\alpha\leq\beta},
and a scaffolding (Definition 3.28)
(ℱβ​αFloer,𝜽β​αFloer)α≤β\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm Floer},{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm Floer}\big)_{\alpha\leq\beta}
such that they are compatible (Definition 3.34). Moreover, such a lift can be upgraded to an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift (Definition 3.44). Further, one can equip the D-chart lift a straightening which is compatible with the collar and scaffoldings.
The proof is completed in Theorem 6.31.
Proof of Theorem B.
From Theorem 4.7
this is a corollary of Theorem 3.49 and the discussion in Section 3.8.1.
∎
Theorem 4.8.
Let (MPSS)+(M^{\rm PSS})^{+} be the outer-collaring of the flow bimodule MPSSM^{\rm PSS} from (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+} to (TFloer)+(T^{\rm Floer})^{+}. Then (MPSS)+(M^{\rm PSS})^{+} has an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift
𝔇PSS=({Cx​pPSS=(𝒰x​pPSS,ℰx​pPSS,𝒮x​pPSS,ψx​pPSS)}x,p,{𝜾β​αPSS}α≤β),{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS}=\Big(\big\{C^{\rm PSS}_{xp}=({\mathcal{U}}^{\rm PSS}_{xp},{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm PSS}_{xp},{\mathcal{S}}^{\rm PSS}_{xp},\psi^{\rm PSS}_{xp})\big\}_{x,p},\big\{{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm PSS}\big\}_{\alpha\leq\beta}\Big),
with a collar structure
{θ^β​αcollar,PSS}α≤β,\{\widehat{\theta}_{\beta\alpha}^{{\rm collar},{\rm PSS}}\}_{\alpha\leq\beta},
and a scaffolding (Definition 3.28)
(ℱβ​αPSS,𝜽β​αPSS)α≤β\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm PSS},{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm PSS}\big)_{\alpha\leq\beta}
such that they are compatible, and they extend the given structures on 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer} and 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}. The same statement holds by reversing the role of (TFloer)+(T^{\rm Floer})^{+} to (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+} and replace PSS{\rm PSS} by SSP{\rm SSP}.
The proof is provided in Section 7. The following assertion is proved similarly as the arguments in Section 6.3.1.
Lemma 4.9.
Both 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS} and 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP} have a straightening extending the straightening on 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}, and they are compatible with the respective collar structure and scaffolding. ∎
As a consequence, Theorem 3.51 and the discussion in Section 3.8.1 define the Λ\Lambda-linear chain maps
ΨPSS:C​M∗​(f,Λ)→C​F∗​(H,Λ),\Psi^{\rm PSS}:CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)\to CF_{*}(H;\Lambda),
ΨSSP:C​F∗​(H,Λ)→C​M∗​(f,Λ).\Psi^{\rm SSP}:CF_{*}(H;\Lambda)\to CM_{*}(f;\Lambda).
Theorem 4.10.
Let (Mpearl)+(M^{\rm pearl})^{+} be the flow bimodule from (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+} to (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+} obtained from the outer-collaring of the pearly bimodule MpearlM^{\rm pearl}. Then (Mpearl)+(M^{\rm pearl})^{+} has an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift
𝔇pearl=({Cx​ypearl=(𝒰x​ypearl,ℰx​ypearl,𝒮x​ypearl,ψx​ypearl)}x,y,{𝜾β​αpearl}α≤β),{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm pearl}=\Big(\big\{C^{\rm pearl}_{xy}=({\mathcal{U}}^{\rm pearl}_{xy},{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm pearl}_{xy},{\mathcal{S}}^{\rm pearl}_{xy},\psi^{\rm pearl}_{xy})\big\}_{x,y},\big\{{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}^{\rm pearl}\big\}_{\alpha\leq\beta}\Big),
with a collar structure
{θ^β​αcollar,pearl}α≤β,\{\widehat{\theta}_{\beta\alpha}^{{\rm collar},{\rm pearl}}\}_{\alpha\leq\beta},
such that the induced derived orbifold presentation on each ℳ¯pearlx​y\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm pearl}_{xy} is single-layered (Definition 3.23), i.e., 𝔇pearl{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm pearl} has a trivial scaffolding. Moreover, the orientation structure, normally complex structure, and the collar structure extend the existing ones on 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}.
The proof is provided in Section 7. By the argument in Section 6.3.1 (but simpler because the scaffolding is absent in this case), we can find a straightening of 𝔇pearl{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm pearl} which is compatible with the collar structure. We fix it once for all.
Corollary 4.11.
The FOP countings associated to the oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift 𝔇pearl{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm pearl} and the chosen straightening thereon defines a chain map
Ψpearl:C​M∗​(f,Λ)→C​M∗​(f,Λ)\Psi^{\rm pearl}:CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)\to CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)
which is unitriangular, i.e.,
Ψpearl−Id∈(EndΛ​(C​M∗​(f,Λ)))+.\Psi^{\rm pearl}-{\rm Id}\in({\rm End}_{\Lambda}(CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)))_{+}.
In particular, the induced map on homology
Ψpearl:H∗​(M,Λ)→H∗​(M,Λ)\Psi^{\rm pearl}:H_{*}(M;\Lambda)\to H_{*}(M;\Lambda)
is invertible.
Proof.
The construction of Ψpearl\Psi^{\rm pearl} follows from Theorem 3.51 and discussions in Section 3.8. To prove that Ψpearl\Psi^{\rm pearl} is unitriangular, observe that for a pair of capped orbits x=(x¯,a)x=(\underline{\smash{x}},a) and y=(y¯,b)y=(\underline{\smash{y}},b), the moduli space ℳ¯x​ypearl\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\rm pearl}_{xy} is nonempty only if ω⁡(b−a)≥0\omega(b-a)\geq 0. Moreover, when ω⁡(b−a)=0\omega(b-a)=0, the only nonempty moduli space contributing to the counting in Ψpearl​(x)\Psi^{\rm pearl}(x) is the moduli space of parametrized gradient flow lines from xx to itself, which is a single point. It implies that the incidence coefficient nx​xpearl=1n^{\rm pearl}_{xx}=1. As a consequence, the statement is proved.
∎
4.6. The homotopy
In this subsection, we describe how to interpolate between the chain maps ΨSSP∘ΨPSS\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS} and Ψpearl\Psi^{\rm pearl} and prove Theorem C.
We choose a 3-parameter family of Hamiltonians parametrized by t∈S1t\in S^{1}, s∈ℝs\in{\mathbb{R}}, and τ∈(−1,1]\tau\in(-1,1] which satisfy the following conditions.
(1)
For each τ\tau, Hτ,s,thmtpH_{\tau,s,t}^{\rm hmtp} is equal to CPSSC^{\rm PSS} for ss near −∞-\infty and is equal to CSSPC^{\rm SSP} for ss near +∞+\infty.
(2)
For all τ,s,t,x\tau,s,t,x there holds
∂Hτ,s,thmtp∂s​(x)≥0.\frac{\partial H_{\tau,s,t}^{\rm hmtp}}{\partial s}(x)\geq 0.
(3)
As τ→−1\tau\to-1, Hτ,s,thmtpH_{\tau,s,t}^{\rm hmtp} converges to the concatenation of Hs,tPSSH_{s,t}^{\rm PSS} and Hs,tSSPH_{s,t}^{\rm SSP}.
(4)
For τ\tau near +1+1, Hτ,s,thmtpH_{\tau,s,t}^{\rm hmtp} does not depend on t∈S1t\in S^{1} and x∈Mx\in M. In particular, the associated Hamiltonian vector field is zero.
Then consider pairs (τ,u)(\tau,u) where τ∈(−1,1]\tau\in(-1,1] and u:Θ→Mu:\Theta\to M solving the equation
∂u∂s+J⁡(∂u∂t−XHτ,s,thmtp​(u))=0.\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+J\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-X_{H_{\tau,s,t}^{\rm hmtp}}(u)\right)=0.
(4.11)
The energy of a solution is defined by
Ehmtp​(τ,u):=‖∂su‖L2​(Θ)2.E^{\rm hmtp}(\tau,u):=\left\|\partial_{s}u\right\|_{L^{2}(\Theta)}^{2}.
As the Hamiltonian perturbation vanishes near s=±∞s=\pm\infty, any finite energy solution converges at the infinities. Hence one can use an element of Π\Pi to label solutions. Given a∈Πa\in\Pi, let
ℳ(a)hmtp∙⁣∙{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\bullet\bullet}^{\rm hmtp}(a)
to be the set of solutions to (4.11) whose Π\Pi-class is aa. Moreover, by intersecting with (un)stable manifolds of Morse critical points, we can define
ℳ(a)hmtpx¯​y¯{\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm hmtp}(a)
to be the set of such solutions whose limit at −∞-\infty resp. +∞+\infty lies in the unstable resp. stable manifold of x¯\underline{\smash{x}} resp. y¯\underline{\smash{y}}. Lastly, given x=(a,x¯),y=(b,y¯)∈𝒫Morsex=(a,\underline{\smash{x}}),y=(b,\underline{\smash{y}})\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}, define
ℳ:=hmtpx​yℳ(b−a)hmtpx¯​y¯.{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}:={\mathcal{M}}{}_{\underline{\smash{x}}\underline{\smash{y}}}^{\rm hmtp}(b-a).
We compactify this space by adding configurations with sphere bubbles, broken Morse trajectories, and (when τ→−1\tau\to-1) breakings at 1-periodic orbits of HH. Denote the compactification by
ℳ¯x​yhmtp.\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}.
We call such moduli spaces the homotopy moduli spaces.
4.6.1. Stratifications on homotopy moduli spaces
The system of the homotopy moduli spaces can be put in a more abstract narrative to include bimodule compositions and bimodule homotopies. However, as its role in the proof of the Arnold conjecture is technical rather than conceptual, we refrain from introducing such frameworks. Here we give a more concrete description of the stratifications on these moduli spaces. Fix x,y∈𝒫Morsex,y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}. We define a poset
𝔸x​yhmtp:=𝔸x​yPSS+SSP⊔𝔸̊x​yhmtp⊔𝔸x​ypearl.{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}:={\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{{\rm PSS}+{\rm SSP}}\sqcup\mathring{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}\sqcup{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm pearl}.
Here as sets
𝔸̊x​yhmtp=𝔸x​ypearl\mathring{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}={\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm pearl}
while as sets
𝔸x​yPSS+SSP:={xx1⋯xkp1⋯psyl⋯y1y|xx1⋯xkp1⋯ps∈𝔸x​psPSS,psyl⋯y1y∈𝔸ps​ySSP}.{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{{\rm PSS}+{\rm SSP}}:=\Big\{xx_{1}\cdots x_{k}p_{1}\cdots p_{s}y_{l}\cdots y_{1}y\ |\ xx_{1}\cdots x_{k}p_{1}\cdots p_{s}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xp_{s}}^{\rm PSS},\ p_{s}y_{l}\cdots y_{1}y\in{\mathbb{A}}_{p_{s}y}^{\rm SSP}\Big\}.
Then one can see that the moduli space ℳ¯x​yhmtp\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp} has a natural stratification whose strata are indexed by the set 𝔸x​yhmtp{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}. This then naturally induces a partial order on 𝔸x​yhmtp{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp} according to the breaking of Morse or Floer trajectories as well as whether the parameter τ\tau hits −1-1 or 11. We use κ,ν\kappa,\nu etc. instead of α,β\alpha,\beta to denote elements of 𝔸x​yhmtp{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}. Then it is straightforward to check that 𝔸x​yhmtp{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp} is a homogeneous poset with a unique maximal element and depth function being
depth⁡(κ)={number​of​breakings,κ∈𝔸̊x​yhmtp,number​of​breakings+1,κ∈𝔸x​ypearl,number​of​breakings,κ∈𝔸x​yPSS+SSP.{\rm depth}(\kappa)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\rm number\ of\ breakings},&\ \kappa\in\mathring{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp},\\
{\rm number\ of\ breakings}+1,&\ \kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm pearl},\\
{\rm number\ of\ breakings},&\ \kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{{\rm PSS}+{\rm SSP}}.\end{array}\right.
The types of codimension one (i.e., strata on which the depth function takes value 11) degenerations of configurations in the homotopy moduli spaces can be described by the following types of stratified embeddings.

The above maps satisfy a list of obvious associativity properties, which we do not describe explicitly here.
4.6.2. Outer collaring
We can also apply the general outer-collaring construction on the homotopy moduli spaces so that it naturally extends the outer-collaring of the Floer, Morse, PSS, and SPP moduli spaces. The same type of structure maps as listed in (4.12)—(4.15) are still present and satisfy the same associativity properties.
4.6.3. Derived orbifold lift
Definition 4.12.
Assume the following objects are given.
(1)
A derived orbifold lift 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer} of the outer-collared Hamiltonian Floer flow category (TFloer)+(T^{\rm Floer})^{+}. (Remember there is also the trivial derived orbifold lift 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse} of the outer-collared Morse flow category (TMorse)+(T^{\rm Morse})^{+}).
(2)
A derived orbifold lift 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS} resp. 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP} of the outer-collared PSS resp. SSP bimodule which extends 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer} and 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}.
(3)
A derived orbifold lift 𝔇pearl{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm pearl} of the outer-collared pearly bimodule (Mpearl)+(M^{\rm pearl})^{+}.
Then a derived orbifold lift of the system of the outer-collared homotopy moduli spaces (ℳ¯)hmtpx​y+(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp})^{+} consists of the following objects.
(1)
For each x,yx,y, an 𝔸x​yhmtp{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}-stratified derived orbifold chart Cx​yhmtpC_{xy}^{\rm hmtp} of (ℳ¯)hmtpx​y+(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp})^{+}.
(2)
Stratified chart embeddings
