(4.19)
These objects need to satisfy the following conditions.
(1)
The obvious associativity relations.
(2)
For each κ∈𝔸x​yhmtp\kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}, there is a product chart CκhmtpC_{\kappa}^{\rm hmtp} obtained from taking corresponding product of certain Morse, Floer, PSS, SSP, pearly, and/or homotopy derived orbifold charts. The above associativity relations induce chart embeddings
𝜾ν​κhmtp:Cκhmtp→∂κCνhmtp​∀κ≤ν.{\bm{\iota}}_{\nu\kappa}^{\rm hmtp}:C_{\kappa}^{\rm hmtp}\to\partial^{\kappa}C_{\nu}^{\rm hmtp}\ \forall\kappa\leq\nu.
Then ((Cκhmtp)κ∈𝔸x​yhmtp,(𝜾ν​κ)κ≤ν)((C_{\kappa}^{\rm hmtp})_{\kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}},({\bm{\iota}}_{\nu\kappa})_{\kappa\leq\nu}) form a derived orbifold presentation of the space (ℳ¯)hmtpx​y+(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp})^{+}.
(3)
When κ∉∂−𝔸x​yhmtp\kappa\notin\partial^{-}{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}, the chart embedding 𝜾ν​κhmtp{\bm{\iota}}_{\nu\kappa}^{\rm hmtp} is an open embedding.
(4)
The obvious strict Π\Pi-equivariance condition.
We also need to discuss the additional structures (collar, scaffolding, and straightening) on the derived orbifold lift of the system of homotopy moduli spaces.
Definition 4.13.
In addition to the assumption of Definition 4.12, assume the following objects are given.
(1)
A compatible package of additional structures on 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}. Remember that there is also a package of additional structures on 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse} which essentially contains only the collar structure.
(2)
A compatible package of additional structures on 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS} resp. 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP} which extend the ones on 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer} and 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}.
(3)
A compatible package of additional structures on 𝔇pearl{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm pearl} which extends the package on 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}.
Let 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} be a derived orbifold lift of the system of homotopy moduli spaces which extends all given derived orbifold lifts.
(1)
A collar structure resp. straightening on 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} is a collection of collar structures resp. straightenings on the derived orbifold charts Cx​yhmtpC_{xy}^{\rm hmtp} satisfying the following condition. Fix x,yx,y. For each κ∈𝔸x​yhmtp\kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}, the product of collar structures resp. straightenings on all derived orbifold chart factors defines a collar structure resp. straightening on the product chart CκhmtpC_{\kappa}^{\rm hmtp}. Then the collection of collar structures resp. straightenings for all κ∈𝔸x​yhmtp\kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp} is a collar structure resp. straightening of the derived orbifold presentation of (ℳ¯)hmtpx​y+(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp})^{+}.
(2)
A scaffolding on 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} consists of a collection of scaffoldings
((ℱν​κ,𝜽ν​κ)κ≤ν)x,y∈𝒫Morse\Big(\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\nu\kappa},{\bm{\theta}}_{\nu\kappa}\big)_{\kappa\leq\nu}\Big)_{x,y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}}
on the collection of derived orbifold presentations ((Cκhmtp)κ∈𝔸x​yhmtp,(𝜾κ≤ν))((C_{\kappa}^{\rm hmtp})_{\kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}},({\bm{\iota}}_{\kappa\leq\nu})) which satisfy the following conditions.
(a)
If ν∈∂−𝔸x​yhmtp\nu\in\partial^{-}{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp} (i.e. a stratum in the τ=−1\tau=-1 slice of the moduli, i.e., a stratum of configurations with breakings at 1-periodic orbits of HH), then (ℱν​κ,𝜽ν​κ)({\mathcal{F}}_{\nu\kappa},{\bm{\theta}}_{\nu\kappa}) is the product from corresponding difference bundles and stabilization maps on 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}, 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS}, 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP}, and/or the trivial one on 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}.
(b)
If κ∉∂−𝔸x​yhmtp\kappa\notin\partial^{-}{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}, then ℱν​κ=0{\mathcal{F}}_{\nu\kappa}=0.
Lastly we discuss orientations and normal complex structures on a system of derived orbifold lifts on homotopy moduli spaces.
Definition 4.14.
Given a derived orbifold lift 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp}, and a collar structure compatible with a scaffolding ((ℱν​κ,𝜽ν​κ)κ≤ν)x,y∈𝒫Morse\Big(\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\nu\kappa},{\bm{\theta}}_{\nu\kappa}\big)_{\kappa\leq\nu}\Big)_{x,y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Morse}}, assume that 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}, 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS}, 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP}, 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse} and their scaffoldings and outer-collarings are endowed with a normal complex structure. Then a normal complex structure on 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} with the given scaffolding and outer-collaring consists of
(1)
A normal complex structure on all the underlying derived orbifold charts of (Cκhmtp)κ∈𝔸x​yhmtp(C_{\kappa}^{\rm hmtp})_{\kappa\in{\mathbb{A}}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}} such that the embeddings (𝜾κ≤ν)({\bm{\iota}}_{\kappa\leq\nu}) all intertwine with the normal complex structures.
(2)
A complex structure on the vector bundles ℱν​κ{\mathcal{F}}_{\nu\kappa} such that the embeddings underlying 𝜽ν​κ{\bm{\theta}}_{\nu\kappa} all respects the induced normal complex structures.
(3)
The structural maps from the outer-collaring and the compatibility equations with the scaffolding intertwine with the normal complex structure as in Definition 3.36.
(4)
The strict Π\Pi-action preserves the normal complex structures.
Definition 4.15.
Let 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}, 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS}, 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP}, 𝔇Morse,𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse},{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} be as in Definition 4.14 with the respective collared structure, scaffolding, and normal complex structure. Moreover, assume that each of 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}, 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS}, 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP}, 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse} is equipped with an orientation in the sense of Definition 3.43 and Definition 3.47. Then an orientation on the given normally complex lift of 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} consists of
(1)
For all x,yx,y, an isomorphism of orientation lines
𝔬Cx​yhmtp→∼𝔬x∨⊗𝔬y.\mathfrak{o}_{C_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{o}_{x}^{\vee}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{y}.
(4.20)
(2)
Isomorphisms of orientation lines
𝔬Cx​ypearl→∼𝔬∂+Cx​yhmtp,\displaystyle{\mathfrak{o}}_{C_{xy}^{\rm pearl}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathfrak{o}}_{\partial^{+}C^{\rm hmtp}_{xy}},
(4.21)
𝔬Cx​pPSS⊗𝔬Cp​ySSP→∼𝔬∂x​p​yCx​yhmtp,\displaystyle{\mathfrak{o}}_{C_{xp}^{\rm PSS}}\otimes{\mathfrak{o}}_{C_{py}^{\rm SSP}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathfrak{o}}_{\partial^{xpy}C_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}},
𝔬Cx​x′Morse⊗𝔬Cx′​yhmtp→∼𝔬∂x​x′​yCx​yhmtp,\displaystyle{\mathfrak{o}}_{C_{xx^{\prime}}^{\rm Morse}}\otimes{\mathfrak{o}}_{C_{x^{\prime}y}^{\rm hmtp}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathfrak{o}}_{\partial^{xx^{\prime}y}C_{xy}^{\rm hmtp}},
𝔬Cx​y′hmtp⊗𝔬y′​yMorse→∼𝔬∂x​y′​yCx​yhmtp.\displaystyle{\mathfrak{o}}_{C_{xy^{\prime}}^{\rm hmtp}}\otimes{\mathfrak{o}}_{y^{\prime}y}^{\rm Morse}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathfrak{o}}_{\partial^{xy^{\prime}y}C^{\rm hmtp}_{xy}}.
Here we omit the cancellation of the orientation lines of the complex scaffolding vector bundles and its dual.
(3)
The above isomorphisms are preserved by the Π\Pi-action.
Theorem 4.16.
There is a Λ\Lambda-linear chain homotopy
Ψhmtp:C​M∗​(f,Λ)→C​M∗−1​(f,Λ)\Psi^{\rm hmtp}:CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)\to CM_{*-1}(f;\Lambda)
such that
Ψpearl−ΨSSP∘ΨPSS=dMorse∘Ψhmtp−Ψhmtp∘dMorse.\Psi^{\rm pearl}-\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}=d^{\rm Morse}\circ\Psi^{\rm hmtp}-\Psi^{\rm hmtp}\circ d^{\rm Morse}.
(4.22)
In particular, there holds
ΨSSP∘ΨPSS=Ψpearl\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}=\Psi^{\rm pearl}
(4.23)
as graded linear maps on H∗​(M,Λ)H_{*}(M;\Lambda).
Proof.
Using the description presented in Section 7, there exists a compatible oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} of the outer-collared homotopy moduli spaces (ℳ¯)hmtpx​y+(\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{\rm hmtp})^{+}, with collared structure and scaffolding. Then the relative version of the arguments in Section 6.3.1 can be used to equip 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} with a straightening compatible with the given straightenings on 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}, 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS}, 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP}, 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}. Therefore, a relative version of Theorem 3.49 allows us to find a compatible system of strongly transverse FOP perturbations of the derived orbifold charts in 𝔇hmtp{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm hmtp} extending the FOP perturbations on 𝔇Floer{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Floer}, 𝔇PSS{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm PSS}, 𝔇SSP{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm SSP}, 𝔇Morse{\mathfrak{D}}^{\rm Morse}. Using the sign read off from (4.20), the map Ψhmtp\Psi^{\rm hmtp} is defined by the algebraic count of zeroes of the FOP sections over moduli spaces of virtual dimension 00. The algebraic relation (4.22) is again read off from the boundary of moduli spaces of virtural dimension 11, using the coherence of orientations (4.21). The Λ\Lambda-linearity is a result of the strict Π\Pi-action as before.
∎
Proof of Theorem C.
This is a direct consequence of Corollary 4.11 and Equation (4.23).
∎
4.7. Proof of the integral Arnold conjecture
Lemma 4.17.
(cf. [HM06, Appendix])
The Novikov ring Λ\Lambda is a principal ideal domain (PID).
Proof.
By definition, we need to show that each ideal is a principal ideal, i.e., generated by a single element. Let I⊆ΛI\subseteq\Lambda be an ideal. Denote by a0∈ℤ+a_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+} the greatest common divisor of the leading coefficients of all elements of II. Then there exists 𝔵0∈I{\mathfrak{x}}_{0}\in I of the form 𝔵0=a0+𝔶0{\mathfrak{x}}_{0}=a_{0}+{\mathfrak{y}}_{0} with 𝔶0∈Λ+{\mathfrak{y}}_{0}\in\Lambda_{+}. We claim that
I=(𝔵0).I=({\mathfrak{x}}_{0}).
Indeed, given any 𝔵∈I{\mathfrak{x}}\in I, a0a_{0} divides the leading order term of 𝔵{\mathfrak{x}}. We can inductively find an element 𝔮∈Λ{\mathfrak{q}}\in\Lambda such that 𝔵=𝔮​𝔵0{\mathfrak{x}}={\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{x}}_{0}. Hence I=(𝔵0)I=({\mathfrak{x}}_{0}) and Λ\Lambda is a PID.
∎
Recall the standard structure results about finitely-generated modules over principal ideal domains. If RR is a principal ideal domain and MM is a finitely-generated RR-module, then
M≅F⊕TM\cong F\oplus T
where FF is a free RR-module of finite rank and TT is a torsion RR-module. Moreover,
(1)
There exist primes p1,…,pk∈Rp_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in R and positive integers m1,…,mkm_{1},\ldots,m_{k} such that
T≅R/(p1m1)⊕⋯⊕R/(pkmk).T\cong R/(p_{1}^{m_{1}})\oplus\cdots\oplus R/(p_{k}^{m_{k}}).
The prime powers pimip_{i}^{m_{i}} are called the elementary divisors.
(2)
There exist nonzero nonunit elements r1,…,rlr_{1},\ldots,r_{l} of RR such that r1|r2​|⋯|​rlr_{1}|r_{2}|\cdots|r_{l} and
T≅R/(r1)⊕⋯⊕R/(rl).T\cong R/(r_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus R/(r_{l}).
The elements rir_{i} are called the invariant factors.
Lemma 4.18.
Let RR be a PID. Let ZZ be a free RR-module of finite rank and S⊆ZS\subseteq Z be a submodule. Suppose
Z/S≅F⊕R/(a1)⊕⋯⊕R/(ak)Z/S\cong F\oplus R/(a_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus R/(a_{k})
where FF is a free RR-module and a1|a2​|⋯|​aka_{1}|a_{2}|\cdots|a_{k} are invariant factors. Then
rank​Z≥rank​F+k{\rm rank}Z\geq{\rm rank}F+k
and
rank​S≥k.{\rm rank}S\geq k.
Proof.
By tensoring with the quotient field of RR, we can kill all torsions. Then it follows that rank​Z−rank​S=rank​F{\rm rank}Z-{\rm rank}S={\rm rank}F. Hence it suffices to prove the first lower bound. Choose a prime divisor pp of a1a_{1}. By tensoring with Rp:=R/(p)R_{p}:=R/(p), we can see that
Z⊗RRp/S⊗RRp≅Rprank​F+k.Z\otimes_{R}R_{p}/S\otimes_{R}R_{p}\cong R_{p}^{{\rm rank}F+k}.
Therefore,
rank​Z=dimRp​Z⊗RRp≥rank​F+k.∎{\rm rank}Z={\rm dim}_{R_{p}}Z\otimes_{R}R_{p}\geq{\rm rank}F+k.\qed
Lemma 4.19.
Let MM be a finitely-generated RR-module and N⊆MN\subseteq M be a submodule. Then
(1)
rank​N≤rank​M{\rm rank}N\leq{\rm rank}M.
(2)
For each prime p∈Rp\in R, the number of elementary divisors of NN which are powers of pp is less than or equal to the number of elementary divisors of MM which are powers of pp.
(3)
The number of invariant factors of NN is not greater than the number of invariant factors of MM.
Proof.
By the structure theorem of finitely-generated modules over PID, we can write
M≅F⊕⨁i=1kR/(pimi),\displaystyle\ M\cong F\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}R/(p_{i}^{m_{i}}),\
N≅F′⊕⨁j=1lR/(qjnj)\displaystyle\ N\cong F^{\prime}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{l}R/(q_{j}^{n_{j}})
where pip_{i}, qjq_{j} are primes of RR and mi,nj≥1m_{i},n_{j}\geq 1. By tensoring the quotient field of RR, we can see that rank​F′≤rank​F{\rm rank}F^{\prime}\leq{\rm rank}F. On the other hand, let M⁡(p)⊆MM(p)\subseteq M be the direct sum of pp-torsions. Then the inclusion N↪MN\hookrightarrow M induces injections N⁡(p)↪M⁡(p)N(p)\hookrightarrow M(p) for each prime pp. Therefore, the collection of qjq_{j}’s is a subset of the collection of pip_{i}’s. Then for each prime pp, denote
M⁡[p]:={x∈M|p​x=0}M[p]:=\{x\in M\ |\ px=0\}
which is a submodule. Then the inclusion N↪MN\hookrightarrow M induces an injection N⁡[p]↪M⁡[p]N[p]\hookrightarrow M[p]. Notice that R/(p)R/(p) is a field and M⁡[p]M[p] is a R/(p)R/(p)-vector space. Clearly N⁡[p]N[p] is a subspace. Moreover, recall that
(R/(pm))​[p]≅R/(p).(R/(p^{m}))[p]\cong R/(p).
Hence it follows that the number of pp-elementary divisors of NN is no greater than the number of pp-elementary divisors of MM. The relation between their numbers of invariant factors follows immediately.
∎
Proof of Theorem A.
Now consider the two ℤ/2​N{\mathbb{Z}}/2N-graded Λ\Lambda-modules, H​F∗​(H,Λ)HF_{*}(H;\Lambda) and H∗​(M,Λ)H_{*}(M;\Lambda). Because ΨSSP∘ΨPSS=Ψpearl=I​d+O⁡(T)\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}=\Psi^{\rm pearl}=Id+O(T), so for each i∈ℤ/2​Ni\in{\mathbb{Z}}/{2N}, the PSS map embeds Hi​(M,Λ)H_{i}(M;\Lambda) as a submodule of H​Fi​(H,Λ)HF_{i}(H;\Lambda). Therefore, for the free part, one has the rank inequality
rank​H​Fi​(H,Λ)≥rank​H∗​(M,Λ),i∈ℤ/2​N.{\rm rank}HF_{i}(H;\Lambda)\geq{\rm rank}H_{*}(M;\Lambda),\ i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N.
As of torsion, Lemma 4.19 implies the inequality between number of invariant factors
τiFloer≥τiMorse,i∈ℤ/2​N\tau_{i}^{\rm Floer}\geq\tau_{i}^{\rm Morse},\ i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N
On the other hand, we know that
H​Fi​(H,Λ)=ker​diFloerimage​di+1FloerHF_{i}(H;\Lambda)=\frac{{\rm ker}d_{i}^{\rm Floer}}{{\rm image}d_{i+1}^{\rm Floer}}
where both the numerator and denominator are free Λ\Lambda-modules. Hence by Lemma 4.18, one has
rank⁡(ker​diFloer)≥rank​Hi​(M,Λ)+τiMorse{\rm rank}({\rm ker}d_{i}^{\rm Floer})\geq{\rm rank}H_{i}(M;\Lambda)+\tau_{i}^{\rm Morse}
and
rank⁡(image​di+1Floer)≥τiMorse.{\rm rank}({\rm image}d_{i+1}^{\rm Floer})\geq\tau_{i}^{\rm Morse}.
Therefore, let #​Per​H\#{\rm Per}H be the number of 11-periodic orbits of HH, we have
#​Per​H=rank​C​F∗​(H,Λ)=∑i∈ℤ/2​Nrank​C​Fi​(H,Λ)=∑i∈ℤ/2​N(rank⁡(ker​diFloer)+rank⁡(image​diFloer))≥∑i∈ℤ/2​Nrank​Hi​(M,Λ)+2​∑i∈ℤ/2​NτiMorse=rank​H∗​(M,ℚ)+2​∑i∈ℤ/2​Nτi(2​N)​(M).\#{\rm Per}H={\rm rank}CF_{*}(H;\Lambda)=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/{2N}}{\rm rank}CF_{i}(H;\Lambda)\\
=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/{2N}}\big({\rm rank}({\rm ker}d_{i}^{\rm Floer})+{\rm rank}({\rm image}d_{i}^{\rm Floer})\big)\\
\geq\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/{2N}}{\rm rank}H_{i}(M;\Lambda)+2\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/{2N}}\tau_{i}^{\rm Morse}\\
=\text{rank}H_{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}})+2\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N}\tau_{i}^{(2N)}(M).
∎
5. Global Kuranishi charts on Floer moduli spaces
In this section we provide the details for the global Kuranishi chart construction of moduli spaces of Floer trajectories. The construction is primarily inspired by the construction by Abouzaid–McLean–Smith [AMS21] of the global chart on a single moduli space of genus zero stable maps. The main effort, however, is to generalize their construction to the case of the Hamiltonian Floer flow categories, where global charts must be constructed consistently for infinitely many moduli spaces. Certain care must be taken in order to have the expected properties. This and the next section serve as the construction of a derived orbifold lift of the Hamiltonian Floer flow category: this section deals with the topological construction while Section 6 deals with the smoothing.
5.1. Basic notions and the main theorem about global charts
To state the main theorem of this section, we introduce an alternative version of charts. Because our construction of FOP perturbations is carried over orbifolds instead of smooth manifolds with an almost-free action of a compact Lie group, we refrain to give a general treatment of “global charts” beyond the concrete geometric setting in this paper.
5.1.1. Kuranishi charts
We slightly generalize the usual notion of Kuranishi charts.
Definition 5.1.
Let 𝔸{\mathbb{A}} be a countable homogeneous poset and XX be an 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-space.
(1)
An 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-stratified (topological) Kuranishi chart (K-chart for short) on XX is a quintuple (G,V,E,S,ψ)(G,V,E,S,\psi) where GG is a compact Lie group, VV is an 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-manifold with a continuous GG-action, E→VE\to V is a GG-equivariant vector bundle, S:V→ES:V\to E is a GG-equivariant section, and ψ:S−1​(0)/G→X\psi:S^{-1}(0)/G\to X is a homeomorphism. We require the following condition: the stabilizer of each point x∈Vx\in V is finite, i.e., the GG-action on VV is almost free.
(2)
A K-chart (G,V,E,S,ψ)(G,V,E,S,\psi) is said to be smooth if VV is a smooth 𝔸{\mathbb{A}}-manifold, the GG-action is smooth, and E→VE\to V is a smooth equivariant vector bundle (we do not impose any smoothness condition on SS).
We often omit the map ψ\psi in the notation because in the context its meaning will always be clear.
Remark 5.2.
Historically, there are different notions of Kuranishi charts which could be defined either via orbifolds or via equivariant objects. In this paper, the orbifold version will be labelled as “derived” and the name “Kuranishi” is reserved for the equivariant version while allowing actions by general compact Lie groups. We also use the prefixes “D-” and “K-” to denote these two versions.
Definition 5.3.
Let K=(G,V,E,S)K=(G,V,E,S) be a K-chart and πF:F→V\pi_{F}:F\to V be a GG-equivariant vector bundle. The stabilization of KK by FF, denoted by StabF​(K){\rm Stab}_{F}(K), is the K-chart
StabF​(K)=(G,F,πF∗​E⊕πF∗​F,πF∗​S⊕τF){\rm Stab}_{F}(K)=(G,F,\pi_{F}^{*}E\oplus\pi_{F}^{*}F,\pi_{F}^{*}S\oplus\tau_{F})
where τF:F→πF∗​F\tau_{F}:F\to\pi_{F}^{*}F is the tautological section.
5.1.2. Change of groups
For K-charts, the notions of open embedding, germ equivalence, and product are almost identical to the case of derived orbifold charts after imposing the equivariance condition with respect to the Lie group action. There are some care to be taken of when the groups of symmetry change. To this end, we introduce the operation of enlarging the symmetry group.
Definition 5.4.
Let K=(G,V,E,S)K=(G,V,E,S) be a K-chart and let G↪G′G\hookrightarrow G^{\prime} be a Lie group embedding. Define the change of group, or the G′G^{\prime}-equivariantization, of KK, to be
G′×GK:=(G′,G′×GV,G′×GE,S′)G^{\prime}\times_{G}K:=(G^{\prime},G^{\prime}\times_{G}V,G^{\prime}\times_{G}E,S^{\prime})
where G′G^{\prime} acts on the bundle G′×GE→G′×GVG^{\prime}\times_{G}E\to G^{\prime}\times_{G}V in the obvious way and
S′​([g′,x])=[g′,S⁡(x)].S^{\prime}([g^{\prime},x])=[g^{\prime},S(x)].
In the rest of the paper, we also use the notations
G′​(K):=G′×GK:=(G′,G′​(V),G′​(E),G′​(S)):=(G′,G′×GV,G′×GE,S′).G^{\prime}(K):=G^{\prime}\times_{G}K:=(G^{\prime},G^{\prime}(V),G^{\prime}(E),G^{\prime}(S)):=(G^{\prime},G^{\prime}\times_{G}V,G^{\prime}\times_{G}E,S^{\prime}).
Lemma 5.5.
Suppose G↪G′G\hookrightarrow G^{\prime} is a Lie group embedding and let VV be a GG-space. If 𝐖{\bm{W}} is a G′G^{\prime}-representation and E→VE\to V is the trivial GG-bundle V×𝐖V\times{\bm{W}} where we view 𝐖{\bm{W}} as a GG-representation, then G′​(E)G^{\prime}(E) is isomorphic to the trivial G′G^{\prime}-bundle over G′​(V)=G′×GVG^{\prime}(V)=G^{\prime}\times_{G}V with fiber 𝐖{\bm{W}}.
Proof.
Define the map ζ:G′​(E)=G′×G(V×𝑾)→(G′×GV)×𝑾\zeta:G^{\prime}(E)=G^{\prime}\times_{G}(V\times{\bm{W}})\to(G^{\prime}\times_{G}V)\times{\bm{W}} by
ζ⁡([g′,(v,w)])=([g′,v],g′​w).\zeta([g^{\prime},(v,w)])=([g^{\prime},v],g^{\prime}w).
Notice that
ζ⁡([g′​g,(v,w)])=([g′​g,v],g′​g​w)=([g′,g​v],g′​(g​w))=ζ⁡([g′,(g​v,g​w)])=ζ⁡([g′,g⁡(v,w)]).\zeta([g^{\prime}g,(v,w)])=([g^{\prime}g,v],g^{\prime}gw)=([g^{\prime},gv],g^{\prime}(gw))=\zeta([g^{\prime},(gv,gw)])=\zeta([g^{\prime},g(v,w)]).
Hence ζ\zeta is well-defined. It is also straightforward to check that ζ\zeta is a map of G′G^{\prime}-equivariant vector bundles over G′×GVG^{\prime}\times_{G}V and is an isomorphism.
∎
5.1.3. Chart embeddings
Because of the presence of compact Lie group, the definition of chart embeddings of K-charts also differs slightly from the notion of chart embeddings of derived orbifold charts.
Definition 5.6.
Let K1=(G1,V1,E1,S1,ψ1)K_{1}=(G_{1},V_{1},E_{1},S_{1},\psi_{1}) and K2=(G2,V2,E2,S2,ψ2)K_{2}=(G_{2},V_{2},E_{2},S_{2},\psi_{2}) be two topological K-charts.
(1)
A weak K-chart embedding from K1K_{1} to K2K_{2}, denoted by
𝜾21:K1⇀K2,{\bm{\iota}}_{21}:K_{1}\rightharpoonup K_{2},
consists of a group embedding G1↪G2G_{1}\hookrightarrow G_{2},1010
10
In the concrete situations of this paper, the group embeddings are always fixed by the geometric data, and the embeddings are induced from inclusions an equivairant topological embedding ι21:G2​(V1)↪V2\iota_{21}:G_{2}(V_{1})\hookrightarrow V_{2} which has a G2G_{2}-invariant neighborhood equivariantly homeomorphic to a G2G_{2}-equivariant vector bundle1111
11
This seemingly redundant requirement is necessary because in the topological category submanifolds do not necessarily have vector bundle neighborhoods., and an equivariant vector bundle embedding ι^21:G2​(E1)↪E2\widehat{\iota}_{21}:G_{2}(E_{1})\hookrightarrow E_{2} covering ι21\iota_{21}. (Due to a small defect of the global chart construction we will use, a weak K-chart embedding does not necessarily intertwine with the Kuranishi sections. This explains the adjective “weak” in the terminology.)

A weak K-chart embedding is called a K-chart embedding if the following diagrams commute.

(3)
A (weak) K-chart embedding is called a (weak) open embedding if ι21\iota_{21} is a homeomorphism onto an open subset and ι^21\widehat{\iota}_{21} is a bundle isomorphism.
Notice that (weak) K-chart embeddings can be composed in an obvious way.
5.1.4. Notations
Notation 5.7.
(1)
For each positive integer dd, let 𝔸d{\mathbb{A}}_{d} be the poset of all ordered partitions of dd, i.e.,
𝔸d:={(d0,…,dl)|d0+⋯+dl=d,di∈ℤ>0}.{\mathbb{A}}_{d}:=\big\{(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\ |\ d_{0}+\cdots+d_{l}=d,\ d_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}\big\}.
The partial order is induced from refinements of partitions. It is a homogeneous poset with a unique maximal element (d)(d) and depth function depth⁡(d0,…,dl)=l{\rm depth}(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})=l. Moreover, there are natural inclusions
𝔸d0×⋯×𝔸dl→∂(d0,…,dl)𝔸d0+⋯+dl{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{l}}\to\partial^{(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})}{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{0}+\cdots+d_{l}}
which satisfy the obvious associativity relation.

For each pair p,q∈𝒫Floerp,q\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer}, define
dp​q:=𝒜Floer​(q)−𝒜Floer​(p).d_{pq}:={\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(q)-{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(p).
(5.1)
By the integrality assumption of the symplectic action (see Hypothesis 4.1), dp​qd_{pq} is an integer. In practice we only consider the situation when dp​q≥0d_{pq}\geq 0. Moreover, there is a natural poset map
δ:𝔸p​qFloer→𝔸dp​qα=pr1⋯rlq↦δ⁡(α)=(dp​r1,…,drl​q)\begin{array}[]{rcl}\delta:{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}&\to&{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{pq}}\\

such that the following diagram commutes.

for all d≥1d\geq 1. Here P​U​(d+1)PU(d+1) acts on ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d} in the standard way. The identification with U⁡(d)U(d) is given by
U⁡(d)∋g′↦[100g′]∈P​U​(d+1).U(d)\ni g^{\prime}\mapsto\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\

Denote
Gp​q:=Gdp​q​∀p,q∈𝒫Floer,𝒜Floer​(p)<𝒜Floer​(q).G_{pq}:=G_{d_{pq}}\ \forall p,q\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer},{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(p)<{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(q).
(4)
For each δ=(d0,…,dl)∈𝔸d\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}, there is a group embedding
Gδ:=Gd0×⋯×Gdl↪GdG_{\delta}:=G_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times G_{d_{l}}\hookrightarrow G_{d}

defined by
([100g0′],…,[100gl′])↦[10⋯00g0′⋯00⋯⋱000⋯gl′].\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\

(5)
For each α=pr1⋯rl∈𝔸p​qFloer\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}, we also denote
Gα:=Gpr1⋯rlq:=Gp​r1×⋯×Grl​q=Gδ⁡(α)G_{\alpha}:=G_{pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q}:=G_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times G_{r_{l}q}=G_{\delta(\alpha)}

5.1.5. The main statement
To simplify the notations, through out this section, we use ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq} to denote the moduli space ℳ¯p​qFloer\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}.
The following definition is the counterpart of Definition 3.22, 3.24 for K-charts.
Definition 5.8.
A weak K-chart presentation (with the system of groups {Gα}α∈𝔸p​qFloer\{G_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}}) of the 𝔸p​qFloer{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}-space ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq} consists of the following objects.
(1)
A collection of K-charts
(Kα=(Gα,Vα,Eα,Sα,ψα))α∈𝔸p​qFloer\Big(K_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha},V_{\alpha},E_{\alpha},S_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})\Big)_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}}
of (∂αℳ¯p​q)α∈𝔸p​qFloer(\partial^{\alpha}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}}.
(2)
A collection of weak K-chart embeddings
{𝜾β​α:Kα⇀∂αKβ}α≤β\big\{{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}:K_{\alpha}\rightharpoonup\partial^{\alpha}K_{\beta}\big\}_{\alpha\leq\beta}
They satisfy the following condition.
(1)
The weak K-chart embeddings satisfy the cocycle condition. Namely, for any triple of strata α≤β≤γ\alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma, one has
𝜾γ​β∘𝜾β​α=𝜾γ​α.{\bm{\iota}}_{\gamma\beta}\circ{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}={\bm{\iota}}_{\gamma\alpha}.
(2)
For each pair of strata α≤β\alpha\leq\beta, there are a GαG_{\alpha}-equivariant vector bundle Fβ​α→VαF_{\beta\alpha}\to V_{\alpha} and a germ of weak open K-chart embedding
𝜽β​α:StabFβ​α​(Kα)⇀∂αKβ{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha}:{\rm Stab}_{F_{\beta\alpha}}(K_{\alpha})\rightharpoonup\partial^{\alpha}K_{\beta}
whose restriction to the zero section coincides with 𝜾β​α{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}. We call 𝜽β​α{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha} a stabilization map.
The weak K-chart presentation is called a K-chart presentation if all weak K-chart embeddings are K-chart embeddings.
Definition 5.9.
A weak K-chart lift of the Hamiltonian Floer flow category TFloerT^{\rm Floer} consists of the following objects.
(1)
A collection of topological K-charts
{Kp​q=(Gp​q,Vp​q,Ep​q,Sp​q,ψp​q)}p<q\big\{K_{pq}=(G_{pq},V_{pq},E_{pq},S_{pq},\psi_{pq})\big\}_{p<q}
of ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}.
(2)
A collection of weak K-chart embeddings
{𝜾p​r​q:Kp​r×Kr​q⇀∂p​r​qKp​q}p<r<q.\big\{{\bm{\iota}}_{prq}:K_{pr}\times K_{rq}\rightharpoonup\partial^{prq}K_{pq}\big\}_{p<r<q}.
(5.5)
These objects need to satisfy the following conditions.
(1)
When p=qp=q, Vp​pV_{pp} is a singleton and Ep​p={0}E_{pp}=\{0\}.
(2)
The weak chart embeddings satisfy the associativity. More precisely, the following diagram commutes,
Kp​r×Kr​s×Ks​q\textstyle{K_{pr}\times K_{rs}\times K_{sq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}∂p​r​sKp​s×Ks​q\textstyle{\partial^{prs}K_{ps}\times K_{sq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Kp​r×∂r​s​qKr​q\textstyle{K_{pr}\times\partial^{rsq}K_{rq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}∂p​r​s​qKp​q\textstyle{\partial^{prsq}K_{pq}}
where the arrows are defined by (5.5).
(3)
For each stratum α=pr1⋯rlq∈𝔸Floerp​q\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}, consider the product topological K-chart
Kα=(Gα,Vα,Eα,Sα,ψα)=Kp​r1×⋯Krl​q.K_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha},V_{\alpha},E_{\alpha},S_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})=K_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots K_{r_{l}q}.
The above property implies that for each pair of strata α≤β\alpha\leq\beta of p​qpq, there is a well-defined weak K-chart embedding
𝜾β​α:Kα⇀∂αKβ.{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}:K_{\alpha}\rightharpoonup\partial^{\alpha}K_{\beta}.
Then the collection ((Kα)α∈𝔸p​q,(𝜾β​α)α≤β)((K_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}},({\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha})_{\alpha\leq\beta}) form a weak K-chart presentation of ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}.
The weak K-chart lift is called a K-chart lift if all the weak chart embeddings are chart embeddings.
Notation 5.10.
We introduce the following notation and convention which are frequently used in this paper. The Gp​qG_{pq}-equivariantization of the product chart KαK_{\alpha} is denoted by
Kα∼:=Gp​q×Gα(Kα)=(Gp​q,Vα∼,Eα∼,Sα∼).K_{\alpha}^{\sim}:=G_{pq}\times_{G_{\alpha}}(K_{\alpha})=(G_{pq},V_{\alpha}^{\sim},E_{\alpha}^{\sim},S_{\alpha}^{\sim}).
Then usually the version of notations KαK_{\alpha} (or Kpr1⋯rlqK_{pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q}) indicates the objects are constructed from taking products and the version of notations Kα∼K_{\alpha}^{\sim} (or Kpr1⋯rlq∼K_{pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q}^{\sim}) indicates the objects are constructed from taking products and an equivariantization (by a certain group which is clear from the context).
Now we are ready to state the first main theorem of this section.
Theorem 5.11.
The Hamiltonian Floer flow category TFloerT^{\rm Floer} admits a weak K-chart lift.
We also refer to the weak K-chart lift as the “global chart.” This theorem certainly does not contain all the properties we will need in the final construction. Eventually, we need to upgrade the weak lift to a lift and equip it with a smooth structure.
5.2. Global chart construction I
Following [AMS21, Section 6] (also in the same spirit as [Sie99, Section 3]), the goal of this subsection is to introduce a system of auxiliary moduli spaces (Bd)d≥1(B_{d})_{d\geq 1} parametrizing stable holomorphic cylinders in ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d}, which serve as Deligne–Mumford type moduli spaces for stable Floer trajectories. Moreover, we will consider the gluing of different (stable) holomorphic cylinders from different complex projective spaces. Such consideration will play an important role in the study of global charts of the boundary strata of moduli spaces of (stable) Floer trajectories.
5.2.1. Moduli spaces of stable cylinders
Following [AMS21] and [AB21], we introduce some auxiliary moduli spaces. Consider a genus zero prestable curve Σ\Sigma with two marked points z−z_{-}, z+z_{+}. The two marked points induce a decomposition
Σ=Σcyl∪Σsph\Sigma=\Sigma_{\rm cyl}\cup\Sigma_{\rm sph}
of Σ\Sigma into the cylindrical components and spherical components, where the cylindrical components are determined by the vertices on the line connecting z−z_{-} and z+z_{+} in the dual graph. Each cylindrical component Σi⊂Σ\Sigma_{i}\subset\Sigma has two special points corresponding to the negative and positive infinities zi,±z_{i,\pm}. There is a ℂ∗{\mathbb{C}}^{*}-action on Σα\Sigma_{\alpha}, given by biholomorphisms fixing zi,±z_{i,\pm}.
To fix the rotational gauge on the cylinder, we introduce the following concept. Identifying ℂ∗{\mathbb{C}}^{*} with S1×ℝS^{1}\times{\mathbb{R}}, a lateral line on a cylindrical component Σi\Sigma_{i} is an ℝ{\mathbb{R}}-orbit Li⊂Σi{\rm L}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}. There are other equivalent notions such as asymptotic markers which can also fix the rotational gauge.
Definition 5.12.
(1)
A prestable cylinder is a pair (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}) where Σ\Sigma is a genus zero pretable curve with two marked points z−,z+z_{-},z_{+} and 𝐋=(Li){\bf L}=({\rm L}_{i}) where each Li⊂Σi{\rm L}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i} is a lateral line on the cylindrical component Σi\Sigma_{i}. The cylindrical irreducible components are also referred to as the horizontal levels of (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}).
(2)
A marked stable cylinder is a triple (Σ,𝐳,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf z},{\bf L}) where (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}) is a prestable cylinder and 𝐳{\bf z} is a list of extra marked points which are different from z±z_{\pm} and the nodal points zi,±z_{i,\pm}, such that each irreducible component is stable (i.e., with at least three special points).
The notion of isomorphisms of marked stable cylinders can be defined in the obvious way. Let ℳ¯0,2,d′ℝ\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}} be the moduli space of marked stable cylinders with d′d^{\prime} marked points. It is a compactification of the moduli space ℳ0,2,d′ℝ{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}, the moduli space of marked stable cylinders with d′d^{\prime} marked points with only one cylindrical component and zero spherical components. Forgetting the lateral line defines a smooth S1S^{1}-fibration ℳ0,2,d′ℝ→ℳ2+d′{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{2+d^{\prime}} to the moduli space of genus 00 curves with 2+d′2+d^{\prime} marked points with smooth domains. Following [KSV95, Section 3.2], ℳ¯0,2,d′ℝ\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}} could be obtained from the Deligne–Mumford space ℳ¯0,2+d′\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,2+d^{\prime}} by performing real blowups along the irreducible components of the normal crossings divisor defined by stable curves with at least two cylindrical components. ℳ¯0,2,d′ℝ\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}} is a smooth manifold with corners: for a more detailed discussion, the reader could refer to [LT98b, Section 2.1] or [AB21, Section 9.7]. To simplify the notation, we usually abbreviate (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}) as Σ\Sigma when there is no ambiguity caused by the context.
5.2.2. Stable maps to projective spaces
We consider the moduli space of genus zero stable holomorphic maps into ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d} with two marked points. Denote by
ℱ:=ℱ0,2​(d)⊂ℳ¯0,2​(ℂ​ℙd,d){\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)
the subset of maps whose image is not entirely contained in any hyperplane. Any two smooth curves in ℱ{\mathcal{F}} can be mapped to another by an element of the symmetry group P​G​L​(d+1)PGL(d+1) of ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d}. Let 𝒞=𝒞0,2​(d)→ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) be the universal curve which has an induced P​G​L​(d+1)PGL(d+1)-action.
Lemma 5.13.
Both ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) and 𝒞0,2​(d){\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d) are smooth quasi-projective varieties. Moreover, the evaluation maps at two marked points
ev+,ev−:ℱ0,2​(d)→ℂ​ℙd{\rm ev}_{+},{\rm ev}_{-}:{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}
are smooth submersions.
Proof.
The first statement follows from [AMS21, Lemma 6.4]. For the submersive property, note that the natural action of P​G​L​(d+1)PGL(d+1) on ℳ¯0,2​(ℂ​ℙd,d)\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d) preserves the subspace ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d). Because the linearization of P​G​L​(d+1)PGL(d+1)-action on ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d} at any point defines a surjection from its Lie algebra to the tangent space of ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d} at this point, the linearization of ev±{\rm ev}_{\pm} is surjective as well.
∎
5.2.3. Stable cylinders in projective spaces
For the purpose of studying Floer theory, one needs to consider the “real” version of the above moduli spaces.
The moduli spaces of stable cylinders in ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d} is the set of equivalence classes of objects
(Σ,𝐋,u)(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)
such that (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}) is a prestable cylinder and u:Σ→ℂ​ℙdu:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d} is a stable map, i.e., every constant component has at least three special points. The equivalence relation is defined as follows: (Σ,𝐋,u)∼(Σ′,𝐋′,u′)(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)\sim(\Sigma^{\prime},{\bf L}^{\prime},u^{\prime}) if there is an isomorphism φ:(Σ,𝐋)≅(Σ′,𝐋′)\varphi:(\Sigma,{\bf L})\cong(\Sigma^{\prime},{\bf L}^{\prime}) such that u=u′∘φu=u^{\prime}\circ\varphi.
Definition 5.14.
Let ℳ¯(ℙd,k)ℝ0,2\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{P}^{d},k) denote the moduli space of stable cylinders in ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d} with degree kk times the generator of H2​(ℂ​ℙd,ℤ)H_{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d};{\mathbb{Z}}). Define
ℱ(d)ℝ0,2={[Σ,𝐋,u]∈ℳ¯(ℂℙd,d)ℝ0,2|[Σ,u]∈ℱ0,2(d)}.{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)=\{[\Sigma,{\bf L},u]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)\ |\ [\Sigma,u]\in{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\}.
Note that the evaluation maps ev±{\rm ev}_{\pm} at the two marked points on ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) naturally lift to evaluation maps on ℱ(d)ℝ0,2{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d).
Lemma 5.15.
There exists a natural structure of smooth 𝔸d{\mathbb{A}}_{d}-manifold on ℱ(d)ℝ0,2{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) satisfying the following conditions.
(1)
The forgetful map ℱ(d)ℝ0,2→ℱ0,2(d){\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) is smooth.
(2)
The evaluation maps ev±:[Σ,𝐋,u]↦u⁡(z±){\rm ev}_{\pm}:[\Sigma,{\bf L},u]\mapsto u(z_{\pm}) are smooth and they are stratified submersive (see Definition 3.19).
(3)
For any element a∈ℱ(d)ℝ0,2a\in{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) represented by (u,Σ,𝐋)(u,\Sigma,{\bf L}), choose a generic collection of d′=d⁡(d+2)d^{\prime}=d(d+2) hyperplanes and w1,…,wd′∈Σw_{1},\ldots,w_{d^{\prime}}\in\Sigma such that uu intersects with HiH_{i} transversely at wiw_{i}. Then the locally defined map given by taking a stable cylinder to the marked stable cylinder where the marked points are specified by the intersections with these hyperplanes is a diffeomorphism to an open subset of ℳ¯0,2+d′ℝ\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2+d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}.
Proof.
Consider the subspace of ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) which consists of stable holomorphic maps with exactly two cylindrical components of degrees d1d_{1} and d2d_{2}. Denote by the closure of this space in ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) by ℱ0,2​(d1,d2){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2}). Then ℱ0,2​(d1,d2){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2}) is a smooth divisor in the quasi-projective variety ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d). We can consider the real blowup of ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) along ℱ0,2​(d1,d2){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2}) ranging over all d1+d2=dd_{1}+d_{2}=d and denote it by ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d){\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d).
We claim that ℱ0,2ℝ​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) is an S1S^{1}-bundle over ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d){\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d). Indeed, given any stable cylinder (Σ,𝐋,u)∈ℱ(d)ℝ0,2(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)\in{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) with kk horizontal levels, denote by θi\theta_{i} to be the S1S^{1}-parameter of the lateral line on the ii-th cylindrical component of Σ\Sigma, with the convention that the first component contains z−z_{-} while the kk-th component contains z+z_{+}. Then the datum
(Σ,u,[θ2−θ1],…,[θk−θk−1])(\Sigma,u,[\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}],\dots,[\theta_{k}-\theta_{k-1}])
defines a point in ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d){\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) because θi−θi−1\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1} can be identified with the S1S^{1}-parameter in the exceptional divisor associated to some ℱ0,2​(d1,d2){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2}). Conversely, given θ∈S1\theta\in S^{1} and an element in ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d){\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d), we can define an element (Σ,𝐋,u)(\Sigma,{\bf L},u) in ℱ0,2ℝ​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) by requiring (Σ,u)(\Sigma,u) to be the image of the blow-down map ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d)→ℱ0,2​(d){\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) and the lateral line 𝐋{\bf L} is defined by the converse process of the previous construction. Note that these constructions are well-defined after choosing local coordinates near each stratum of ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) and they can be patched up together. Using this description, we can view ℱ0,2ℝ​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) as manifold with corners because ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d){\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) is so. Moreover, ℱ0,2ℝ​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) is actually a manifold with faces, with codimension-11 faces corresponding to a partition d=d1+d2d=d_{1}+d_{2}.
The forgetful map ℱ0,2ℝ​(d)→ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) is defined by (u,Σ,𝐋)↦(u,Σ)(u,\Sigma,{\bf L})\mapsto(u,\Sigma), which factors through the projection ℱ0,2ℝ​(d)→ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) and the blow-down map ℝ​ℱ0,2​(d)→ℱ0,2​(d){\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d), therefore it is smooth. The evaluation map ev+\text{ev}_{+} (resp. ev−\text{ev}_{-}) is the composition of the forgetful map ℱ0,2ℝ​(d)→ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) and the ordinary evaluation map ev+:ℱ0,2​(d)→ℂ​Pd\text{ev}_{+}:{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}P}^{d} (resp. ev−:ℱ0,2​(d)→ℂ​Pd\text{ev}_{-}:{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}P}^{d}), so ev±\text{ev}_{\pm} are smooth as well. Accordingly, ev±\text{ev}_{\pm} are stratified submersive following the same proof of Lemma 5.13 because the action of P​G​L​(d+1)PGL(d+1) preserves the strata of the simple normal crossings divisor defined by F0,2​(d1+d2)F_{0,2}(d_{1}+d_{2}).
For the last statement, it follows from [AMS21, Proposition 6.5] and the descriptions of ℱ0,2ℝ​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) and ℳ¯0,2+d′ℝ\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2+d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}} as S1S^{1}-bundles over real blowups.
∎
5.2.4. A system of auxiliary moduli spaces
Stable cylinders in projective spaces serve as models of domains of Floer trajectories. Because of the existence of broken Floer trajectories, we need to construct a system of moduli spaces of stable cylinders to capture the information from these boundary strata.
Notation 5.16.
(1)
Whenever 𝒜Floer​(p)≤𝒜Floer​(r)<𝒜Floer​(s)≤𝒜Floer​(q){\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(p)\leq{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(r)<{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(s)\leq{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(q), embed ℂ​ℙdr​s\mathbb{CP}^{d_{rs}} into ℂ​ℙdp​q\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}} using the map
[z0,…,zdr​s]↦[0,…,0⏟dp​r,z0,…,zdr​s,0,…,0⏟ds​q].[z_{0},\ldots,z_{d_{rs}}]\mapsto\big[\ \underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d_{pr}},z_{0},\ldots,z_{d_{rs}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d_{sq}}\ \big].
(2)
The system of auxiliary moduli spaces is the collection of moduli spaces
Bd:={x=[Σ,𝐋,u]∈ℱ(d)ℝ0,2|ev−(x)=[1,0,…,0]∈ℂℙd}.B_{d}:=\big\{x=[\Sigma,{\bf L},u]\in{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\ |\ {\rm ev}_{-}(x)=[1,0,\ldots,0]\in\mathbb{CP}^{d}\big\}.
For a partition of dd given by δ\delta from which d=d0+⋯+dld=d_{0}+\cdots+d_{l}, we define
Bδ:=Bd0×⋯×Bdl.B_{\delta}:=B_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times B_{d_{l}}.
Using this notation, define
Bα:=Bδ⁡(α),where​α∈𝔸p​qFloerB_{\alpha}:=B_{\delta(\alpha)},\ {\rm where}\ \alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}
where α↦δ⁡(α)\alpha\mapsto\delta(\alpha) is the poset map (5.2).
Lemma 5.17.
BdB_{d} is a smooth 𝔸d{\mathbb{A}}_{d}-manifold with a smooth GdG_{d}-action.
Proof.
This is a consequence of the blowup description of ℱ(d)ℝ0,2{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d) as from Lemma 5.15, the fact that ev−:ℱ0,2ℝ​(d)→ℂ​ℙd{\rm ev}_{-}:{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{0,2}(d)\rightarrow\mathbb{CP}^{d} is a stratified submersive, and Lemma 3.20. ∎
For a partition δ∈𝔸d\delta\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}, denote by ∂δB̊\partial^{\delta}\mathring{B} the locally closed smooth GdG_{d}-submanifold of BdB_{d} given by
∂δB̊:=∂δB∖⋃δ′<δ∂δ′B.\partial^{\delta}\mathring{B}:=\partial^{\delta}B\setminus\bigcup_{\delta^{\prime}<\delta}\partial^{\delta^{\prime}}B.
Definition 5.18 (Normalized evaluation).
Given x∈Bdx\in B_{d}, its normalized evaluation at z+z_{+} is a unit vector (a0,…,ad)∈ℂd(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d} specified as follows. Suppose δ=(d0,…,dl)\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l}) and x∈∂δB̊x\in\partial^{\delta}\mathring{B}. Assume that xx is represented by a stable cylinder (Σ,𝐋,u)(\Sigma,{\bf L},u). Let Θi0\Theta_{i}^{0} be ii-th cylindrical component of the domain Σ\Sigma and let ui0u_{i}^{0} be the restriction of uu to this cylindrical component. Using the lateral lines, one can identify Θi\Theta_{i} with ℂ∗{\mathbb{C}}^{*} with the lateral line identified with the positive real axis, and with zi,−z_{i,-} resp. zi,+z_{i,+} identified with 00 resp. ∞\infty. Moreover, ui0u_{i}^{0} can be written as
ui0​(z)=[fi,0​(z),…,fi,d​(z)]u_{i}^{0}(z)=[f_{i,0}(z),\ldots,f_{i,d}(z)]
where fi,0,…,fi,df_{i,0},\ldots,f_{i,d} are complex polynomials of degrees at most did_{i}. Let di0≤did_{i}^{0}\leq d_{i} be the maximal degree of these polynomials. We call a list of complex polynomials (fi,0,…,fi,d)(f_{i,0},\ldots,f_{i,d}) a polynomial representative of uiu_{i}. For each ii, the polynomial representative is only unique up to rescaling by a common factor in ℂ∗{\mathbb{C}}^{*} and a common reparametrization z↦λi​zz\mapsto\lambda_{i}z by a positive real number λi\lambda_{i}. However, there exists a unique set of polynomial representatives for all uiu_{i} such that
(1)
(f0,0​(0),…,f0,d​(0))=(1,0,…,0)∈ℂd+1(f_{0,0}(0),\ldots,f_{0,d}(0))=(1,0,\ldots,0)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}.
(2)
For each ii, the “evaluation” of (fi,0,…,fi,d)(f_{i,0},\ldots,f_{i,d}) at the marked point zi,+=∞z_{i,+}=\infty
(ai,0,…,ai,d):=limz→∞1zdi0​(fi,0​(z),…,fi,d​(z))(a_{i,0},\ldots,a_{i,d}):=\lim_{z\to\infty}\frac{1}{z^{d_{i}^{0}}}(f_{i,0}(z),\ldots,f_{i,d}(z))
is a unit vector of ℂd+1{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}.
(3)
For each i≥2i\geq 2, (fi,0​(0),…,fi,d​(0))=(ai−1,0,…,ai−1,d)(f_{i,0}(0),\ldots,f_{i,d}(0))=(a_{i-1,0},\ldots,a_{i-1,d}).
Then the unit vector (a0,…,ad):=(al,0,…,al,d)(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}):=(a_{l,0},\ldots,a_{l,d}) is called the normalized evaluation of x∈Bdx\in B_{d} at z+z_{+}. In particular,
ev+​(x)=[a0,…,ad]∈ℂ​ℙd.{\rm ev}_{+}(x)=[a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}]\in\mathbb{CP}^{d}.
We denote the normalized evaluation map defined as above by
ev~+:Bd→ℂd+1.\widetilde{\rm ev}_{+}:B_{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}.
It is easy to see that ev~+\widetilde{\rm ev}_{+} is smooth from its construction.
5.2.5. Boundary strata of the auxiliary moduli spaces
We introduce the following definitions in order to compare the boundary strata of the auxiliary moduli spaces and the products of moduli spaces with lower degrees. This should be thought of as a toy model for the comparison between the restriction of the geometrically-constructed K-charts along the boundary strata and the product of the K-charts from the factorization description of the boundary strata.
Definition 5.19 (Fans and Flags).
Fix d≥1d\geq 1 and δ=(d0,…,dl)∈𝔸d\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}.
(1)
A fan of type δ\delta is a list of linear subspaces of ℂd+1{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}
(Wd0,…,Wdl)(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}})
satisfying
(a)
dimℂ​Wdi=di+1{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}W_{d_{i}}=d_{i}+1.
(b)
Wd0+⋯+Wdl=ℂd+1W_{d_{0}}+\cdots+W_{d_{l}}={\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}.
(c)
dimℂ​(Wdi−1∩Wdi)=1{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(W_{d_{i-1}}\cap W_{d_{i}})=1.
Let Fanδ{\rm Fan}_{\delta} be the set of all fans of type δ\delta. Then there is a canonical map
∂δBd→Fanδ\partial^{\delta}B_{d}\to{\rm Fan}_{\delta}
which sends any equivalence class of stable cylinders to the fan for which WdiW_{d_{i}} is the subspace such that the image of the (i+1)(i+1)-th level is contained in the projectivization of WdiW_{d_{i}}. Note that such a map is well-defined because the stable cylinder at the (i+1)(i+1)-th level has degree did_{i}.
(2)
A flag of type δ\delta is a list of subspaces of ℂd+1{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}
V0⊂⋯⊂Vl=ℂd+1V_{0}\subset\cdots\subset V_{l}={\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}
satisfying
dimℂ​Vi=d0+⋯+di+1.{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}V_{i}=d_{0}+\cdots+d_{i}+1.
Let Flagδ{\rm Flag}_{\delta} be the set of all flags of type δ\delta. Then there is a standard flag of each type δ\delta where
Vi=ℂd0+⋯+di+1×{0}di+1+⋯+dl,i=0,…,l.V_{i}={\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+\cdots+d_{i}+1}\times\{0\}^{d_{i+1}+\cdots+d_{l}},i=0,\ldots,l.
(3)
There is a canonical map
Fanδ→Flagδ{\rm Fan}_{\delta}\to{\rm Flag}_{\delta}
which maps (Wd0,…,Wdl)(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}}) to the flag where
Vi=Wd0+⋯+Wdi.V_{i}=W_{d_{0}}+\cdots+W_{d_{i}}.
(4)
A fan of type δ\delta is said to be in the normal position if the following is true. For each ii, let
Wi−:=Wd0+⋯+Wdi,\displaystyle\ W_{i}^{-}:=W_{d_{0}}+\cdots+W_{d_{i}},\
Wi+:=Wdi+1+⋯+Wdl.\displaystyle\ W_{i}^{+}:=W_{d_{i+1}}+\cdots+W_{d_{l}}.
The condition for being a fan implies that Wi−∩Wi+W_{i}^{-}\cap W_{i}^{+} is a line LiL_{i}. Let W̊i±\mathring{W}_{i}^{\pm} be the orthogonal complement of LiL_{i} in Wi±W_{i}^{\pm} (with respect to the standard Hermitian inner product of ℂd+1{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}). Then W̊i−\mathring{W}_{i}^{-} and W̊i+\mathring{W}_{i}^{+} are orthogonal for all i=0,…,l−1i=0,\ldots,l-1.
(5)
A point x∈Bdx\in B_{d} is said to be in the δ\delta-normal position if x∈∂δBdx\in\partial^{\delta}B_{d} and its associated fan of type δ\delta is in the normal position. Let (∂δBd)normal⊂∂δBd(\partial^{\delta}B_{d})^{\rm normal}\subset\partial^{\delta}B_{d} be the subset of stable cylinders which are in δ\delta-normal position.
We first describe the case of a codimension-one stratum of the auxiliary moduli space as a warm-up. Fix d≥2d\geq 2 and a partition (d0,d1)∈𝔸d(d_{0},d_{1})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}. There is a corresponding stratum ∂(d0,d1)Bd⊂Bd\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d}\subset B_{d} from Notation 5.16. We would like to define a map
ζ(d0,d1):Bd0×Bd1↪∂(d0,d1)Bd.\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}:B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\hookrightarrow\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d}.
(5.6)
Given two arbitrary points x0∈Bd0x_{0}\in B_{d_{0}}, x1∈Bd1x_{1}\in B_{d_{1}}, ζ(d0,d1)​(x0,x1)\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1}) can be defined as follows. Let u0:Σ0→ℂ​ℙd0u_{0}:\Sigma_{0}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{0}}, u1:Σ1→ℂ​ℙd1u_{1}:\Sigma_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{1}} be representatives. Let (a0,…,ad0)(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}) be the normalized evaluation of x0x_{0} (see Definition 5.18). One can represent u0u_{0} resp. u1u_{1} as a list of holomorphic sections
(f0,…,fd0)​resp.(g0,…,gd1)(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d_{0}})\ {\rm resp}.\ (g_{0},\ldots,g_{d_{1}})
of the line bundle L0=u0∗​𝒪​(1)→Σ0L_{0}=u_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(1)\to\Sigma_{0} resp. L1=u1∗​𝒪​(1)→Σ1L_{1}=u_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(1)\to\Sigma_{1}. Then define ζ(d0,d1)​(x0,x1)∈∂(d0,d1)Bd\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1})\in\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d} to be the point represented by the map
u(d0,d1)\displaystyle u_{(d_{0},d_{1})}
:Σ0∨Σ1→ℂ​ℙd,\displaystyle:\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},
(5.7)
z\displaystyle z
↦{[f0​(z),…,fd0​(z),0,…,0],z∈Σ0,[a0​g0​(z),…,ad0​g0​(z),g1​(z),…,gd1​(z)],z∈Σ1.\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm[}f_{0}(z),\ldots,f_{d_{0}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{0},\\
{\rm[}a_{0}g_{0}(z),\ldots,a_{d_{0}}g_{0}(z),g_{1}(z),\ldots,g_{d_{1}}(z){\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{1}.\end{array}\right.
Lemma 5.20.
The map ζ(d0,d1)\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})} is equivariant with respect to the group embedding Gd0×Gd1↪GdG_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\hookrightarrow G_{d} from Notation 5.7.
Proof.
Straightforward from the definitions.
∎
Hence ζ(d0,d1)\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})} can be extended to a GdG_{d}-equivariant map
ζ(d0,d1)∼:Gd×G(d0,d1)(Bd0×Bd1)→∂(d0,d1)Bd.\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}:G_{d}\times_{G_{(d_{0},d_{1})}}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}})\to\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d}.
(5.10)
However, the above map is not surjective as configurations in the image are those stable cylinders whose two levels are in certain “normal” positions.
Lemma 5.21.
ζ(d0,d1)∼\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim} is bijective onto (∂(d0,d1)Bd)normal(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d})^{\rm normal}.
Proof.
From the definition we know that the image of ζ(d0,d1)∼\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim} is contained in (∂(d0,d1)Bd)normal(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d})^{\rm normal}. We first prove the surjectivity. For any x∈(∂(d0,d1)Bd)normalx\in(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d})^{\rm normal} with associated fan (Wd0,Wd1)(W_{d_{0}},W_{d_{1}}), denote Lx:=Wd0∩Wd1L_{x}:=W_{d_{0}}\cap W_{d_{1}}. Let the domain of xx be Σ=Σ0∪Σ1\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}. By using a unitary transformation on ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d} which fixes the point [1,0,…,0][1,0,\ldots,0], we may assume that Wd0W_{d_{0}} is spanned by the first d0+1d_{0}+1 coordinates. Then the first level of xx can be viewed as a stable cylinder in ℂ​ℙd0\mathbb{CP}^{d_{0}}. Let the normalized evaluation be (a0,…,ad0)(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}). Then the nodal point of xx is mapped to [a0,…,ad0,0,…,0][a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}},0,\ldots,0]. Then by the definition of being in the normal position, W̊0+\mathring{W}_{0}^{+} is the subspace spanned by the last d1d_{1} coordinates and Wd1=W̊0++LxW_{d_{1}}=\mathring{W}_{0}^{+}+L_{x}. Then the second level of xx is represented by the map
ur​q​(z)=[a0​f0​(z),…,ad0​f0​(z),f1​(z),…,fd1​(z)]u_{rq}(z)=[a_{0}f_{0}(z),\ldots,a_{d_{0}}f_{0}(z),f_{1}(z),\ldots,f_{d_{1}}(z)]
where (f0,…,fd1)(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d_{1}}) is a list of holomorphic sections of a degree d1d_{1} line bundle over Σ1\Sigma_{1}. As (a0,…,ad0)(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}) is the normalized evaluation of the first level of xx, one can see that x=ζ(d0,d1)​(x0,x1)x=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1}) where x0x_{0} is the first level of xx and x1x_{1} is represented by the map z↦[f0​(z),…,fd1​(z)]z\mapsto[f_{0}(z),\ldots,f_{d_{1}}(z)]. Therefore ζ(d0,d1)∼\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim} is surjective.
To prove that ζ(d0,d1)∼\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim} is injective, suppose
x=ζ(d0,d1)∼​([g,x0,x1])=ζ(d0,d1)∼​([g′,x0′,x1′]).x=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}([g,x_{0},x_{1}])=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}([g^{\prime},x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}]).
We may assume g′=1g^{\prime}=1. Then the flag associated to xx is the standard one and g∈Gdg\in G_{d} preserves this flag. Hence g∈Gd0×Gd1g\in G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}. Hence we may also assume g=1g=1. Then ζ(d0,d1)​(x0,x1)=ζ(d0,d1)​(x0′,x1′)\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1})=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}), which implies x0=x0′x_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime} and x1=x1′x_{1}=x_{1}^{\prime}. Hence ζ(d0,d1)∼\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim} is injective.
∎
It is important to prove the associativity of the product construction.
Proposition 5.22.
For a partition (d0,d1,d2)(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}) of d≥1d\geq 1, x0∈Bd0x_{0}\in B_{d_{0}}, x1∈Bd1x_{1}\in B_{d_{1}}, and x2∈Bd2x_{2}\in B_{d_{2}}, there holds
ζ(d0,d1+d2)​(x0,ζ(d1,d2)​(x1,x2))=ζ(d0+d1,d2)​(ζ(d0,d1)​(x0,x1),x2).\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}(x_{0},\zeta_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{1},x_{2}))=\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}(\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1}),x_{2}).
Proof.
Choose representatives u0,u1,u2u_{0},u_{1},u_{2} of x0,x1,x2x_{0},x_{1},x_{2} respectively. Let
(a0,…,ad0),\displaystyle\ (a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}),\
(b0,…,bd1)\displaystyle\ (b_{0},\ldots,b_{d_{1}})
be the normalized evaluations of u0u_{0} and u1u_{1} at z+z_{+}. Then by definition, x(d1,d2):=ζ(d1,d2)​(x1,x2)x_{(d_{1},d_{2})}:=\zeta_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{1},x_{2}) is represented by the map with domain Σ1∨Σ2\Sigma_{1}\vee\Sigma_{2}
u(d1,d2)​(z)={[u1,0​(z),…,u1,d1​(z),0,…,0],z∈Σ1,[b0​u2,0​(z),…,bd1​u2,0​(z),u2,1​(z),…,u2,d2​(z)],z∈Σ2.u_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm[}u_{1,0}(z),\ldots,u_{1,d_{1}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},\ z\in\Sigma_{1},\\
{\rm[}b_{0}u_{2,0}(z),\ldots,b_{d_{1}}u_{2,0}(z),u_{2,1}(z),\ldots,u_{2,d_{2}}(z){\rm]},\ z\in\Sigma_{2}.\end{array}\right.
Then ζ(d0,d1+d2)​(x0,x(d1,d2))\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}(x_{0},x_{(d_{1},d_{2})}) is represented by the map with domain Σ0∨Σ1∨Σ2\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}\vee\Sigma_{2}
u012​(z)={[u0,0​(z),…,u0,d0​(z),0,…,0],z∈Σ0,[a0​u1,0​(z),…,ad0​u1,0​(z),u1,1​(z),…,u1,d1​(z),0,…,0],z∈Σ1,[b0​a0​u2,0​(z),…,b0​ad0​u2,0​(z),b1​u2,0​(z),…,bd1​u2,0​(z),u2,1​(z),…,u2,d2​(z)],z∈Σ2.u_{012}(z)\\
=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm[}u_{0,0}(z),\ldots,u_{0,d_{0}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},&z\in\Sigma_{0},\\
{\rm[}a_{0}u_{1,0}(z),\ldots,a_{d_{0}}u_{1,0}(z),u_{1,1}(z),\ldots,u_{1,d_{1}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{1},\\
{\rm[}b_{0}a_{0}u_{2,0}(z),\ldots,b_{0}a_{d_{0}}u_{2,0}(z),b_{1}u_{2,0}(z),\ldots,b_{d_{1}}u_{2,0}(z),u_{2,1}(z),\ldots,u_{2,d_{2}}(z){\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{2}.\end{array}\right.
On the other hand, x(d0,d1):=ζ(d0,d1)​(x0,x1)x_{(d_{0},d_{1})}:=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1}) is represented by the map u(d0,d1):Σ0∨Σ1→ℂ​ℙd0+d1u_{(d_{0},d_{1})}:\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{0}+d_{1}} whose representation is the same as u012|Σ0∨Σ1u_{012}|_{\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}} above after removing the last d2d_{2} zeroes. Its evaluation at z+z_{+} is represented by the vector
(b0​a0,…,b0​ad0,b1,…,bd1)∈ℂd0+d1+1.(b_{0}a_{0},\ldots,b_{0}a_{d_{0}},b_{1},\ldots,b_{d_{1}})\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}.
which is a unit vector and is the normalized evaluation of x(d0,d1)x_{(d_{0},d_{1})}. Hence we can see from the definition of ζ(d0+d1,d2)\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})} that the point ζ(d0+d1,d2)​(x(d0,d1),x2)\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{(d_{0},d_{1})},x_{2}) is also represented by the map u012u^{012}.
∎
Now given a partition d=d1+d2+d3d=d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}, use (5.4), we can define a map
ζ(d0,d1,d2)∼:Gd×Gd0×Gd1×Gd2(Bd0×Bd1×Bd2)→∂(d0,d1,d2)Bd\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{\sim}:G_{d}\times_{G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\times G_{d_{2}}}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}})\to\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}B_{d}
as follows. For any [g,x0,x1,x2]∈Gd×Gd0×Gd1×Gd2(Bd0×Bd1×Bd2)[g,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]\in G_{d}\times_{G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\times G_{d_{2}}}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}}), define
ζ(d0,d1,d2)∼​([g,x0,x1,x2]):=g⁡(ζ(d0,d1+d2)​(x0,ζ(d1,d2)​(x1,x2))).\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{\sim}([g,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]):=g(\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}(x_{0},\zeta_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{1},x_{2}))).
It is straightfoward to check that this is a well-defined equivariant map. On the other hand, we can also define a GdG_{d}-equivariant map
[g,x0,x1,x2]↦g⁡(ζ(d0+d1,d2)​(ζ(d0,d1)​(x0,x1),x2)).[g,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]\mapsto g(\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}(\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1}),x_{2})).
The following commutative diagram coming from Proposition 5.22
Gd​(Bd0×Bd1×Bd2)\textstyle{G_{d}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Gd​(Bd0×∂(d1,d2)Bd1+d2)\textstyle{G_{d}\big(B_{d_{0}}\times\partial^{(d_{1},d_{2})}B_{d_{1}+d_{2}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Gd​(∂(d0,d1)Bd0+d1×Bd2)\textstyle{G_{d}\big(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d_{0}+d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}∂(d0,d1,d2)Bd\textstyle{\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}B_{d}}
shows that these two equivariantization maps agree with each other. Therefore, the map ζ(d0,d1,d2)∼\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{\sim} is indeed unambiguously well-defined, independent of the ways of grouping the partitions.
It is straightforward to carry out the above discussion to the case with more factors. Recall for δ=(d0,…,dl)∈𝔸d\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}, we defined
Bδ:=Bd0×⋯×BdlB_{\delta}:=B_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times B_{d_{l}}
which has the action of GδG_{\delta}. Using the group embedding (5.4), define
Bδ∼:=Gd×GδBδ.B_{\delta}^{\sim}:=G_{d}\times_{G_{\delta}}B_{\delta}.
Then there is a GdG_{d}-equivariant map
ζδ∼:Bδ∼↪∂δBd,\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}:B_{\delta}^{\sim}\hookrightarrow\partial^{\delta}B_{d},
(5.11)
which, for instance, can be constructed by writing Bδ∼B_{\delta}^{\sim} as
Gd(Bd0×⋯×Gdl−2×Gdl−1+dl(Gdl−1+dl×Gdl−1×Gdl(Bdl−1×Bdl))).G_{d}(B_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times_{G_{d_{l-2}}\times G_{d_{l-1}+d_{l}}}(G_{d_{l-1}+d_{l}}\times_{G_{d_{l-1}}\times G_{d_{l}}}(B_{d_{l-1}}\times B_{d_{l}}))).
Proposition 5.22 indicates that such an inductive construction of the map ζδ∼\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim} is independent of the order of the factorization. This embedding is smooth. The following statement follows from the arguments in the proof of Lemma 5.21 and we record it here.
Lemma 5.23.
ζδ∼\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim} is bijective onto (∂δBd)normal(\partial^{\delta}B_{d})^{\rm normal}. ∎
Lastly we need to analyze the difference between Bδ∼B_{\delta}^{\sim} and ∂δBd\partial^{\delta}B_{d}. We will show that in fact, ∂δBd\partial^{\delta}B_{d} can be viewed as the total space of a GδG_{\delta}-equivariant vector bundle over Bδ∼B_{\delta}^{\sim}.
We introduce certain notations which will also be used in the thickening construction. For all d≥0d\geq 0, denote
𝑸d=𝑸~d/ℝ+where𝑸~d:={h~∈ℂ(d+1)×(d+1)|h~†=h~,h~00≠0}.{\bm{Q}}_{d}=\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d}/{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\ {\rm where}\ \tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d}:=\Big\{\tilde{h}\in\mathbb{C}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}\ |\ \tilde{h}^{\dagger}=\tilde{h},\ \tilde{h}_{00}\neq 0\Big\}.
(5.12)
Here our convention is that the indices of the Hermitian matrix h~∈𝑸~d\tilde{h}\in\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d} range from 00 to dd; the multiplicative group ℝ+{\mathbb{R}}_{+} acts on 𝑸~d\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d} by scalar multiplication on each entry. The ℝ+{\mathbb{R}}_{+}-orbit of h~∈𝑸~d\tilde{h}\in\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d} is denoted by [h~][\tilde{h}]. We identify 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d} with
𝑸d∗:={h∈ℂ(d+1)×(d+1)|h†=h,h00=0}{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{*}:=\Big\{h\in\mathbb{C}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}\ |\ h^{\dagger}=h,\ h_{00}=0\Big\}
(5.13)
in the way that a Hermitian matrix hh with h00=0h_{00}=0 is identified with the ℝ+{\mathbb{R}}_{+}-orbit of h~=Id+1+h\tilde{h}=I_{d+1}+h. Then 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d} is a real vector space with dimension equal to d2+2​dd^{2}+2d. Moreover, by identifying GdG_{d} with U⁡(d)⊂U⁡(d+1)U(d)\subset U(d+1) using the correspondence (5.3), the usual conjugation action of U⁡(d+1)U(d+1) on (d+1)×(d+1)(d+1)\times(d+1) Hermitian matrices restricts to a linear action of GdG_{d} on 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d}.
To go further, for each partition δ=(d0,…,dl)∈𝔸d\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}, define
𝑸δ:={[h~]∈𝑸d|h~i​j≠0⟹∃a≥−1s.t.d0+⋯+da≤i,j≤d0+⋯+da+1}{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}:=\Big\{[\tilde{h}]\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}\ |\ \tilde{h}_{ij}\neq 0\Longrightarrow\exists\ a\geq-1\ {\rm s.t.}\ d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a}\leq i,j\leq d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a+1}\Big\}
where for a=−1a=-1, the constraints on the entries are given by 0≤i,j≤d00\leq i,j\leq d_{0},
and its complement
𝑸ˇδ:={[h~]∈𝑸d|h~i​j=0∀a=0,…,l,d0+⋯+da≤i,j≤d0+⋯+da+1}≅{h∈𝑸d∗|hi​j=0∀a=0,…,l,d0+⋯+da≤i,j≤d0+⋯+da+1}.\begin{split}\check{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}:=&\ \Big\{[\tilde{h}]\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}\ |\ \tilde{h}_{ij}=0\ \forall a=0,\ldots,l,\ d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a}\leq i,j\leq d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a+1}\Big\}\\
\cong&\ \Big\{h\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{*}\ |\ h_{ij}=0\ \forall a=0,\ldots,l,\ d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a}\leq i,j\leq d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a+1}\Big\}.\end{split}
This vector space is invariant under the adjoint action of GδG_{\delta}. Hence we have a GδG_{\delta}-equivariant splitting
𝑸d=𝑸δ⊕𝑸d,δ.{\bm{Q}}_{d}={\bm{Q}}_{\delta}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}.
Moreover, 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d} is stratified by partitions. When δ≤η\delta\leq\eta, one has the obvious inclusion
𝑸δ⊂𝑸η.{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}\subset{\bm{Q}}_{\eta}.
Define
𝑸η​δ:=𝑸η∩𝑸d,δ.{\bm{Q}}_{\eta\delta}:={\bm{Q}}_{\eta}\cap{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}.
Using the GδG_{\delta}-action on the space 𝑸d,δ{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}, we define a GδG_{\delta}-equivariant vector bundle
Qd,δ:=Bδ×𝑸d,δ.Q_{d,\delta}:=B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}.
The GdG_{d}-equivariantization of this vector bundle is denoted by
Qd,δ∼:=Gd×GδQd,δ→Bδ∼.Q_{d,\delta}^{\sim}:=G_{d}\times_{G_{\delta}}Q_{d,\delta}\to B_{\delta}^{\sim}.
(5.14)
Proposition 5.24.
There is a GdG_{d}-equivariant diffeomorphism
ρδ∼:Qd,δ∼≅∂δBd\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}:Q_{d,\delta}^{\sim}\cong\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
which extends the embedding ζδ∼:Bδ∼↪∂δBd\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}:B_{\delta}^{\sim}\hookrightarrow\partial^{\delta}B_{d}.
Proof.
We first define a map ρδ:Qd,δ→∂δBd\rho_{\delta}:Q_{d,\delta}\to\partial^{\delta}B_{d} which extends ζδ:Bδ→∂δBd\zeta_{\delta}:B_{\delta}\to\partial^{\delta}B_{d} as follows. Given a Hermitian matrix h∈𝑸ˇδh\in\check{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}, we identify it with a complex matrix ρh\rho_{h} obtained by changing all entries in hh that are below the diagonal to zero. Then define
ρδ​(h,x0,…,xl):=(Id+1+ρh)​(ζδ​(x0,…,xd)).\rho_{\delta}(h,x_{0},\ldots,x_{l}):=(I_{d+1}+\rho_{h})(\zeta_{\delta}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{d})).
Note that Id+1+ρhI_{d+1}+\rho_{h} is a nonsingular matrix because ρh\rho_{h} is nilpotent. It is straightforward to check that this map is equivariant with respect to the group embedding Gδ→GdG_{\delta}\to G_{d}. Hence it extends to a GdG_{d}-equivariant map
ρδ∼:Qd,δ∼→∂δBd.\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}:Q_{d,\delta}^{\sim}\to\partial^{\delta}B_{d}.
We need to show that this map is a diffeomorphism.
(1)
(Injectivity) Suppose
x=ρδ∼​([g,h,x0,…,xl])=ρδ∼​([g′,h′,x0′,…,xl′]).x=\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}([g,h,x_{0},\ldots,x_{l}])=\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}([g^{\prime},h^{\prime},x_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{l}^{\prime}]).
Then by the definition of ρδ∼\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}, we may assume that g′=1∈Gdg^{\prime}=1\in G_{d}. Then, notice that the flag induced by ρδ​(h′,x0′,…,xl′)\rho_{\delta}(h^{\prime},x_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{l}^{\prime}) is the standard one and gg preserves the flag. Then g∈Gδg\in G_{\delta}. Hence we may also assume g=1∈Gp​qg=1\in G_{pq}. By considering the fans associated to stable maps, one can also conclude that h=h′h=h^{\prime}. As Id+1+ρhI_{d+1}+\rho_{h} is invertible, one obtains that xi=xi′x_{i}=x_{i}^{\prime} for all i=0,…,li=0,\ldots,l. Hence ρδ∼\rho_{\delta}^{\sim} is injective.
(2)
(Surjectivity) Let x∈∂δBdx\in\partial^{\delta}B_{d} be represented by a map
u=(u0,…,ul):Σ0∨⋯∨Σl→ℂ​ℙd.u=(u_{0},\ldots,u_{l}):\Sigma_{0}\vee\cdots\vee\Sigma_{l}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}.
Let (V0,…,Vl)(V_{0},\ldots,V_{l}) be the induced flag. Then there exists a unitary matrix gg which transforms this flag to the standard one. Hence we may assume the flag associated to xx is standard. Let (Wd0,…,Wdl)(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}}) be the fan associated to xx. We construct an element h∈𝑸d,δh\in{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta} such that
(Idp​q+1+ρh)​(Wd0,…,Wdl)(I_{d_{pq}+1}+\rho_{h})(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}})
is in the normal position. hh can be constructed inductively. First, let y0∈ℂd0+1y_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+1} be the noramlized evaluation of u0u_{0} at z+z_{+}. Regard y0y_{0} as a vector in ℂd0+d1+1\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}. Then there exist vectors w1,…,wd1∈ℂd0+d1+1w_{1},\ldots,w_{d_{1}}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1} such that
Wd1=span⁡(y0,w1,…,wd1).W_{d_{1}}={\rm span}(y_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{d_{1}}).
Then as Wd0=ℂd0+1W_{d_{0}}={\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+1} and Wd0+Wd1=ℂd0+d1+1W_{d_{0}}+W_{d_{1}}={\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}, there exists a complex matrix Ad1d0A_{d_{1}}^{d_{0}} whose nonzero entries are contained in the upper-right d0×d1d_{0}\times d_{1}-block such that
(Idp​r2+1−Ad1d0)​(w1,…,wd1)=(ed0+1,…,ed0+d1)(I_{d_{pr_{2}}+1}-A_{d_{1}}^{d_{0}})(w_{1},\ldots,w_{d_{1}})=(e_{d_{0}+1},\ldots,e_{d_{0}+d_{1}})
(where e0,…,ed0+d1e_{0},\ldots,e_{d_{0}+d_{1}} form the standard basis of ℂd0+d1+1{\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}). Inductively, one can construct a matrix
A:=[0Ad1d0Ad2d0⋯Adld000Ad2d1⋯Adld1⋮⋮⋮⋱⋮00000]A:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&A_{d_{1}}^{d_{0}}&A_{d_{2}}^{d_{0}}&\cdots&A_{d_{l}}^{d_{0}}\\
0&0&A_{d_{2}}^{d_{1}}&\cdots&A_{d_{l}}^{d_{1}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{array}\right]
such that (Id+1−A)​(Wd0,…,Wdl)(I_{d+1}-A)(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}}) is a fan in the normal position. Apply Id+1−AI_{d+1}-A to the stable map uu, one obtains a stable map u′u^{\prime} which is in the normal position in ∂δBd\partial^{\delta}B_{d}. The nilpotence of AA and its block form imply that −A=ρh-A=\rho_{h} for some h∈𝑸d,δh\in{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}. As stable maps in normal positions are all in the image of ζδ∼\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}, the above argument implies that ρ\rho is surjective.
The smoothness of ρδ∼\rho_{\delta}^{\sim} and its inverse follows from the definition and the smoothness of ζδ∼\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}.
∎
5.2.6. Product of the bundle of quadratic forms
The bundles of quadratic forms play an crucial role in the construction of global charts, as they will be part of the obstruction bundle. We describe the multiplicative structures of these bundles. Define for each d≥1d\geq 1 the GdG_{d}-equivariant bundle
Qd:=Bd×𝑸d.Q_{d}:=B_{d}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}.
We would like to define a collection of equivariant bundle embeddings
Qd0⊞Qd1\textstyle{Q_{d_{0}}\boxplus Q_{d_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Qd0+d1\textstyle{Q_{d_{0}+d_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Bd0×Bd1\textstyle{B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}ζ(d0,d1)\scriptstyle{\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}}Bd0+d1\textstyle{B_{d_{0}+d_{1}}}
which are associative. Let us look at the partition d=d0+d1d=d_{0}+d_{1} and let
Qd0→d,Qd1→d→B(d0,d1)≅Bd0×Bd1Q_{d_{0}\to d},Q_{d_{1}\to d}\to B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\cong B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}
be the bundles B(d0,d1)×𝑸d0B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}} and B(d0,d1)×𝑸d1B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}. Define embeddings
ζ^d0→dQ:Qd0→d→Qd,\displaystyle\ \widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}:Q_{d_{0}\to d}\to Q_{d},\
ζ^d1→dQ:Qd1→d→Qd\displaystyle\ \widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}:Q_{d_{1}\to d}\to Q_{d}
as follows.
(1)
For ζ^d0→dQ\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}: for each element hd0∈𝑸d0h_{d_{0}}\in{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}} (which is regarded as a Hermitian matrix whose (0,0)(0,0)-entry vanishes), define
(ζ^d0→dQ​(xd0,xd1,hd0))i​j={(hd0)i​j, 0≤i,j≤d0,0,otherwise,\Big(\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}};h_{d_{0}})\Big)_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(h_{d_{0}})_{ij},&\ 0\leq i,j\leq d_{0},\\
0,&\ {\rm otherwise,}\end{array}\right.
which gives a Hermtian matrix in 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d} (regarded as a matrix whose (0,0)(0,0)-entry vanishes).
(2)
For θ^d1→dQ\widehat{\theta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}: for x=(xd0,xd1)x=(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}}), let the normalized evaluation of xd0x_{d_{0}} at z+z_{+} to be (a0,…,ad0)(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}). Then there exists an element gx∈U⁡(d0+1)g_{x}\in U(d_{0}+1) such that
gx​(0,…,1)=(a0,…,ad0).g_{x}(0,\ldots,1)=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}).
(5.15)
Embed gxg_{x} into P​U​(d0+1)PU(d_{0}+1) with image
[(gx)(d0+1)×(d0+1)00Id1]\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}(g_{x})_{(d_{0}+1)\times(d_{0}+1)}&0\\
0&I_{d_{1}}\end{array}\right]
and denote it by gx+g_{x}^{+}. Then for hd1∈𝑸d1h_{d_{1}}\in{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}, define the Hermitian matrix with vanishing (0,0)(0,0)-entry
hd1+∈𝑸d,(hd1+)i​j={(hd1)i−d0​j−d0,d0≤i,j≤d,0,otherwiseh_{d_{1}}^{+}\in{\bm{Q}}_{d},\ (h_{d_{1}}^{+})_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(h_{d_{1}})_{i-d_{0}\ j-d_{0}},&\ d_{0}\leq i,j\leq d,\\
0,&\ {\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.
and define
ζ^d1→dQ​(xd0,xd1,hd1)=gx+​hd1+​(gx+)−1.\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}};h_{d_{1}})=g_{x}^{+}h_{d_{1}}^{+}(g_{x}^{+})^{-1}.
We can easily check that this is independent of the choice of gxg_{x} satisfying (5.15).
Then ζ^d0→dQ\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q} and ζ^d1→dQ\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q} canonically correspond to bundle maps, which are denoted by the same symbols respectively. Then define a map
ζ^(d0,d1)Q:Qd0→d⊕Qd1→d→B(d0,d1)×𝑸d\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q}:Q_{d_{0}\to d}\oplus Q_{d_{1}\to d}\to B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}
to be
ζ^(d0,d1)Q=ζ^d0→dQ+ζ^d1→dQ.\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q}=\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}+\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}.
Lemma 5.25.
The bundle map ζ^(d0,d1)Q\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q} is a Gd0×Gd1G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}-equivariant linear bundle embedding with image being the bundle B(d0,d1)×𝐐(d0,d1)B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}.
Proof.
The linearity follows from the definition of 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d} and the definition of the maps. The Gd0×Gd1G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}-equivariance of ζ^d0→dQ\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q} is obvious from the definition; the Gd1G_{d_{1}}-equivariance of ζ^d1→dQ\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q} is also straightforward. However, the Gd0G_{d_{0}}-equivariance of ζ^d1→dQ\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q} requires a verification. Indeed, fix x=(xd0,xd1)x=(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}}); suppose the normalized evaluation of xd0x_{d_{0}} is z=(a0,…,ad0)z=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}). Then choose g∈Gd0g\in G_{d_{0}}. Then g​x=(g​xd0,xd1)gx=(gx_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}}) and the normalized evaluation of g​xd0gx_{d_{0}} is g​zgz. So if [z]=[a0,…,ad0]=gx​[0,…,1][z]=[a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}]=g_{x}[0,\ldots,1], then [g​z]=g​gx​[0,…,1][gz]=gg_{x}[0,\ldots,1]. Then one can check the Gd0G_{d_{0}}-equivariance. The injectivity and the fact that the image is exactly B(d0,d1)×𝑸(d0,d1)B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{(d_{0},d_{1})} is also straightforward to verify following the proof of Lemma 5.21.
∎
The following proposition is the counterpart of Proposition 5.22 for quadratic bundles, which can be proved based on keeping track of the action of gxg_{x} from (5.15).
Proposition 5.26.
The bundle embeddings defined above are associative. Namely, for d0,d1,d2d_{0},d_{1},d_{2} such that d=d0+d1+d2d=d_{0}+d_{1}+d_{2}, recall
B(d0,d1,d2)=Bd0×Bd1×Bd2.B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}=B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}}.
Then given x∈B(d0,d1,d2)x\in B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})} and
(hd0,hd1,hd2)∈𝑸d0×𝑸d1×𝑸d2,(h_{d_{0}},h_{d_{1}},h_{d_{2}})\in{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{2}},
one has
ζ^(d0,d1+d2)Q​(x,hd0,ζ^(d1,d2)Q​(x,hd1,hd2))=ζ^(d0+d1,d2)Q​(x,ζ^(d0,d1)Q​(x,hd0,hd1),hd2).∎\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}^{Q}(x,h_{d_{0}},\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{1},d_{2})}^{Q}(x,h_{d_{1}},h_{d_{2}}))=\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}^{Q}(x,\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q}(x,h_{d_{0}},h_{d_{1}}),h_{d_{2}}).\qed
As a consequence, there is a well-defined Gd0×Gd1×Gd2G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\times G_{d_{2}}-equivariant linear bundle embedding
ζ^(d0,d1,d2)Q:B(d0,d1,d2)×𝑸d0×𝑸d1×𝑸d2→B(d0,d1,d2)×𝑸d,\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{Q}:B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{2}}\to B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d},
whose image is B(d0,d1,d2)×𝑸(d0,d1,d2)B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}. The associativity further implies that for any δ=(d0,…,dl)∈𝔸d\delta=(d_{0},\dots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}, there exists a GδG_{\delta}-equivariant linear bundle embedding
ζ^δQ:Qδ:=Bδ×𝑸d0×⋯×𝑸dl→∼Bδ×𝑸δ⊂Bδ×𝑸d.\widehat{\zeta}_{\delta}^{Q}:Q_{\delta}:=B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{l}}\xrightarrow{\sim}B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}\subset B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}.
(5.16)
Take the GdG_{d}-equivariantization using the group embedding Gδ↪GdG_{\delta}\hookrightarrow G_{d}, we obtain a GdG_{d}-equivariant vector bundle
Qδ∼=Gd×GδQδ→Bδ∼Q_{\delta}^{\sim}=G_{d}\times_{G_{\delta}}Q_{\delta}\to B_{\delta}^{\sim}
which is GdG_{d}-equivariantly embedded in Bδ∼×𝑸dB_{\delta}^{\sim}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}.
Combined with the gluing map (5.11), we obtain a GdG_{d}-equivariant bundle embedding
(ζ^δQ)∼:Qδ∼→Qd|∂δBd(\widehat{\zeta}_{\delta}^{Q})^{\sim}:Q_{\delta}^{\sim}\to Q_{d}|_{\partial^{\delta}B_{d}}
covering the embedding ζδ∼:Bδ∼↪∂δBd\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}:B_{\delta}^{\sim}\hookrightarrow\partial^{\delta}B_{d}. Using the bundle Qˇδ∼\check{Q}_{\delta}^{\sim} from (5.14), we can stabilize ζδ∼\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim} by Qˇδ∼\check{Q}_{\delta}^{\sim} to get a diffeomorphism onto its image.
5.2.7. Normal complex structure
Here we prove the following result.
Proposition 5.27.
For each d≥1d\geq 1, (Bd×𝐐d)/Gd(B_{d}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d})/G_{d} is a normally complex orbifold with corners.
Proof.
We first re-examine the relation between BdB_{d} and 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d}. Define
𝑸d′:={A=[0∗0a]|a†=a}{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime}:=\left\{\left.A=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&*\\
0&a\end{array}\right]\ \right|\ a^{\dagger}=a\right\}
Then one can check that 𝑸d′≅𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime}\cong{\bm{Q}}_{d} as GdG_{d}-spaces where on both the GdG_{d} action is given by the conjugation. Then we define an “action” of 𝑸d′{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime} on BdB_{d} by
A⋅x↦(Id+1+A)​(x).A\cdot x\mapsto(I_{d+1}+A)(x).
It is easy to see that the 𝑸d′{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime}-orbit through xx is a local slice of the GdG_{d}-action. Suppose Γ⊂Gd\Gamma\subset G_{d} is a finite subgroup and x∈(Bd)Γx\in(B_{d})^{\Gamma}. Let 𝑸dΓ⊂𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\Gamma}\subset{\bm{Q}}_{d} be the Γ\Gamma-invariant subspace. Then we see that the 𝑸dΓ{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\Gamma}-orbit through xx is contained in the Γ\Gamma-fixed locus. We can check that locally it coincides with the fixed locus. Hence the normal direction to to the Γ\Gamma-fixed locus of BdB_{d} and the orthogonal complement of 𝑸dΓ{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\Gamma} are isomorphic representations of Γ\Gamma. Hence one can define a natural normal complex structure on (Bd×𝑸d)/Gd(B_{d}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d})/G_{d}.
∎
5.2.8. Abelian gauge theory on punctured spheres
We include certain simple facts about abelian gauge theory over the infinite cylinder.Let Θ=ℝ×S1\Theta={\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1} be the infinite cylinder, equipped with the standard flat Riemannian metric. Let (s,t)(s,t) be the standard coordinates where s∈ℝs\in{\mathbb{R}} and t∈S1t\in S^{1}. Consider an everywhere non-negative 2-form
Ω=σ⁡(s,t)​d​s∧d​t,σ⁡(s,t)≥0\Omega=\sigma(s,t)ds\wedge dt,\sigma(s,t)\geq 0
which decays exponentially on the ends: there exist a positive real number δ>0\delta>0 and a sequence of positive real numbers C0,C1,…C_{0},C_{1},\ldots such that
|∇lσ(s,t)|≤Cle−δ​|s|,l=0,1,….|\nabla^{l}\sigma(s,t)|\leq C_{l}e^{-\delta|s|},\ l=0,1,\ldots.
(5.17)
We assume that
∫ΘΩ=d∈ℤ≥0.\int_{\Theta}\Omega=d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}.
Lemma 5.28.
There exists a unitary connection A=AΩA=A_{\Omega} on the trivial line bundle L=Θ×ℂL=\Theta\times{\mathbb{C}}, unique up to gauge transformation, which satisfies the following conditions:
(1)
The curvature form of AΩA_{\Omega} is equal to −2​π​𝒊​Ω-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega.
(2)
The holonomy of AΩA_{\Omega} along any circle {s}×S1⊂Θ\{s\}\times S^{1}\subset\Theta, which is a well-defined element in U⁡(1)U(1), converges to the identity as s→±∞s\to\pm\infty.
Proof.
Choose two integers m−m_{-} and m+m_{+} such that d=m−−m+d=m_{-}-m_{+}. Choose a reference smooth connection A0A_{0} which is equal to d−𝒊​m±​d​td-{\bm{i}}m_{\pm}dt near ±∞\pm\infty. Consider an arbitrary connection of the form
A=A0+ϕ​d​s+ψ​d​t.A=A_{0}+\phi ds+\psi dt.
Then the curvature condition FA=−2​π​𝒊​ΩF_{A}=-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega and a gauge fixing condition induced by the trivial product connection give the equations
{∂ψ∂s−∂ϕ∂t=∗(−2π𝒊Ω−FA0),∂ϕ∂s+∂ψ∂t=0.\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial s}-\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}&=*(-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega-F_{A_{0}}),\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial s}+\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}&=0.\end{array}\right.
(5.18)
The left-hand-side is indeed the standard Cauchy–Riemann operator on f=ϕ+𝒊​ψf=\phi+{\bm{i}}\psi. Introduce a small ϵ>0\epsilon>0. Then the operator
∂∂z¯:W1,p,ϵ​(Θ)⊗ℂ→Lp,ϵ​(Θ)⊗ℂ\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}:W^{1,p,\epsilon}(\Theta)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\to L^{p,\epsilon}(\Theta)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
is a Fredholm operator with index −1-1 with trivial kernel and 1-dimensional cokernel spanned by a function with nonzero total integral. By the exponential decay property of Ω\Omega (see (5.17)), when ϵ<δ\epsilon<\delta, the right hand side of (5.18) belongs to Lp,ϵL^{p,\epsilon}. The choice of the reference connection A0A_{0} implies that the right hand side of (5.18) is in the image of the Cauchy–Riemann operator. Therefore there exists a unique solution f=ϕ+𝒊​ψf=\phi+{\bm{i}}\psi of class W1,p,ϵW^{1,p,\epsilon} to (5.18). Hence the existence is proved.
For uniqueness, one can see that any other solution A′A^{\prime} differs from AA by a closed 1-form. Up to gauge transformation, we can assume that the 1-form is a​d​tadt for a constant aa. Then the holonomy condition forces that a∈ℤa\in{\mathbb{Z}}, which is given by the effect of a gauge transformation.
∎
Now we would like to realize AA as certain singular connection on a degree dd holomorphic line bundle over ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1}. To this end, let Ld→ℂ​ℙ1L_{d}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1} be a degree dd holomorphic line bundle equipped with a Hermitian metric and let AdA_{d} be the Chern connection. Choosing A0A_{0} to be the same reference connection as in the proof of Lemma 5.28, then over Θ=ℂ​ℙ1∖{±∞}\Theta=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{\pm\infty\} there exists a trivialization of LdL_{d} such that
Ad=A0+ϕd​d​s+ψd​d​tA_{d}=A_{0}+\phi_{d}ds+\psi_{d}dt
such that ϕd\phi_{d}, ψd\psi_{d}, and all of their derivatives converge exponentially to 00 as s→±∞s\to\pm\infty. Moreover, the difference of curvature ΩAd−ΩA\Omega_{A_{d}}-\Omega_{A}, measured in cylindrical metric, decays exponentially like e−δ​|s|e^{-\delta|s|} with all derivatives. Then consider a general complex gauge transformation g=eh=eh′+𝒊​h′′g=e^{h}=e^{h^{\prime}+{\bm{i}}h^{\prime\prime}} where h′,h′′:Θ→ℝh^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}:\Theta\to{\mathbb{R}} are functions.
