Consider the equation (eh)∗​Ad=A(e^{h})^{*}A_{d}=A which is equivalent to
𝒊​d​h′′+𝒊∗d​h′=Ad−A{\bm{i}}dh^{\prime\prime}+{\bm{i}}*dh^{\prime}=A_{d}-A
which is equivalent to
{𝒊⁡(∂h′′∂s−∂h′∂t)=ϕd−ϕ,𝒊⁡(∂h′∂s+∂h′′∂t)=ψd−ψ.\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle{\bm{i}}\left(\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}}{\partial s}-\frac{\partial h^{\prime}}{\partial t}\right)&=\phi_{d}-\phi,\\
\displaystyle{\bm{i}}\left(\frac{\partial h^{\prime}}{\partial s}+\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}}{\partial t}\right)&=\psi_{d}-\psi.\end{array}\right.
(5.19)
This is again the standard Cauchy–Riemann operator on cylinder. If we regard the left-hand-side as a Fredholm operator from W1,p,ϵW^{1,p,\epsilon} to Lp,ϵL^{p,\epsilon}, then it has (complex) index −1-1 with the cokernel generated by a function whose total integral is nonzero. Now we allow hh to have nonzero limits at ±∞\pm\infty so that h−h⁡(±∞)h-h(\pm\infty) is of class W1,p,ϵW^{1,p,\epsilon} near the infinities. Then the Fredholm index becomes 11 with kernel being the subspace of constant functions. Therefore, there exists a solution to (5.19) unique up to adding a constant. We may then view AA as the Chern connection on LdL_{d} with respect to the Hermitian metric rescaled by e2​h′e^{2h^{\prime}}, which may be a singular metric on ℂ​ℙ1\mathbb{CP}^{1} but is smooth over the cylinder and continuous over the poles. We summarize these elementary discussions as the following lemma.
Lemma 5.29.
Given a 2-form Ω\Omega as in Lemma 5.28, there exists a continuous Hermitian metric on LdL_{d} whose Chern connection has curvature form equal to −2​π​𝐢​Ω-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega. Moreover two such Hermitian metrics differ by a constant.
We generalize the above lemma to the case of prestable cylinders. On a nodal curve, a smooth/holomorphic function or map means a continuous function or map whose pullback to the normalization is smooth/holomorphic. A smooth/holomorphic kk-form (with k≥1k\geq 1) means a collection of smooth/holomorphic kk-forms on its normalization. Then given a genus zero nodal curve Σ\Sigma with irreducible components Σ1,…,Σs\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{s} and integers d1,…,dsd_{1},\ldots,d_{s}, there exists a unique up to isomorphism smooth/holomorphic line bundle over Σ\Sigma whose restriction to Σi\Sigma_{i} has degree did_{i}. Moreover, the automorphism group of such a holomorphic line bundle is isomorphic to ℂ∗{\mathbb{C}}^{*}. Then Lemma 5.29 has the following corollary.
Corollary 5.30.
(cf. [AMS21, Lemma 6.8])
Let (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}) be a prestable cylinder and Ω\Omega be a smooth 2-form on Σ\Sigma. Suppose
(1)
the integration of Ω\Omega over each irreducible component is integral, and
(2)
over cylindrical components, Ω\Omega satisfies (5.17) for some common δ>0\delta>0.
Then there exists a holomorphic Hermitian line bundle on Σ\Sigma whose curvature form is −2​π​𝐢​Ω-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega. Moreover, this line bundle is unique up to isomorphism.
Under Hypothesis 4.1, suppose u:Σ→Xu:\Sigma\to X is a smooth map whose restriction to each cylindrical component converges to a periodic orbit of HH at ±∞\pm\infty in an exponential rate. Then consider the 2-form Ωu,H∈Ω2​(Σ)\Omega_{u,H}\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma) defined as follows:
Ωu,H:={u∗​ω on spherical componentsu∗​ω−d⁡(Ht​(u)​d​t) on cylindrical components.\Omega_{u,H}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\displaystyle u^{*}\omega&\text{ on spherical components}\\
\displaystyle u^{*}\omega-d(H_{t}(u)dt)&\text{ on cylindrical components.}\end{array}\right.
(5.20)
Then by Corollary 5.30 there is a unique Hermitian line bundle Lu→ΣL_{u}\to\Sigma if the integration of the 22-form Ωu,H\Omega_{u,H} is integral over each cylindrical component. In particular, each stable Floer trajectory induces a line bundle over its domain, thanks to Hypothesis 4.1. The desired integrality property of Ωu,H\Omega_{u,H} for stable Floer trajectories follows from the Stokes’ formula and the integrality of symplectic actions. Moreover, the Hermitian line bundle LuL_{u} has strictly positive curvature 22-forms over the nontrivial components of uu.
5.3. Global chart construction II
In this subsection, we provide a construction of a global Kuranishi chart for a single moduli space of Floer trajectories. The purpose is to showcase how to use the auxiliary moduli space Bp​qB_{pq} as a model for the Deligne–Mumford space in the Hamiltonian Floer theory which allows one to present the moduli space ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq} as a global quotient, and to present how to adapt the geometric regularization method from [AMS21] to this setting.
5.3.1. Description of the K-chart.
We first define the notion of framed curves in the case of Floer trajectories.
Definition 5.31.
(cf. [AMS21, Definition 6.10])
Given a moduli space ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq} of Floer trajectories and a stratum α=pr1⋯rlq\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q, denoting d=dp​qd=d_{pq}, a framed cylinder (of type α\alpha) is a tuple (u,Σ,F)(u,\Sigma,F) where
(1)
Σ\Sigma is a prestable cylinder with l+1l+1 horizontal levels (see Definition 5.12).
(2)
u:Σ→Mu:\Sigma\to M is a smooth map whose restriction to each cylindrical component converges to periodic orbits prescribed by the capped orbits p,r1,…,rl,qp,r_{1},\dots,r_{l},q at ±∞\pm\infty in an exponential rate, and the topological energy of each horizontal level is prescribed by (4.3). Moreover, the 22-form Ωu,H\Omega_{u,H} defined in Equation (5.20) is non-negative and is strictly positive on each unstable component of uu.
(3)
F=(f0,f1,…,fd)F=(f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}) is basis of global sections of the line bundle LuL_{u} constructed using the 22-form (5.20). Moreover, the induced holomorphic map
ιF:Σ→ℂ​ℙd,w↦[f0​(w),…,fd​(w)]\iota_{F}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},\ w\mapsto[f_{0}(w),\ldots,f_{d}(w)]
(5.21)
is a stable map, which represents a point [ιF]∈ℱ0,2​(d)[\iota_{F}]\in{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d). Denote by
ι~F:Σ→𝒞=𝒞0,2​(d)\tilde{\iota}_{F}:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)
to be the identification between Σ\Sigma and the fiber of the universal curve 𝒞0,2​(d)→ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d) over [ιF][\iota_{F}].1212
12
ι~F\tilde{\iota}_{F} is called a “domain map” in [AMS21].
Note that Condition (2) above guarantees the existence of the Hermitian line bundle Lu→ΣL_{u}\to\Sigma by Corollary 5.30 whose restriction to each unstable component has strictly positive degree. We can define the notion of isomorphisms of framed curves in an obvious way.
Definition 5.32.
An isomorphism from a framed cylinder (u,Σ,F)(u,\Sigma,F) to another framed cylinder (u′,Σ′,F′)(u^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},F^{\prime}) consists of an isomorphism φ:Σ→Σ′\varphi:\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\prime} of prestable cylinders such that u′∘φ=uu^{\prime}\circ\varphi=u and an isomorphism of holomorphic line bundles φ^:Lu→Lu′\widehat{\varphi}:L_{u}\to L_{u^{\prime}} which covers φ\varphi and which is an isometry up to a constant factor, such that fi′∘φ=φ^∘fif_{i}^{\prime}\circ\varphi=\widehat{\varphi}\circ f_{i} for all i=0,…,di=0,\ldots,d. Two framed cylinders are isomorphic if there exists an isomorphism between them. Notice that scaling the frame F=(f0,…,fd)F=(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d}) by a nonzero complex number produces an isomorphic framed curve.
Given a framed cylinder (u,Σ,F)(u,\Sigma,F) and an element g∈P​G​L​(d+1)g\in PGL(d+1), we can construct another framed cylinder in the following way. Observe that the sections f0,…,fdf_{0},\dots,f_{d} define an embedding Σ→ℂ​Pd\Sigma\to{\mathbb{C}P}^{d}, under which the line bundle LuL_{u} is the pullback of 𝒪⁡(1){\mathcal{O}}(1) and the basis (f0,…,fd)(f_{0},\dots,f_{d}) is obtained by pulling back the standard hyperplane sections of 𝒪⁡(1){\mathcal{O}}(1). As an element of the automorphism group of 𝒞​Pd{\mathcal{C}P}^{d}, the action of gg can be lifted to an automorphism of the line bundle 𝒪⁡(1){\mathcal{O}}(1). Therefore, gg takes the standard hyperplane sections of 𝒪⁡(1){\mathcal{O}}(1) to another basis of 𝒪⁡(1){\mathcal{O}}(1). Composing such an action with the embedding induced by (f0,…,fd)(f_{0},\dots,f_{d}) defines another framed cylinder and we denote it by
(u,Σ,g∗​F).(u,\Sigma,g_{*}F).
(5.22)
We construct a geometric thickening of a moduli space of Floer trajectories using framed cylinders. We choose the following data.
(1)
A Hermitian connection ∇T​M\nabla^{TM} on T​MTM with respect to the Hermitian metric induced from ω\omega and JJ satisfying the following condition: ∇T​M\nabla^{TM} is flat near all 1-periodic orbits (which are all embedded and disjoint by assumption) of HH and its holonomy along each such orbit is nondegenerate, i.e., does not have 11 as an eigenvalue.
(2)
For each moduli space ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}, a positive integer kk.1313
13
Later we will need kk to be sufficiently large to achieve transversality. We will also need to choose a list of integers instead of a single one to obtain a Kuranishi flow category.
The following definition introduces the thickening induced by one single line bundle. The discussion here is of expository nature, and the actual thickening we need involves multiple line bundles, as presented in detail in Section 5.3.3.
Definition 5.33 (Thickened moduli).
Fix a nonempty moduli space ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}. Abbreviate d=dp​qd=d_{pq}. We define the following objects.
(1)
The symmetry group Gp​qG_{pq} is Gdp​qG_{d_{pq}}.
(2)
The thickened moduli space Vp​qV_{pq} parametrizes isomorphism classes of quadruples of the form
(u,Σ,F,η)(u,\Sigma,F,\eta)
where
(a)
(u,Σ,F)(u,\Sigma,F) is a framed cylinder. Suppose it is of type α\alpha for some stratum α=pr1⋯rlq\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q.
(b)
The framing F=(f0,…,fd)F=(f_{0},\dots,f_{d}) satisfies
[f0(z−):⋯:fd(z−)]=[1:0:⋯:0].[f_{0}(z_{-}):\cdots:f_{d}(z_{-})]=[1:0:\cdots:0].
(c)
η\eta is an element of the vector space
H0​(Hom¯​(ιF∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,u∗​TM)⊗ιF∗​𝒪​(k))⊗ℂH0​(ιF∗​𝒪​(k))0¯.H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}}.
(5.23)
Here the first H0H^{0} is the kernel of the Cauchy–Riemann operator induced from the Hermitian connection ∇T​M\nabla^{TM} on T​MTM and the standard complex structure of ℂ​ℙd\mathbb{CP}^{d}; on the other hand, H0​(ιF∗​𝒪​(k))0H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0} is the space of holomorphic sections of ιF∗​𝒪​(k)→Σ\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\to\Sigma which vanish at the two marked points z−z_{-} and z+z_{+}.
The quadruple (u,Σ,F,η)(u,\Sigma,F,\eta) needs to satisfy the following perturbation of the Floer’s equation (cf. [AMS21, Equation (6.7)]): on each component of Σ\Sigma one has
∂¯J,H​u+⟨η⟩∘d​ιF=0,\overline{\partial}_{J,H}u+\langle\eta\rangle\circ d\iota_{F}=0,
(5.24)
where ∂¯J,H​u=(d​u−XH​(u))J0,1\overline{\partial}_{J,H}u=(du-X_{H}(u))_{J}^{0,1} in which XH​(u)∈Ω1​(Σ,u∗​T​M)X_{H}(u)\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,u^{*}TM) is defined by
XH​(u):={0 on spherical componentsd​t⊗XHt on cylindrical components,X_{H}(u):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\displaystyle 0&\text{ on spherical components}\\
\displaystyle dt\otimes X_{H_{t}}&\text{ on cylindrical components,}\end{array}\right.
and the map η↦⟨η⟩\eta\mapsto\langle\eta\rangle is induced from the Hermitian pairing on 𝒪⁡(k){\mathcal{O}}(k).
(3)
The obstruction bundle Ep​q→Vp​qE_{pq}\to V_{pq} is the direct sum
Ep​q=Op​q⊕Qp​q.E_{pq}=O_{pq}\oplus Q_{pq}.
Here
Qp​q:=Vp​q×𝑸dp​qQ_{pq}:=V_{pq}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{pq}}
and 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d} for all d≥1d\geq 1 was defined in (5.12); the fiber of Op​qO_{pq} at a point x∈Vp​qx\in V_{pq} represented by (u,Σ,F,η)(u,\Sigma,F,\eta) is
H0​(Hom¯​(ιF∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,u∗​TM)⊗ιF∗​𝒪​(k))⊗ℂH0​(ιF∗​𝒪​(k))0¯.H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}}.
(4)
The group Gp​qG_{pq} acts on Ep​q→Vp​qE_{pq}\to V_{pq} (on the left) as follows. Given a quadruple (u,Σ,F,η)(u,\Sigma,F,\eta) where F=(f0,f1,…,fd)F=(f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}) and g∈Gp​q⊂P​G​L​(d+1)g\in G_{pq}\subset PGL(d+1), define the the framed curve
(u,Σ,F′):=(u,Σ,g∗​F)(u,\Sigma,F^{\prime}):=(u,\Sigma,g_{*}F)
as from Equation (5.22). Moreover, gg induces linear isomorphisms
g:H0​(Hom¯​(ιF∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,u∗​TM)⊗ιF∗​𝒪​(k))→H0​(Hom¯​(ιF′∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,u∗​TX)⊗ιF′∗​𝒪​(k))g:H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))\to H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F^{\prime}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TX)\otimes\iota_{F^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))
and
g:H0​(ιF∗​𝒪​(k))0→H0​(ιF′∗​𝒪​(k))0.g:H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}\to H^{0}(\iota_{F^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}.
The action on the Qp​qQ_{pq}-component of the obstruction bundle is defined as
g⋅Q↦g​Q​g−1,g∈Gp​q,Q∈𝑸d.g\cdot Q\mapsto gQg^{-1},\ g\in G_{pq},\ Q\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}.
(5)
The Kuranishi map is
Sp​q:Vp​q→Ep​q[u,Σ,F,η]↦(η,Q⁡(u,Σ,F))\begin{split}S_{pq}:V_{pq}&\ \to E_{pq}\\
[u,\Sigma,F,\eta]&\ \mapsto(\eta,Q(u,\Sigma,F))\end{split}
where Q⁡(u,Σ,F)Q(u,\Sigma,F) the image of (d+1)×(d+1)(d+1)\times(d+1) Hermitian matrix whose (i,j)(i,j)-entry is
[∫Σ⟨fi,fj⟩​Ωu,H]∈𝑸d.\left[\int_{\Sigma}\langle f_{i},f_{j}\rangle\Omega_{u,H}\right]\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}.
It is easy to see that Q⁡(u,Σ,F)Q(u,\Sigma,F) only depends on the isomorphism class of the framed curve.
(6)
If [u,Σ,F,η]∈Sp​q−1​(0)[u,\Sigma,F,\eta]\in S_{pq}^{-1}(0), one can see that u:Σ→Mu:\Sigma\to M represents an element of ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}. Define the footprint map
ψ~p​q:Sp​q−1​(0)→ℳ¯p​q,[u,Σ,F,η]↦[u]\tilde{\psi}_{pq}:S_{pq}^{-1}(0)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq},\ [u,\Sigma,F,\eta]\mapsto[u]
which induces a continuous map
ψp​q:Sp​q−1(0)/Gp​q→ℳ¯.p​q\psi_{pq}:S_{pq}^{-1}(0)/G_{pq}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}.
Remark 5.34.
Our definition closely follows [AMS21]. However one difference is that in [AMS21], they used the obstruction space
H0​(Hom¯​(ι~F∗​T​𝒞,u∗​TM)⊗ι~F∗​ℒk)⊗H0​(ι~F∗​ℒk)¯H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\tilde{\iota}_{F}^{*}T{\mathcal{C}},u^{*}TM)\otimes\tilde{\iota}_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{k})\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\tilde{\iota}_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{k})}
where T​𝒞T{\mathcal{C}} is the tangent bundle of the universal curve 𝒞0,2​(d){\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d) and ℒ{\mathcal{L}} denotes a relatively ample line bundle over the universal family 𝒞0,2​(d)→ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d), and ι~F\tilde{\iota}_{F} is the domain map,
while we replace this space by ours (5.23).
Lemma 5.35.
ψp​q\psi_{pq} is a homeomorphism.
Proof.
See [AMS21, Lemma 6.14].
∎
Lemma 5.36.
For each x∈Sp​q−1​(0)x\in S_{pq}^{-1}(0), the stabilizer Gx⊂Gp​qG_{x}\subset G_{pq} is isomorphic to the stabilizer of ψp​q​(x)∈ℳ¯p​q\psi_{pq}(x)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}. Moreover, in a Gp​qG_{pq}-invariant open neighborhood of Sp​q−1​(0)S_{pq}^{-1}(0) every point has finite isotropy group.
Proof.
See the proof of [AMS21, Lemma 6.4].
∎
Therefore, we see that Definition 5.33 presents ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq} as a global quotient preserving the stabilizers. Now we can discuss about the regularity properties.
5.3.2. Transversality of the perturbed Floer equation
We review Gromov’s graph trick used in the specific setting of global Kuranishi charts in [AMS21]. Let (N,JN)(N,J_{N}) be an almost complex manifold and E→NE\to N be a Hermitian vector bundle with a Hermitian connection ∇E\nabla^{E}. Then using the decomposition T​E=Th​E⊕Tv​ETE=T^{h}E\oplus T^{v}E of the tangent bundle of the total space of EE into horizontal and vertical tangent bundles induced from ∇\nabla, there is an induced “product type” almost complex structure JEJ^{E} on EE by combining JNJ_{N} with the fiberwise complex structure on EE. Now consider a ℝ{\mathbb{R}}-linear bundle map
Ψ:E⊕T​N→T​N\Psi:E\oplus TN\to TN
satisfying
JN​(Ψ⁡(e,h))=−Ψ⁡(e,JN​(h))J_{N}(\Psi(e,h))=-\Psi(e,J_{N}(h))
(5.25)
and
Ψ⁡(e,Ψ⁡(e,h))=0.\Psi(e,\Psi(e,h))=0.
(5.26)
We define
Φ:Tv​E⊕Th​E→Tv​E⊕Th​E\Phi:T^{v}E\oplus T^{h}E\to T^{v}E\oplus T^{h}E
whose restriction at each e∈Ee\in E is
Φ⁡(v,h)=(v,h+Ψ⁡(e,h)).\Phi(v,h)=(v,h+\Psi(e,h)).
It is easy to check that
Φ−1​(v,h)=(v,h−Ψ⁡(e,h)).\Phi^{-1}(v,h)=(v,h-\Psi(e,h)).
Define an almost complex structure on EE by
JΨE=Φ∘JE∘Φ−1:T​E→T​E.J_{\Psi}^{E}=\Phi\circ J^{E}\circ\Phi^{-1}:TE\to TE.
More explicitly,
JΨE​(v,h)=(JE​v,JN​h+2​Ψ​(e,JN​h)).J_{\Psi}^{E}(v,h)=(J^{E}v,J_{N}h+2\Psi(e,J_{N}h)).
Now we consider the Cauchy–Riemann equation with respect to the sheared almost complex structure on the total space, with an additional Hamiltonian perturbation. Let Σ\Sigma be a Riemann surface with complex structure jj. Let X∈Ω0,1​(Σ,Vect⁡(N))X\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,{\rm Vect}(N)) be a perturbation. The Hermitian connection on EE induces a horizontal lift of XX on the total space, denoted by XEX^{E}. Then consider the Cauchy–Riemann equation for u~:Σ→E\tilde{u}:\Sigma\to E
∂¯JΨE​u~+XE​(u~)=0.\overline{\partial}_{J^{E}_{\Psi}}\tilde{u}+X^{E}(\tilde{u})=0.
If we write u~=(u,s)\tilde{u}=(u,s) where u:Σ→Nu:\Sigma\to N and s∈Γ⁡(u∗​E)s\in\Gamma(u^{*}E), then with respect to the horizontal-vertical decomposition of T​ETE, one has
d​u~=d​u⊕∇Esd\tilde{u}=du\oplus\nabla^{E}s
and
(d​u~)JΨE0,1=12​(d​u+JΨE∘d​u∘j)⊕(∇Es)0,1=(∂¯JN​u+X⁡(u)+Ψ⁡(s,JN∘d​u∘j))⊕(∇Es)0,1.\begin{split}(d\tilde{u})^{0,1}_{J^{E}_{\Psi}}=&\ \frac{1}{2}\left(du+J^{E}_{\Psi}\circ du\circ j\right)\oplus(\nabla^{E}s)^{0,1}\\
=&\ \left(\overline{\partial}_{J_{N}}u+X(u)+\Psi(s,J_{N}\circ du\circ j)\right)\oplus(\nabla^{E}s)^{0,1}.\end{split}
Lemma 5.37.
[AMS21, Lemma 6.18] Let Σ\Sigma be a Riemann surface with complex structure jj and u~=(u,s):Σ→E\tilde{u}=(u,s):\Sigma\to E be a smooth map. Then u~\tilde{u} corresonds to a smooth map u:=πE∘u~:Σ→Nu:=\pi_{E}\circ\tilde{u}:\Sigma\to N together with a section s∈Γ⁡(u∗​E)s\in\Gamma(u^{*}E). Under this correspondence, u~\tilde{u} is J~ΨE\tilde{J}_{\Psi}^{E}-holomorphic if and only if ss is a holomorphic (with respect to the (0,1)(0,1)-part of ∇\nabla) and uu satisfies
∂¯JN​u+X⁡(u)+Ψ⁡(s,JN∘d​u∘j)=0.∎\overline{\partial}_{J_{N}}u+X(u)+\Psi(s,J_{N}\circ du\circ j)=0.\qed
The geometric thickening revisited.
Recall that one has the smooth quasiprojective variety 𝒞=𝒞0,2​(d){\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d), which is the universal curve of a submanifold ℱ=ℱ0,2​(d)⊂ℳ¯0,2​(ℂ​ℙd,d){\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d) (after imposing the constraint at z−z_{-} by the point [1,0,…,0][1,0,\ldots,0]0. Denote by
univ:𝒞→ℱ{\rm univ}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{F}}
the canonical holomorphic projection map. Also denote by
ev:𝒞→ℂ​ℙd{\rm ev}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}
the evaluation map. Consider the almost complex manifold
N=M×𝒞N=M\times{\mathcal{C}}
equipped with the product almost complex structure JN=JM×J𝒞J_{N}=J_{M}\times J_{\mathcal{C}}. Using the projection pM:M×𝒞→Mp_{M}:M\times{\mathcal{C}}\to M, p𝒞:X×𝒞→𝒞p_{\mathcal{C}}:X\times{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}} and the evaluation map ev:𝒞→ℂ​ℙd{\rm ev}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}, define the vector bundles1616
16
Again, kk is chosen to be sufficiently large so that they are vector bundles
E0​(k):=Hom¯​((ev∘p𝒞)∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,pM∗​TM)⊗(ev∘p𝒞)∗​𝒪​(k),E1​(k):=p𝒞∗​univ∗​(univ∗​(ev∗​𝒪​(k)))0.\begin{split}E^{0}(k):=&\ \overline{\rm Hom}(({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},p_{M}^{*}TM)\otimes({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k),\\
E^{1}(k):=&\ p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}{\rm univ}^{*}({\rm univ}_{*}({\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)))_{0}.\end{split}
Here (univ∗​(ev∗​𝒪​(k)))0({\rm univ}_{*}({\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)))_{0} is the bundle whose fibers are fiberwise global sections of ev∗​𝒪​(k){\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k) which vanish at the two marked points. Define
E⁡(k):=E0​(k)⊗ℂE1​(k)¯.E(k):=E^{0}(k)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{E^{1}(k)}.
(5.27)
Before talking about the shearing map we also need to specify a Hermitian metric and Hermitian connection on EE. From the definition we see that all factors of EE has a natural Hermitian metric and connection except the bundle (univ∗​(ev∗​𝒪​(k)))0({\rm univ}_{*}({\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)))_{0} which only has a complex structure but no canonical Hermitian structure. We choose an arbitrary one which then induces a Hermitian metric and a Hermitian connection on EE. This choice does not affect the structures we are going to construct. It is only an auxiliary object to be used to show regularity. The regularity is independent of the choices.
The shearing map Ψ:E⊕T​N→T​N≅pM∗​T​M⊕p𝒞∗​T​𝒞\Psi:E\oplus TN\to TN\cong p_{M}^{*}TM\oplus p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}T{\mathcal{C}} is defined to be
Ψ⁡(η,v1,v2)=⟨η⟩​(v2)⊕0.\Psi(\eta,v_{1},v_{2})=\langle\eta\rangle(v_{2})\oplus 0.
(5.28)
Here the map ⟨η⟩\langle\eta\rangle is defined at each point y∈𝒞y\in{\mathcal{C}} (whose evaluation is z∈ℂ​ℙdz\in\mathbb{CP}^{d}) with a fiber 𝒞y⊂𝒞{\mathcal{C}}_{y}\subset{\mathcal{C}} the pairing between e∈𝒪⁡(k)|ze\in{\mathcal{O}}(k)|_{z} and the value of ζ∈H0​(𝒞y,ev∗​𝒪​(k)|𝒞y)0¯\zeta\in\overline{H^{0}({\mathcal{C}}_{y},{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)|_{{\mathcal{C}}_{y}})_{0}} using the Hermitian metric on 𝒪⁡(k){\mathcal{O}}(k), where we identify H0​(𝒞y,ev∗​𝒪​(k)|𝒞y)H^{0}({\mathcal{C}}_{y},{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)|_{{\mathcal{C}}_{y}}) with the space of global holomorphic sections on the curve 𝒞y{\mathcal{C}}_{y}.
Lift the Hamiltonian.
We need to lift the Hamiltonian vector field to the total space of E=E⁡(k)E=E(k). Let XHt∈Γ⁡(T​M)X_{H_{t}}\in\Gamma(TM) be the time tt Hamiltonian vector field on MM. Then it pulls back to a vector field on M×𝒞M\times{\mathcal{C}} which is zero in the T​𝒞T{\mathcal{C}}-direction. On the other hand, the Hermitian connection on EE induces a horizontal-vertical decomposition of T​ETE. By identifying the horizontal distribution with the pullback of the tangent bundle of the base, one can lift the vector field XHtX_{H_{t}} to the total space EE, which is denoted by X~Ht\tilde{X}_{H_{t}}. Notice that by the definition of EE and the choice of the Hermitian connection ∇T​M\nabla^{TM}, all 1-periodic orbits in the total space of EE are contained in the zero section and are exactly the same as the 1-periodic orbits of the original Hamiltonian on MM multiplied by the additional factor 𝒞{\mathcal{C}}.
An alternative description of the thickened moduli space.
Definition 5.38.
Let (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}) be a prestable cylinder. A smooth map u~:Σ→E\tilde{u}:\Sigma\to E is called a (J~Ψ,H)(\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H)-holomorphic map if it is J~Ψ\tilde{J}_{\Psi}-holomorphic on all sphere components and on each cylindrical components it satisfies the equation
∂u~∂s+J~Ψ​(∂u~∂t−X~Ht​(u~))=0.\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial s}+\tilde{J}_{\Psi}\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial t}-\tilde{X}_{H_{t}}(\tilde{u})\right)=0.
(5.29)
(This is the same as (5.24).)
Now we give another description of the thickened moduli space.
Definition 5.39.
Let ℳJ~Ψ,H{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H} be the moduli space of (J~Ψ,H)(\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H)-holomorphic maps u~\tilde{u} from a prestable cylinder (Σ,𝐋)(\Sigma,{\bf L}) to the total space of EE satisfying the following conditions. Write the projection of u~\tilde{u} to the base of EE, which is N=M×𝒞N=M\times{\mathcal{C}}, by (u,μ)(u,\mu).
(1)
μ:Σ→𝒞\mu:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}} is a domain map, i.e., it is an isomorphism onto a fiber of 𝒞{\mathcal{C}} which sends the marked points to the marked points.
(2)
u~\tilde{u} converges to periodic orbits of X~Ht\tilde{X}_{H_{t}} at cylindrical nodes. Moreover, its projection onto MM has the same homotopy type as elements in ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}.
(3)
For each component Σα⊂Σ\Sigma_{\alpha}\subset\Sigma, the degree of the restriction of the underlying map μ¯:Σ→ℂ​ℙd\underline{\smash{\mu}}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d} is equal to the degree determined by the restriction u:Σα→Mu:\Sigma_{\alpha}\to M.
Notice that there is also a Gp​qG_{pq}-action on ℳJ~Ψ,H{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}.
Now we compare the thickened moduli space Vp​qV_{pq} with ℳJ~Ψ,H{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}. Indeed, given any point of Vp​qV_{pq} represented by (Σ,u,F,η)(\Sigma,u,F,\eta), we can identify it with a stable (J~Ψ,H)(\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H)-holomorphic map u~=(u,μ,η)\tilde{u}=(u,\mu,\eta) where we just replace the frame FF by the induced map μ¯=ιF\underline{\smash{\mu}}=\iota_{F}. This map is clearly well-defined and GdG_{d}-equivariant.
Lemma 5.40.
(cf. [AMS21, Lemma 6.25] The natural map
Vp​q→ℳJ~Ψ,HV_{pq}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}
is an equivariant open embedding (of topological spaces).
∎
Therefore one can identify the obstruction bundle and the Kuranishi map as defined over the space ℳJ~Ψ,H{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H} after restricting to a Gp​qG_{pq}-invariant open subset.
Regularity.
Remember that the thickened moduli space depends on a choice of an integer kk. To emphasize the role of these integers, we temporarily denote
ℳJ~Ψ,H=ℳJ~Ψ,H​(k).{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}={\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k).
Theorem 5.41.
(cf. [AMS21, Corollary 6.27])
For a fixed p​qpq, there exists a positive integer kp​q>0k_{pq}>0 such that if k≥kp​qk\geq k_{pq}, then each element of Sp​q−1​(0)S_{pq}^{-1}(0) is a regular element of ℳJ~Ψ,H​(k){\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k), i.e., the linearization of (5.29) is surjective.
Proof.
Choose x∈Sp​q−1​(0)x\in S_{pq}^{-1}(0). Notice that this element can be viewed as a representative of ℳJ~Ψ,H​(k){\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k) for all k≥1k\geq 1. We will prove that for sufficiently large kk, xx is regular in ℳJ~Ψ,H​(k){\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k). By compactness of the moduli space ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}, one can find a common large kk which regularizes all elements of Sp​q−1​(0)S_{pq}^{-1}(0).
Suppose xx is represented by the framed cylinder (Σ,u,F,0)(\Sigma,u,F,0) where FF is a unitary frame of the line bundle Lu→ΣL_{u}\to\Sigma which is induced from the 2-form Ωu,H\Omega_{u,H} whose curvature form is equal to −2​π​𝒊​Ωu,H-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega_{u,H}. The existence of LuL_{u} is guaranteed by Corollary 5.30. Abbreviate d=dp​qd=d_{pq}. Denote the frame FF by
F:=(f0,…,fd).F:=(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d}).
Then the map
Σ→ℂ​ℙd,z↦[f0​(z),…,fd​(z)]\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},\ z\mapsto[f_{0}(z),\ldots,f_{d}(z)]
represents an element in ℱ0,2​(d){\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d). Therefore, the unitary frame FF induces a domain map μ:Σ→𝒞\mu:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}}. Notice that this construction is independent of kk. Now for any kk, denote (u,μ):Σ→M×𝒞(u,\mu):\Sigma\to M\times{\mathcal{C}} which together with the inclusion map of the zero section of u∗​E​(k)u^{*}E(k) gives an element in ℳJ~Ψ,H​(k){\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k).
We would like to show that the linearization of Equation (5.29) at xx is surjective when kk is sufficiently large. We only show its surjectivity over each component, while skipping the argument showing the surjectivity after imposing the matching conditions at nodes (which is the same as the case of pseudoholomorphic curves).
Over each component Σα⊂Σ\Sigma_{\alpha}\subset\Sigma, the deformation space of the map (u,μ,0)(u,\mu,0) splits as
W1,p​(Σα,μ∗​T​𝒞)⊕W1,p​(Σα,u∗​T​M)⊕W1,p​(Σα,(u,μ)∗​E​(k))W^{1,p}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\mu^{*}T{\mathcal{C}})\oplus W^{1,p}(\Sigma_{\alpha},u^{*}TM)\oplus W^{1,p}(\Sigma_{\alpha},(u,\mu)^{*}E(k))
and the linearization is of the block form
[Dμ000DuP00DE]\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}D_{\mu}&0&0\\
0&D_{u}&P\\
0&0&D_{E}\end{array}\right]
(5.30)
where the diagonal terms are the standard linearization of the Cauchy–Riemann equation (with or without Hamiltonian perturbation term, depending on whether the component is spherical or cylindrical), and the off-diagonal term PP is the perturbation of the T​MTM-direction (i.e. the inhomogeneous term of (5.24)). Notice that the operator DμD_{\mu} is always surjective, reflecting the fact that domain maps to 𝒞{\mathcal{C}} are always unobstructed. This is a classical fact due to the “convexity” of the bundle μ∗​T​𝒞\mu^{*}T{\mathcal{C}}. Hence one only needs to consider the lower-right 2×22\times 2-block.
We first consider a spherical component Σβ⊂Σ\Sigma_{\beta}\subset\Sigma where the Hamiltonian perturbation term is zero. If Σβ\Sigma_{\beta} has positive degree, then the line bundle μ¯∗​𝒪​(k)\underline{\smash{\mu}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k) is a positive line bundle on this component. When kk is sufficiently large, one can argue in the same way as the case of [AMS21] to show that the linearized operator is surjective, even after restricting to the subspace where the values at nodes vanish. On the other hand, if Σβ\Sigma_{\beta} is a ghost component. Then (u,μ)∗​E​(k)|Σβ(u,\mu)^{*}E(k)|_{\Sigma_{\beta}} is a trivial vector bundle equipped with the trivial Cauchy–Riemann operator. This is the same as the linearized operator in the T​MTM-direction. Hence the linear operator is surjective with kernels being constant sections.
Second, we consider a cylindrical component Σα⊂Σ\Sigma_{\alpha}\subset\Sigma. We identify Σα≅(−∞,+∞)×S1\Sigma_{\alpha}\cong(-\infty,+\infty)\times S^{1} with cylindrical coordinates (s,t)(s,t). Using the peak-section argument as in [AMS21], one can show that the T​MTM-direction is surjective. Hence we only need to show that the operator
DE:W1,p​((u,μ)∗​E​(k))→Lp​(Λ0,1⊗(u,μ)∗​E​(k))D_{E}:W^{1,p}((u,\mu)^{*}E(k))\to L^{p}(\Lambda^{0,1}\otimes(u,\mu)^{*}E(k))
is surjective. When Σα\Sigma_{\alpha} is a ghost component (i.e. a trivial cylinder), this surjectivity is obvious (its kernel is zero). When Σα\Sigma_{\alpha} is not a ghost component, we need to use the positivity of 𝒪⁡(k){\mathcal{O}}(k). One can show that the curvature of (u,μ)∗​E​(k)(u,\mu)^{*}E(k) is everywhere positive when kk is sufficiently large. Indeed, for S>0S>0 sufficiently large, u((−∞,−S]∪[S,+∞))u((-\infty,-S]\cup[S,+\infty)) is contained in a neighborhood of the union of periodic orbits where ∇T​M\nabla^{TM} is flat. Hence over the region where |s|≥S|s|\geq S, the curvature of (u,μ)∗​E​(k)(u,\mu)^{*}E(k) is pulled back from the universal curve 𝒞{\mathcal{C}}. Hence when kk is sufficiently large, the curvature is everywhere positive. Then by Lemma 5.42 below, DED_{E} is surjective on this component.
∎
Lemma 5.42.
Let E→ΘE\to\Theta be a Hermitian vector bundle and ∇\nabla be a Hermitian connection on EE whose curvature form is everywhere positive definite and whose limiting holonomy has no eigenvalue 11. Then the Cauchy–Riemann operator
∇0,1:W1,p​(Θ,E)→Lp​(Θ,Λ0,1⊗E)\nabla^{0,1}:W^{1,p}(\Theta,E)\to L^{p}(\Theta,\Lambda^{0,1}\otimes E)
is surjective.
Proof.
We prove by using the maximal principle. Suppose this is not the case. Then there exists ξ\xi in the kernel of the formal adjoint of ∇0,1\nabla^{0,1}. Using the local coordinate (s,t)(s,t) on Θ\Theta we write
∇=d+ϕ​d​s+ψ​d​t.\nabla=d+\phi ds+\psi dt.
Let the curvature form of ∇\nabla be Q​d​s​d​tQdsdt. Then one can identify ∇0,1\nabla^{0,1} with the operator
D0,1=(∂∂s+ϕ)+𝒊⁡(∂∂t+ψ).D^{0,1}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+\phi\right)+{\bm{i}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\psi\right).
Then its formal adjoint is −D1,0-D^{1,0}. As ξ∈Lq\xi\in L^{q}, the function |ξ|2|\xi|^{2} has a maximal point z0∈Θz_{0}\in\Theta. Then
0≥Δ​|ξ|2=∂∂z¯​∂∂z​|ξ|2=|D0,1​ξ|2+⟨ξ,D1,0​D0,1​ξ⟩=|D0,1​ξ|2+⟨ξ,Q​ξ⟩.0\geq\Delta|\xi|^{2}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|\xi|^{2}=|D^{0,1}\xi|^{2}+\langle\xi,D^{1,0}D^{0,1}\xi\rangle=|D^{0,1}\xi|^{2}+\langle\xi,Q\xi\rangle.
As QQ is everywhere positive, this is a contradiction.
∎
Lemma 5.43.
When kk is sufficiently large, the curvature of the connection on Hom¯​(ιF∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,u∗​TM)⊗ιF∗​𝒪​(k)\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k) is everywhere positive.
Proof.
The curvature is the pullback of the curvatures on the corresponding bundles on the target X×ℂ​ℙdX\times\mathbb{CP}^{d}. Notice that near ±∞\pm\infty, uM∗​T​Mu_{M}^{*}TM is flat. Therefore, the positivity of the curvature of 𝒪⁡(k){\mathcal{O}}(k) implies that the positivity of the curvature of the induced connection on Hom¯​(ιF∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,u∗​TM)⊗ιF∗​𝒪​(k)\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k) is everywhere positive for kk sufficiently large, because such a claim is true for the corresponding vector bundles on the target X×ℂ​ℙdX\times\mathbb{CP}^{d}.
∎
5.3.3. Multi-layered thickening
In Floer theory we need to deal with many moduli spaces simultaneously, and it is important to make sure that various structures on different moduli spaces fit together in a coherent way. The expected relations between different moduli spaces then require us to consider a more complicated thickening procedure which is referred to as the multi-layered thickening. Namely, instead of just considering the perturbation induced from one single relatively ample bundle ev∗​𝒪​(k)→𝒞\text{ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\to{\mathcal{C}}, we need to look at direct sum of such line bundles. This slight generalization of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith’s perturbation scheme will be used in the construction of the K-chart lift.
Fix a positive integer dd and abbreviate 𝒞=𝒞0,2​(d){\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d). Suppose we have a sequence of positive integers k1<k2<⋯<ksk_{1}<k_{2}<\cdots<k_{s}. We are going to define complex vector bundles inductively
E1′,E1,…,Es′,Es→M×𝒞=M×𝒞0,2​(dp​q).E_{1}^{\prime},E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime},E_{s}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}}=M\times{\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d_{pq}).
First, define
E1′:=Hom¯​((ev∘p𝒞)∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,pM∗​TM)⊗(ev∘p𝒞)∗​𝒪​(k1).E_{1}^{\prime}:=\overline{\rm Hom}(({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},p_{M}^{*}TM)\otimes({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{1}).
and
E1:=E1′⊗ℂuniv∗​(univ∗​ev∗​𝒪​(k1))0¯.E_{1}:=E_{1}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\rm univ}^{*}({\rm univ}_{*}{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{1}))_{0}}.
Inductively, suppose we have defined E1′,E1,…,Ei−1′,Ei−1E_{1}^{\prime},E_{1},\ldots,E_{i-1}^{\prime},E_{i-1}. Then define
Ei′:=Hom¯​((ev∘p𝒞)∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,pM∗​TM⊕⨁j=1i−1Ei)⊗(ev∘p𝒞)∗​𝒪​(ki)E_{i}^{\prime}:=\overline{\rm Hom}\Big(({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},p_{M}^{*}TM\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{i-1}E_{i}\Big)\otimes({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i})
and
Ei:=Ei′⊗ℂuniv∗​(univ∗​ev∗​𝒪​(ki))0¯.E_{i}:=E_{i}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{{\rm univ}^{*}({\rm univ}_{*}{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i}))_{0}}.
(5.31)
Notice that once T​MTM is equipped with a Hermitian connection (and hence a Cauchy–Riemann operator). Together with the holomorphic structure of the involved bundles over 𝒞{\mathcal{C}}, the connection on T​MTM induces a Cauchy–Riemann operator on EiE_{i}.
Remark 5.44.
We explain the above construction for the simplest nontrivial example E2E_{2}. The total space of E1→M×𝒞E_{1}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}} is an almost complex manifold which has a projection p𝒞:E1→𝒞p_{\mathcal{C}}:E_{1}\to{\mathcal{C}}. The goal is to construct certain perturbations of a (perturbed) JJ-holomorphic map
u:Σ→E1u:\Sigma\to E_{1}
such that p𝒞∘u:Σ→𝒞p_{\mathcal{C}}\circ u:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}} is a domain map, which in particular is holomorphic. Note that
(d​u)J0,1∈Hom¯​(T​Σ,u∗​TE1)=Hom¯​(T​Σ,u∗​TM⊕u∗​T​𝒞⊕u∗​E1),(du)^{0,1}_{J}\in\overline{\rm Hom}(T\Sigma,u^{*}TE_{1})=\overline{\rm Hom}(T\Sigma,u^{*}TM\oplus u^{*}T{\mathcal{C}}\oplus u^{*}E_{1}),
where the equality is induced from a Hermitian connection on E1E_{1}. Therefore, to keep the map p𝒞∘up_{\mathcal{C}}\circ u being a domain map, the perturbation should take value in Hom¯​(T​Σ,u∗​TM⊕u∗​E1)\overline{\rm Hom}(T\Sigma,u^{*}TM\oplus u^{*}E_{1}). The vector bundle E2→M×𝒞E_{2}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}} is constructed just as the construction of E1→M×𝒞E_{1}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}} in order to host an equation of the form (5.24), with the assistance of the relatively ample line bundle ev∗​𝒪​(k2)→𝒞{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{2})\to{\mathcal{C}}.
Definition 5.45 (Multi-layered thickening).
For any pair p,qp,q of capped periodic orbits, denote d=dp​qd=d_{pq}. Let Vp​qd=Vp​q​(k1,…,kd)V_{pq}^{d}=V_{pq}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}) be the moduli space of tuples
(Σ,u,F,η1,…,ηd)(\Sigma,u,F,\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d})
where Σ\Sigma is a prestable cylinder, u:Σ→Mu:\Sigma\to M is a smooth map with topological energy dd converging to pp and qq at the two marked points, FF is a frame of the line bundle Lu,Ω,H→ΣL_{u,\Omega,H}\to\Sigma inducing a holomorphic map ιF:Σ→ℂ​ℙd\iota_{F}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d} such that the framing F=(f0,…,fd)F=(f_{0},\dots,f_{d}) satisfies [f0(z−):⋯:fd(z−)]=[1:0:⋯:0][f_{0}(z_{-}):\cdots:f_{d}(z_{-})]=[1:0:\cdots:0], and the corresponding domain map ι~F:Σ→𝒞\tilde{\iota}_{F}:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}}, and (denoting by u~=(u,ι~F):Σ→M×𝒞\tilde{u}=(u,\tilde{\iota}_{F}):\Sigma\to M\times{\mathcal{C}})
ηi∈u~∗​Ei=Γ⁡(Hom¯​(ιF∗​T​ℂ​ℙd,u∗​TM⊕⨁j=1i−1u~∗​Ej)⊗ιF∗​𝒪​(ki))⊗H0​(ιF∗​𝒪​(ki))0¯\eta_{i}\in\tilde{u}^{*}E_{i}=\Gamma\left(\overline{\rm Hom}\big(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{i-1}\tilde{u}^{*}E_{j}\big)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i})\right)\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i}))_{0}}
satisfying the following equations
{∂¯J,H​u+πu∗​T​M​(∑i=1d⟨ηi⟩∘d​ιF)=0,∂¯​ηi+πEi​(∑j=i+1d⟨ηj⟩∘d​ιF)=0,i=1,…,d.\left\{\begin{aligned} \overline{\partial}_{J,H}u+\pi_{u^{*}TM}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\langle\eta_{i}\rangle\circ d\iota_{F}\right)&\ =0,\\
\overline{\partial}\eta_{i}+\pi_{E_{i}}\left(\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\langle\eta_{j}\rangle\circ d\iota_{F}\right)&\ =0,\ i=1,\ldots,d.\end{aligned}\right.
(5.32)
Remark 5.46.
Continuing Remark 5.44, we explain the origin of the above equations for d=2d=2. Note that the pair (u~,η)(\tilde{u},\eta) defines a smooth map u~1:Σ→E1\tilde{u}_{1}:\Sigma\to E_{1}. The purpose is to write down a perturbation of certain ∂¯\overline{\partial}-equation for u~1\tilde{u}_{1} using a section of η2∈u~∗​E2\eta_{2}\in\tilde{u}^{*}E_{2}, which could be schematically written as
∂¯J~Ψ,H​u1+⟨η2⟩∘d​ιF=0,\overline{\partial}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}u_{1}+\langle\eta_{2}\rangle\circ d\iota_{F}=0,
where J~Ψ\tilde{J}_{\Psi} is the sheared almost complex structure constructed using (5.28) and u1u_{1} is obtained from u~1\tilde{u}_{1} by forgetting the domain map. Then Equation (5.32) is obtained by projecting the above equation using πu∗​T​M\pi_{u^{*}TM} and πE1\pi_{E_{1}}. For general d≥2d\geq 2, one just need to carry out this construction iteratively.
Similar to the case with a single thickening bundle, we have a regularity result for the above defined multi-layered thickened moduli space. The details are given in Subsection 5.3.4. We remark that the Fredholm model for this thickened moduli space depends on certain choices which may not be canonical. However, being regular or not is a condition independent of these choices. As in Definition 5.33, Vp​qdV_{pq}^{d} is part of the data of a K-chart.
Obstruction bundle The first part of the obstruction bundle has its fiber over a point represented by (Σ,u,F,η)(\Sigma,u,F,\eta) the vector space
{ζ=(ζ1⋮ζd)|ζi∈Γ(Hom¯(ιF∗Tℂℙd,u∗TM⊕⨁j=1i−1u~∗Ej)⊗ιF∗𝒪(ki)⊗H0​(ιF∗​𝒪​(ki))0¯,∂¯​ζi+πEi​(∑j=i+1d⟨ζj⟩∘d​ιF)=0}.\left\{\zeta=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\zeta_{d}\end{array}\right)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{i}\in\Gamma\left(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM\oplus\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j=1}^{i-1}\tilde{u}^{*}E_{j}\right)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i})\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i}))_{0}},\\
\overline{\partial}\zeta_{i}+\pi_{E_{i}}\left(\displaystyle\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\langle\zeta_{j}\rangle\circ d\iota_{F}\right)=0\end{array}\right.\right\}.
(5.33)
It is standard that it is finite-dimensional. We denote this bundle by
Op​q=Op​q(d)→Vp​q.O_{pq}=O_{pq}^{(d)}\to V_{pq}.
On the other hand, as before, the second factor of the obstruction bundle is the trivial bundle Qp​q:=QdQ_{pq}:=Q_{d} whose fiber 𝑸d{\bm{Q}}_{d} is defined by (5.12). Let Qp​q→Vp​qQ_{pq}\to V_{pq} be the trivial bundle. Note that from its definition, Qp​qQ_{pq} is only a fiber bundle with a canonical smooth section, where the section corresponds to the element in QdQ_{d} represented by the identity matrix. However, using the vector space 𝑸d∗{\bm{Q}}^{*}_{d} defined by (5.13), the bundle Qp​qQ_{pq} can be endowed with a vector bundle structure. We switch between these two viewpoints in different contexts. We define
Ep​q=Op​q⊕Qp​q→Vp​q.E_{pq}=O_{pq}\oplus Q_{pq}\to V_{pq}.
Moreover, similar to the single-layered case, there is a Gp​qG_{pq}-action on Vp​qV_{pq} as well as on the bundles Op​qO_{pq} and Qp​qQ_{pq} making Ep​qE_{pq} an equivariant vector bundle.
Properties of obstruction bundle. There are many structural facts about the obstruction bundles which will play important roles in the construction of the K-chart lift of the Hamiltonian Floer flow category. We summarize them here. First, recall that the thickening depends on the choice of the sequence k1<⋯<kdk_{1}<\cdots<k_{d}. We (will) fix an increasing sequence {ki}i=1∞\{k_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} such that for all p​qpq, the thickening Vp​q​(k1,…,kdp​q)V_{pq}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d_{pq}}) is regular (see Proposition 5.49). However, we can have higher obstruction spaces. Given d′>d≥dp​qd^{\prime}>d\geq d_{pq}, one can construct thickenings Vp​q​(k1,…,kd)V_{pq}(k_{1},\dots,k_{d}) and Vp​q​(k1,…,kd′)V_{pq}(k_{1},\dots,k_{d^{\prime}}), over which live the two vector bundles
Op​q(d)→Vp​q​(k1,…,kd),\displaystyle\ O_{pq}^{(d)}\to V_{pq}(k_{1},\dots,k_{d}),\
Op​q(d′)→Vp​q​(k1,…,kd′).\displaystyle\ O_{pq}^{(d^{\prime})}\to V_{pq}(k_{1},\dots,k_{d^{\prime}}).
As we use sections with higher kik_{i} labels to perturb sections with lower kik_{i} labels, there is a natural inclusion
Op​q(d)↪Op​q(d′)O_{pq}^{(d)}\hookrightarrow O_{pq}^{(d^{\prime})}
(5.34)
covering the natural inclusion Vp​q​(k1,…,kd)↪Vp​q​(k1,…,kd′)V_{pq}(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\hookrightarrow V_{pq}(k_{1},\dots,k_{d^{\prime}})
such that when d<d′<d′′d<d^{\prime}<d^{\prime\prime}, the following diagram commutes
Op​q(d) Op​q(d′) Op​q(d′′) .\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 12.40923pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.40923pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{O_{pq}^{(d)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.40923pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 87.93935pt\raise-5.93481pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 36.40923pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{O_{pq}^{(d^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.93254pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.93254pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{O_{pq}^{(d^{\prime\prime})}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}.
Then we can define the direct limit of the obstruction bundles as
Op​q∞:=limd→∞Op​q(d).O_{pq}^{\infty}:=\lim_{d\to\infty}O_{pq}^{(d)}.
(5.35)
The obstruction bundle also splits over each boundary stratum. Let α=pr1⋯rlq∈𝔸p​qFloer\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer} and let ∂αVp​q⊂Vp​q\partial^{\alpha}V_{pq}\subset V_{pq} be the subspace of the thickened moduli space which consists of elements such that the underlying map (u,Σ)(u,\Sigma) is of type α\alpha (see Definition 5.31). Then one has a direct sum decomposition
Op​q(d)|∂αVp​q≅Op​q;p​r1(d)⊕⋯⊕Op​q;rl​q(d)O_{pq}^{(d)}|_{\partial^{\alpha}V_{pq}}\cong O_{pq;pr_{1}}^{(d)}\oplus\cdots\oplus O_{pq;r_{l}q}^{(d)}
(5.36)
where the fiber of Op​q;ri​ri+1(d)O_{pq;r_{i}r_{i+1}}^{(d)} over the point [Σ,u,F,η][\Sigma,u,F,\eta] is
{ζ=(ζ1,…,ζd)T|supp​ζ⊂Σri​ri+1⊂Σ},\left\{\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{d})^{T}\ |\ {\rm supp}\zeta\subset\Sigma_{r_{i}r_{i+1}}\subset\Sigma\right\},
where we use the convention that r0=pr_{0}=p and rl+1=qr_{l+1}=q.
(5.36) is a direct sum because elements of Op​q(d)O_{pq}^{(d)} necessarily vanish at nodes connecting cylindrical components.
Kuranishi section. Define
Sp​qd:Vp​q→Ep​q[Σ,u,F,η]↦(η,HF)\begin{split}S^{d}_{pq}:V_{pq}&\ \to E_{pq}\\
[\Sigma,u,F,\eta]&\ \mapsto(\eta,H_{F})\end{split}
where HF∈QdH_{F}\in Q_{d} is represented by the Hermitian matrix whose entries are
∫Σ⟨fi,fj⟩Lu,Ω,H​Ωu,\int_{\Sigma}\langle f_{i},f_{j}\rangle_{L_{u,\Omega,H}}\Omega_{u},
where f0,…,fdf_{0},\dots,f_{d} is a basis representing the frame FF.
It is also the same as the single-layered case that Sp​qdS^{d}_{pq} is Gp​qG_{pq}-equivariant,
Therefore, one obtains a quadruple
Kp​q=(Gp​q,Vp​q,Ep​q,Sp​q):=(Gp​q,Vp​qd,Ep​qd,Sp​qd)K_{pq}=(G_{pq},V_{pq},E_{pq},S_{pq}):=(G_{pq},V^{d}_{pq},E^{d}_{pq},S^{d}_{pq})
(5.37)
which is a candidate for a global Kuranishi chart on ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}. Still, there is a canonical map
Sp​q−1​(0)/Gp​q→ℳ¯p​qS_{pq}^{-1}(0)/G_{pq}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
which defines a homeomorphism onto its image.
5.3.4. Regularity for the multi-layered thickening
Now we prove the regularity of the multi-layered thickening. First we transform the description of the thickened moduli space to a moduli space where one can use Gromov’s graph trick. Recall that one has the bundles E1,…,Ed→M×𝒞dE_{1},\ldots,E_{d}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}}_{d}. Then one can define the tower of bundles
ℰd→ℰd−1→⋯→ℰ1→M×𝒞d{\mathcal{E}}_{d}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{d-1}\to\cdots\to{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
where each ℰi{\mathcal{E}}_{i} is the total space of the pullback of Ei→M×𝒞dE_{i}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}}_{d} onto the previous ℰi→M×𝒞d{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\to M\times{\mathcal{C}}_{d}. Then ℰi{\mathcal{E}}_{i} is still a complex vector bundle over M×𝒞dM\times{\mathcal{C}}_{d}. To proceed we would like to equip each ℰi{\mathcal{E}}_{i} with a Hermitian metric and a Hermitian connection. From the construction one can see that all the ingredients in constructing these bundles have a natural Hermitian structure except for the bundle (univ∗​ev∗​𝒪​(ki))0({\rm univ}_{*}{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i}))_{0}, which is the bundle of fiberwise global sections of the holomorphic line bundle ev∗​𝒪​(ki){\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i}) that vanish at the markings z−z_{-} and z+z_{+}. We choose an arbitrary smooth Hermitian metric on univ∗​ev∗​𝒪​(ki){\rm univ}_{*}{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k_{i}). Then each ℰi{\mathcal{E}}_{i} has an induced Hermitian metric. Moreover, together with the Chern connection on all involved holomorphic vector bundles and the chosen Hermitian metric on T​MTM, ℰi{\mathcal{E}}_{i} is equipped with a Hermitian connection.
Now we inductively define a sheared almost complex structure on the total space of ℰi{\mathcal{E}}_{i} similar to the constructions in Section 5.3.2. The details are almost identical and omitted. We denote the resulting almost complex structure on ℰd{\mathcal{E}}_{d} by JΨJ_{\Psi}.
Theorem 5.47.
For a given list k1<⋯<kd−1k_{1}<\cdots<k_{d-1} of positive integers. There exists k∗>0k^{*}>0 such that for all kd≥k∗k_{d}\geq k^{*}, for all pairs p​qpq with dp​q≤dd_{pq}\leq d, all elements of Sp​q−1​(0)S_{pq}^{-1}(0) are regular elements of ℳJΨ~,H​(k1,…,kd){\mathcal{M}}_{J_{\tilde{\Psi}},H}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}).
Proof.
The proof is essentially the same as the single-layered case. Indeed, we can replace the almost complex manifold (M,J)(M,J) by the pair (ℰd−1,Jd−1)({\mathcal{E}}_{d-1},J_{d-1}) where Jd−1J_{d-1} is the sheared almost complex structure on the total space ℰd−1{\mathcal{E}}_{d-1} lifted via the chosen connections. Then we can proceed by writing the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator in the block form as in (5.30). As long as kdk_{d} is sufficiently positive, we can make the linearization of the first equation in (5.32) surjective by the peak section argument from [AMS21, Proposition 6.26]. For the surjectivity of the linearization of the second part in (5.32), apply Lemma 5.42 and Lemma 5.43.
∎
Lemma 5.48.
Each fiber of the natural forgetful map
πp​q:Vp​q→Bp​q\pi_{pq}:V_{pq}\to B_{pq}
(5.38)
which takes a representative (Σ,u,F,η1,…,ηd)(\Sigma,u,F,\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d}) to the equivalence class of the holomorphic map
ιF:Σ→ℂ​Pd\iota_{F}:\Sigma\to{\mathbb{C}P}^{d}
has a canonical structure of smooth manifold and the restriction of the obstruction bundle Op​q(d)O_{pq}^{(d)} to each fiber is canonically a smooth vector bundle. Moreover, each g∈Gp​qg\in G_{pq} induces diffeomorphisms between fibers and smooth isomorphisms between the fiberwise restrictions of the obstruction bundle.
Proof.
As the domain curve of elements in each fiber of πp​q\pi_{pq} is fixed, a fiber of πp​q\pi_{pq} is the zero locus of a smooth Fredholm section of a Banach vector bundle. The regularity along the vertical direction implies that the fibers are smooth because the complex structure on the domain is unchanged. For the same reason the restriction of Op​q(d)O_{pq}^{(d)} to each fiber is smooth. Lastly, if g∈Gp​qg\in G_{pq}, the gg induces a smooth identification between two Banach vector bundles which intertwines with the Fredholm sections.
∎
In fact, the forgetful map πp​q:Vp​q→Bp​q\pi_{pq}:V_{pq}\to B_{pq} is a Gp​qG_{pq}-equivariant map between stratified spaces, where the underlying map between posets is the same as (5.2). Lemma 5.48 says that this map further has a fiberwise smooth structure. However, Lemma 5.48 does not assert that the locally closed stratum ∂αV̊p​q\partial^{\alpha}\mathring{V}_{pq} has a smooth structure. Indeed, the 𝔸d{\mathbb{A}}_{d}-stratification on BdB_{d} ignores the appearance of sphere bubbles. More refined structures of πp​q\pi_{pq} will be explored in Section 6.
5.4. Global chart construction III
5.4.1. Inductive construction of the thickened moduli spaces
Proposition 5.49.
There exists an increasing sequence of positive integers
k1<k2<⋯<kd<⋯k_{1}<k_{2}<\cdots<k_{d}<\cdots
which satisfies the following condition: for each d≥1d\geq 1, each p​qpq with dp​q≤dd_{pq}\leq d, the thickened moduli space Vp​q​(k1,…,kd)V_{pq}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}) is regular near the zero locus of the Kuranishi map.
Proof.
This statement can be proved by induction on dp​qd_{pq}, combining the proof for the case of a single multi-layered thickening in Theorem 5.47.
∎
Remark 5.50.
One can choose the sequence k1,k2,…k_{1},k_{2},\ldots such that the corresponding thickening of PSS{\rm PSS}, SSP{\rm SSP}, pearl{\rm pearl}, and the homotopy moduli spaces are all regular (see Section 7).
From now on we fix the sequence k1<k2<⋯k_{1}<k_{2}<\cdots which satisfies the conditions of Proposition 5.49. By shrinking to an open neighborhood of Sp​q−1​(0)S_{pq}^{-1}(0) inside Vp​qV_{pq}, one obtains a global Kuranishi chart. Just as Lemma 5.48, there are also the natural forgetful maps
πp​q:Vp​q→Bp​q.\pi_{pq}:V_{pq}\to B_{pq}.
These maps should be thought of as the natural forgetful map which takes a JJ-holomorphic curve to its moduli parameter in the Deligne–Mumford space.
5.4.2. Product construction: the case of two factors
To obtain a K-chart lifting, one needs to define embeddings of products of charts into corresponding boundary strata of a bigger chart. We call the definition of such embeddings the product construction. This construction needs to be compatible with the product construction of the auxiliary moduli spaces BdB_{d}.
We first consider the case of two factors. Consider a nonempty codimension one stratum ℳ¯p​r​q⊂ℳ¯p​q\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{prq}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}. We would like to define an equivariant map
ϑp​r​q:Vp​r×Vr​q→∂p​r​qVp​q\vartheta_{prq}:V_{pr}\times V_{rq}\to\partial^{prq}V_{pq}
which lifts the map ζp​r​q\zeta_{prq} (see (5.6) and (5.10)). Suppose a point x~p​r∈Vp​r\tilde{x}_{pr}\in V_{pr} is represented by a quadruple (Σp​r,up​r,Fp​r,ηp​r)(\Sigma_{pr},u_{pr},F_{pr},\eta_{pr}) and a point x~r​q∈Vr​q\tilde{x}_{rq}\in V_{rq} is represented by a quadruple (Σr​q,ur​q,Fr​q,ηr​q)(\Sigma_{rq},u_{rq},F_{rq},\eta_{rq}). Let xp​r∈Bp​rx_{pr}\in B_{pr}, xr​q∈Br​qx_{rq}\in B_{rq} be the underlying stable maps into projective spaces. Then using the map ζp​r​q:Bp​r×Br​q→∂p​r​qBp​q\zeta_{prq}:B_{pr}\times B_{rq}\to\partial^{prq}B_{pq} one can define a point
xp​r​q:=ζp​r​q​(xp​r,xr​q)x_{prq}:=\zeta_{prq}(x_{pr},x_{rq})
which can be represented by a stable cylinder μ¯p​r​q:Σp​r​q→ℂ​ℙdp​q\underline{\smash{\mu}}_{prq}:\Sigma_{prq}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}}. Moreover, there is a natural framing
Fp​r​q=(f0,…,fdp​q)F_{prq}=(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d_{pq}})
which lifts up​r​qu_{prq} defined as follows. Let Lp​r​q→Σp​r​qL_{prq}\to\Sigma_{prq} be the pullback of 𝒪⁡(1)→ℂ​ℙdp​q{\mathcal{O}}(1)\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}} by up​r​qu_{prq} which carries the pullback of the Fubini–Study metric. Then the map up​r​qu_{prq} can be represented by a basis
f0,…,fdp​qf_{0},\ldots,f_{d_{pq}}
of H0​(Lp​r​q)H^{0}(L_{prq}) which is well-defined up to a ℂ∗{\mathbb{C}}^{*}-factor. Moreover, the MM-factors of up​ru_{pr} and ur​qu_{rq} naturally define a map
up​r​q:Σp​r​q→Mu_{prq}:\Sigma_{prq}\to M
which converges to the correct periodic orbits at z±z_{\pm}. Lastly, one needs to define the combined vector ηp​r​q\eta_{prq}.
We first describe the single-layered case. The map up​r​q:Σp​r​q→ℂ​ℙdp​qu_{prq}:\Sigma_{prq}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}} induces linear embeddings
ℂ​ℙdp​r≅Yp​r⊂ℂ​ℙdp​q,\displaystyle\ \mathbb{CP}^{d_{pr}}\cong Y_{pr}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}},\
ℂ​ℙdr​q≅Yr​q⊂ℂ​ℙdp​q.\displaystyle\ \mathbb{CP}^{d_{rq}}\cong Y_{rq}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}}.
(5.39)
Indeed, using the notations similar to (5.2.5), the first embedding is
[z0,…,zdp​r]↦[z0,…,zdp​r,0,…,0⏟dr​q][z_{0},\ldots,z_{d_{pr}}]\mapsto[z_{0},\ldots,z_{d_{pr}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d_{rq}}]
and the second embedding is
[z0,…,zdr​q]↦[a0​z0,…,adp​r​z0,z1,…,zdr​q].[z_{0},\ldots,z_{d_{rq}}]\mapsto[a_{0}z_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{pr}}z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{d_{rq}}].
Here (a0,…,adp​r)∈ℂdp​r+1(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{pr}})\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{pr}+1} is the normalized evaluation of the first component at z+z_{+}. Over these embeddings there are the natural bundle maps

By definition,
ηp​r∈H0​(Hom¯​(ιFpr∗​T​ℂ​ℙdpr,u∗​TM)⊗ιFpr∗​𝒪​(k))⊗H0​(ιFp​r∗​𝒪​(k))0¯.\eta_{pr}\in H^{0}\left(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pr}},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\right)\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}}.
Notice that there are natural linear inclusions
H0​(ιFp​r∗​𝒪​(k))0↪H0​(ιFp​r​q∗​𝒪​(k))0,\displaystyle\ H^{0}(\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}\hookrightarrow H^{0}(\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0},\

defined by extending by zero. Moreover, the linear embeddings (5.39) induce bundle maps

These are defined by extending elements in H​o​mHom by zero in the normal directions to Yp​rY_{pr} resp. Yr​qY_{rq}. As elements of H0​(Hom¯​(ιFpr∗​T​ℂ​ℙdpr,u∗​TM)⊗ιFpr∗​𝒪​(k))H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pr}},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)) vanish at nodes (because of the holonomy of ∇T​M\nabla^{TM}), one naturally has the linear inclusions
H0​(Hom¯​(ιFpr∗​T​ℂ​ℙdpr,upr∗​TM)⊗ιFpr∗​𝒪​(k))→H0​(Hom¯​(ιFprq∗​T​ℂ​ℙdpq,uprq∗​TM)⊗ιFprq∗​𝒪​(k))H^{0}\left(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pr}},u_{pr}^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F_{pr}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\right)\to H^{0}\left(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}},u_{prq}^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\right)
and
H0​(Hom¯​(ιFrq∗​T​ℂ​ℙdrq,urq∗​TM)⊗ιFrq∗​𝒪​(k))→H0​(Hom¯​(ιFprq∗​T​ℂ​ℙdpq,uprq∗​TM)⊗ιFprq∗​𝒪​(k)).H^{0}\left(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F_{rq}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d_{rq}},u_{rq}^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F_{rq}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\right)\to H^{0}\left(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}},u_{prq}^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\right).
Therefore, by taking the sum of the images of ηp​r\eta_{pr} and ηr​q\eta_{rq}, one obtains an element
ηp​r​q∈H0​(Hom¯​(ιFprq∗​T​ℂ​ℙdpq,uprq∗​TM)⊗ιFprq∗​𝒪​(k))⊗H0​(ιFp​r​q∗​𝒪​(k))0¯.\eta_{prq}\in H^{0}\left(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}},u_{prq}^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\right)\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F_{prq}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}}.
Consider the quadruple (Σp​r​q,up​r​q,Fp​r​q,ηp​r​q)(\Sigma_{prq},u_{prq},F_{prq},\eta_{prq}). One can check that this is a framed cylinder of type p​r​qprq which represents an element x~p​r​q\tilde{x}_{prq} in the thickened moduli space Vp​r​qV_{prq}. It is also straightforward to see that we just defined a continuous embedding
ϑp​r​q:Vp​r×Vr​q→∂p​r​qVp​q\vartheta_{prq}:V_{pr}\times V_{rq}\to\partial^{prq}V_{pq}
which is equivariant with respect to the group inclusion Gp​r×Gr​q↪Gp​qG_{pr}\times G_{rq}\hookrightarrow G_{pq}. Denote the image of ϑp​r​q\vartheta_{prq} by Vp​r​qV_{prq}. The equivariance implies that one can extend ϑp​r​q\vartheta_{prq} to
ϑp​r​q∼:Gp​q×Gp​r​q(Vp​r×Vr​q)→∂p​r​qVp​q\vartheta_{prq}^{\sim}:G_{pq}\times_{G_{prq}}(V_{pr}\times V_{rq})\to\partial^{prq}V_{pq}
(5.40)
whose image is denoted (in the same pattern as B(d0,d1)∼B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}) by
Vp​r​q∼⊂∂p​r​qVp​q⊂Vp​q.V_{prq}^{\sim}\subset\partial^{prq}V_{pq}\subset V_{pq}.
Remark 5.51.
However, the restriction of the Kuranishi map Sp​q:Vp​q→Ep​qS_{pq}:V_{pq}\to E_{pq} to the stratum ∂p​r​qVp​q\partial^{prq}V_{pq} does not match with the Kuranishi map of the product of Sp​r×Sr​qS_{pr}\times S_{rq}. For example, if xp​r∈Vp​rx_{pr}\in V_{pr} and xr​q∈Vr​qx_{rq}\in V_{rq} have orthonormal framings, the corresponding framing on ϑp​r​q​(xp​r,xr​q)\vartheta_{prq}(x_{pr},x_{rq}) is not necessarily orthonormal (with respect to the L2L^{2}-pairing). Nonetheless, the Hermitian matrix part of the Kuranishi maps are given by two different kinds of normalization scheme, which can be interpolated once we have an outer-collaring (see Subsection 5.7).

Embedding obstruction bundles. We now describe how the obstruction bundles Ep​r→Vp​rE_{pr}\to V_{pr} and Er​q→Vr​qE_{rq}\to V_{rq} can be embedded into Ep​qE_{pq} restricted to Vp​r​qV_{prq}. Recall that
Ep​r=Op​r⊕Qp​r,\displaystyle\ E_{pr}=O_{pr}\oplus Q_{pr},\
Er​q=Or​q⊕Qr​q\displaystyle\ E_{rq}=O_{rq}\oplus Q_{rq}
where the OO-factors are the spaces of the sections η\eta and the QQ-factors are the trivial bundle which are use to normalize the framings. The normalization strategy is to use the L2L^{2}-metric on the domain curves and to require the framings are orthonormal.

Beyond the ingredients of showing the commutativity of (5.41), Proposition 5.26 covers the associativity of the superposition of the QQ-component of the obstruction bundle.
5.4.3. The general case
Now we describe the product construction in the general situation, i.e. with multi-layered thickenings and multiple breakings. Consider a stratum α=pr1⋯rlq∈𝔸Floerp​q\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}. We can form the product chart
Kα:=Kp​r1×⋯×Krk​qK_{\alpha}:=K_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times K_{r_{k}q}
whose domain is Vα:=Vp​r1×⋯×Vrl​qV_{\alpha}:=V_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times V_{r_{l}q} and whose obstruction bundle is
Eα:=Ep​r1⊞⋯⊞Erl​q.E_{\alpha}:=E_{pr_{1}}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus E_{r_{l}q}.
We would like to construct an equivariant embedding VαV_{\alpha} into ∂αVp​q\partial^{\alpha}V_{pq} and embed the obstruction bundle equivariantly as well.
To embed the thickened moduli space, the first step is to unify the number of layers. Recall that in the multi-layered thickening strategy, each moduli space ℳ¯r​s\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{rs} is thickened to Vr​sdr​sV_{rs}^{d_{rs}} which depends on the sequence of integers k1<⋯<kdr​sk_{1}<\cdots<k_{d_{rs}}.
Lemma 5.52.
There exists a natural Gr​sG_{rs}-equivariant inclusion
Vr​sd↪Vr​sd′​if​d<d′V_{rs}^{d}\hookrightarrow V_{rs}^{d^{\prime}}\ {\rm if}\ d<d^{\prime}
defined by
[Σ,u,F,η1,…,ηd]↦[Σ,u,F,η1,…,ηd,0,…,0⏟d′−d][\Sigma,u,F,\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d}]\mapsto[\Sigma,u,F,\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d^{\prime}-d}]
where the last d′−dd^{\prime}-d zeroes are the zero vectors of the bundles of (u,ιF)∗​Ekd+1(u,\iota_{F})^{*}E_{k_{d+1}}, …\ldots, (u,ιF)∗​Ekd′(u,\iota_{F})^{*}E_{k_{d^{\prime}}}.
Proof.
Indeed, as we use ηj\eta_{j} to perturb the equation for ηi\eta_{i} if j>ij>i, the degree dd version of (5.32) implies that the representative (Σ,u,F,η1,…,ηd,0,…,0⏟d′−d)(\Sigma,u,F,\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d^{\prime}-d}) satisfies the degree d′d^{\prime} version of (5.32). As the Gr​sG_{rs}-action changes the framing FF, such a map is indeed Gr​sG_{rs}-equivariant.
∎
Now abbreviate d=dp​qd=d_{pq}. For α=pr1⋯rlq\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q, one then obtains a GαG_{\alpha}-equivariant inclusion between the thickened moduli spaces
Vα=Vdp​r1p​r1×⋯×Vdrl​qrl​q↪Vdα:=Vdp​r1×⋯×Vdrl​q.V_{\alpha}=V^{d_{pr_{1}}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times V^{d_{r_{l}q}}_{r_{l}q}\hookrightarrow V^{d}_{\alpha}:=V^{d}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times V^{d}_{r_{l}q}.
The space VαdV^{d}_{\alpha} on the right hand side can be embedded into ∂αVp​qdp​q\partial^{\alpha}V_{pq}^{d_{pq}}. Indeed, it is straightforward to generalize the construction in the previous single-layered case with only two horizontal levels and the associativity can be verified as before.
Embedding obstruction bundles
Now for each stratum α=pr1⋯rlq∈𝔸p​qFloer\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}, define the bundles
Oα:=Op​r1→p​q⊕⋯⊕Orl​q→p​q:=Op​r1⊞⋯⊞Orl​q→Vα=Vp​r1×⋯×Vrl​qO_{\alpha}:=O_{pr_{1}\to pq}\oplus\cdots\oplus O_{r_{l}q\to pq}:=O_{pr_{1}}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus O_{r_{l}q}\to V_{\alpha}=V_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times V_{r_{l}q}
and
Qα:=Qp​r1→p​q⊕⋯⊕Qrl​q→p​q:=Qp​r1⊞⋯⊞Qrl​q→Vα=Vp​r1×⋯×Vrl​q.Q_{\alpha}:=Q_{pr_{1}\to pq}\oplus\cdots\oplus Q_{r_{l}q\to pq}:=Q_{pr_{1}}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus Q_{r_{l}q}\to V_{\alpha}=V_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times V_{r_{l}q}.
The construction of the two-factor case and the corresponding associativity induce GαG_{\alpha}-equivariant bundle embeddings
Eα:=Oα⊕Qα ϑ^α=ϑ^αO⊕ϑ^αQ Ep​q|∂αVp​q Vα ϑα ∂αVp​q .\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
